On Steenrod squares for even and odd Khovanov homology Thanks: AR was supported by NSF Grant DMS-2136090
Advika Rajapakse

Abstract
. For an arbitrary link L ⊂ S 3 L\subset S^{3} , Sarkar-Scaduto-Stoffregen construct a family 𝒳 l ​ ( L ) \mathcal{X}_{l}(L) , l ≥ 0 l\geq 0 , of spaces, giving a family of spatial refinements of even and odd Khovanov homology. We give a computation of Sq 2 \Sq^{2} on these spaces, determining the stable homotopy type of 𝒳 l ​ ( K ) \mathcal{X}_{l}(K) for all l l and all knots K K up to 11 crossings. We also prove that the Steenrod squares Sq 0 2 \Sq_{0}^{2} , Sq 1 2 \Sq_{1}^{2} defined by Schütz do arise as Steenrod squares on these spaces.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Khovanov homologies

Khovanov homology, a categorification of the Jones polynomial, gives a bigraded vector space
Kh^{i,j}(L)
for each link
L\subset S^{3}
whose graded Euler characteristic recovers the Jones polynomial
V(L)

[
Kho00
]
. Furthermore,
Kh^{i,j}(L)
is an invariant of the isotopy class of
L
. Following its discovery, we have seen several generalizations, such as tangle invariants
[
Kho02
,
BN05
]
, and perturbations
[
BN05
,
Lee05
]
. We have also seen that Khovanov homology has a functorial property where associated to a link cobordism in
\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
, there is a homomorphism of Khovanov chain complexes
[
BN05
]
. This result has found many exciting applications, such as Rasmussen’s
s
-invariant
[
Ras10
]
giving a lower bound for the slice genus of a knot and a proof
[
Pic20
]
that the Conway knot is not slice.

In
[
ORS13
]
, Ozsváth, Rasmussen, and Szabó construct a modified version of
Kh
, which we call
Kh_{o}
, that shares the same
\mathbb{Z}/2
reduction as
Kh
, but differ over
\mathbb{Q}
.
Kh_{o}
shares similar structural properties and reduced theories, and there are even chain maps associated to cobordisms
[
Put14
]
.

1.2.
Khovanov homotopy types

Lipshitz-Sarkar
[
LS14a
]
have constructed a space-level link invariant
\mathcal{X}_{Kh}(L)
that refines Khovanov homology. In particular, taking the cohomology of
\mathcal{X}_{Kh}(L)
recovers
Kh^{i,j}(L)
, and furthermore, the stable homotopy type of
\mathcal{X}_{Kh}(L)
is a link inviariant. It has been shown
[
LLS20
]
that
\mathcal{X}_{Kh}(L)
enjoys further structural properties, in particular, regarding split unions, connect sums, and mirrors. Furthermore, stable cohomology operations, like the Steenrod squares
\Sq^{n}
, on these spaces give operations
\Sq^{n}:Kh^{i,j}(L)\to Kh^{i+n,j}(L)
which are not generally trivial
[
LLS20
]
.

Sarkar-Scaduto-Stoffregen
[
SSS20
]
have since constructed modifications of this space, defining a family of spaces
\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)
,
l\geq 0
beginning with
\mathcal{X}_{0}(L)=\mathcal{X}_{Kh}(L)
and
\mathcal{X}_{1}(L)=\mathcal{X}_{o}(L)
. For
l
even,
\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)
is a refinement of
Kh
, and for
l
odd,
\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)
is a refinement of
Kh_{o}
. It has been a question whether the stable homotopy type of
\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)
only depends on
l\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2
, and whether these spectra
\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)
share structural properties analagous to the original
\mathcal{X}_{Kh}(L)
, and. We answer the former question in the negative, and some of the latter questions follow immediately from our computation of the second Steenrod square
\Sq^{2}
on these spaces
\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)
.

Theorem 1.1
.

The spectrum
\mathcal{X}_{2}(T_{3,4})
is a wedge sum of Moore spaces, so in particular,
\mathcal{X}_{e}(T_{3,4})\not\sim\mathcal{X}_{2}(T_{3,4})
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{X}_{1}(T_{3,4})
is a wedge sum of Moore spaces while
\mathcal{X}_{3}(T_{3,4})
is not, so we have
\mathcal{X}_{1}(T_{3,4})\not\sim\mathcal{X}_{3}(T_{3,4})

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\vee
denote the Spanier-Whitehead dual operation. We have
\mathcal{X}_{1}(m(T_{3,4}))\not\sim\mathcal{X}_{1}(T_{3,4})^{\vee}
,
\mathcal{X}_{3}(m(T_{3,4}))\not\sim\mathcal{X}_{3}(T_{3,4})^{\vee}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

We have
\mathcal{X}_{o}(T_{2,3}\coprod T_{2,3})\not\sim\mathcal{X}_{o}(T_{2,3})\wedge\mathcal{X}_{o}(T_{2,3})
.

Theorem 1.4
.

We have
\mathcal{X}^{-11}_{o}(T_{3,-4})\not\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}^{-12}_{o}(T_{3,-4})\vee\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}^{-10}_{o}(T_{3,-4})
.

1.3.
Steenrod squares

Lipshitz-Sarkar
[
LS14b
]
have given an explicit formula for the Steenrod square
\Sq^{2}
on the even Khovanov homotopy type
\mathcal{X}_{e}(L)
, computing the stable homotopy type of
\mathcal{X}_{e}(L)
for all prime links up to 11 crossings and giving an computable definition for
\Sq^{2}:Kh^{i,j}\to Kh^{i,j}
. Schütz
[
Sch22
]
modified Lipshitz-Sarkar’s definition of
\Sq^{2}
defining operations
\Sq_{0}^{2}:Kh_{o}^{i,j}\to Kh_{o}^{i+2,j}
,
\Sq_{1}^{2}:Kh_{o}^{i,j}\to Kh_{o}^{i+2,j}
, which are themselves link invariants, and give rise to new
s
-invariants. Schütz conjectured that the operations
\Sq_{0}^{2}
,
\Sq_{1}^{2}
, arise from the
\Sq^{2}
operations on the odd Khovanov spectra
\mathcal{X}_{2l+1}(L)
. We confirm this conjecture:

Theorem 1.5
.

When viewing the second Steenrod square
\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)}
on the space
\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)
as an operation on
Kh(L;\mathbb{F}_{2})
, we have
\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{1}(L)}=\Sq_{1}^{2}
and
\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{3}(L)}=\Sq_{0}^{2}
.

We might ask whether we can there are other Steenrod squares arising from the rest of these spaces
\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)
. The answer is that there are only four total, including the
\Sq_{0}^{2}
,
\Sq_{1}^{2}
on odd
Kh
, and the original
\Sq^{2}
on even
Kh
.

Theorem 1.6
.

The second Steenrod square
\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)}
, viewed as an operation on
Kh(L;\mathbb{F}_{2})
, only depends on
l\ \mathrm{mod}\ 4
. Furthermore,
\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{0}(L)}+\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{1}(L)}+\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{2}(L)}+\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{3}(L)}=0
.

Acknowledgements

The author would like to thank Sucharit Sarkar for many helpful conversations, and for introducing the author to this problem. The author would also like to thank Robert Lipshitz for his helpful comments.

2.
Outline of argument and review of
\Sq^{2}

Our goal is to compute the Steenrod square
\Sq^{2}:H^{*}(X)\to H^{*+2}(X)
of the odd Khovanov spectrum
X:=\mathcal{X}_{o}(L)
of a link
L
. In our construction of
X
,
X
is the formal desuspension
\Sigma^{-N}Y^{\prime}
of some CW complex
Y^{\prime}
. Our focus now turns to studying
\Sq^{2}:H^{*}(Y)\to H^{*+2}(Y)
. We simplify further by studying
\Sq^{2}:H^{m}(Y^{\prime})\to H^{m+2}(Y^{\prime})
for a simpler complex
Y^{\prime}
with only cells of dimension
m
,
(m+1)
,
(m+2)
, where
m>2
. Our strategy is now as follows.

(1)

Fix a cycle
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}

(2)

Construct an Eilenberg MacLane space
K_{m}:=K(\mathbb{Z}/2,m)
with one
m
-cell
e^{m}
, one
(m+1)
-cell
e^{m+1}
, one
(m+2)
-cell
e^{m+2}
, and higher-dimension cells (
e^{m}
shall be the fundamental class
\iota
). We only need the
(m+2)
skeleton
K_{m}^{(m+2)}
.

(3)

Construct a map
\mathfrak{c}:Y^{\prime}\to K_{m}^{(m+2)}
such that
\mathfrak{c}^{*}\iota=[\mathbf{c}^{\prime}]
.

(4)

Conclude that
\Sq^{2}([\mathbf{c}^{\prime}])=\mathfrak{c}^{*}\Sq^{2}(\iota)=\mathfrak{c}^{*}[e^{m+2}]=[\mathfrak{c}^{*}e^{m+2}]
.

The following lemmas will help us construct
K_{m}^{(m+2)}
:
For
m>2
, we construct a model of the
m^{\text{th}}
Eilenberg Maclane space
K(m,\mathbb{Z}/2)
, which we call
K_{m}
. We can choose the
m
-cell to be
e^{m}
with the entire boundary
\partial e^{m}
glued to the basepoint. To satisfy
\pi_{m}(K_{m})=\mathbb{Z}/2
, we attach a single
(m+1)
-cell by a degree
2
map
\partial e^{m+1}\to K_{k}^{(k)}=S^{m}
. The resulting
(m+1)
skeleton
K_{m}^{(m+1)}
has
\pi_{m+1}(K_{m}^{(m+1)})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
, which is a consequence of the following lemmas:

Lemma 2.1
.

\pi_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S})=\mathbb{Z}/2
, with generator represented by
\eta
, where
\eta:S^{3}\to S^{2}
is the Hopf map.

Proof.

From the fiber bundle
S^{1}\to S^{3}\xrightarrow{\eta}S^{2}
, we have that
\cong\pi_{3}(S^{2})\cong\pi_{3}(S^{3})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and from the Freudenthal suspension theorem, we see that the sequence
\pi_{2}(S^{1})\to\pi_{3}(S^{2})\to\pi_{4}(S^{3})\to\ldots
stabilizes at
\pi_{4}(S^{3})
. Furthermore, the map
\pi_{3}(S^{2})\to\pi_{4}(S^{3})
is surjective, so
\pi_{4}(S^{3})
is cyclic, generated by
\eta
. We do not give a full proof that
\pi_{1}^{s}(\mathbb{S})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
, but we only show that
0\neq[\Sigma\eta]\in\pi_{4}(S^{3})
. For if
\Sigma\eta=0
, then
\Sq^{2}
would act trivially on the (reduced) mapping cone
C(\Sigma\eta)
. But
C(\Sigma\eta)=\Sigma C(\eta)=\Sigma\mathbb{C}P^{2}
, which has nontrivial
\Sq^{2}
.
∎

Lemma 2.2
.

\pi_{2}^{s}(\mathbb{R}P^{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
, with generator represented by
S^{3}\xrightarrow{\eta}S^{2}\xhookrightarrow{\Sigma i}\Sigma\mathbb{R}P^{2}
, where
\eta
is the Hopf map and
i:S^{1}\to\mathbb{R}P^{2}
is the inclusion of the
1
-skeleton. In fact, we have the suspension sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{2}(\mathbb{R}P^{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{3}(\Sigma\mathbb{R}P^{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{4}(\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{R}P^{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\eta\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}/2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mod 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

which stabilizes by
\pi_{4}(\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{R}P^{2})
. Furthermore, the suspension map
\Sigma^{i}:\pi_{2}(\mathbb{R}P^{2})\to\pi_{2+i}(\mathbb{R}P^{2})
is nullhomotopic for
i\geq 2
.

Proof.

The first homomorphism being multiplication by
2
is explained from the fact
\Sigma q\cong 2\eta
. The third and following homomorphisms are isomorphism by the Freudenthal suspension theorem. It remains to explain the second homomorphism is
\mathrm{mod}\ 2
. We use the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\eta\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\Sigma\eta\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{3}(S^{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{4}(S^{3})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{5}(S^{4})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{3}(\Sigma\mathbb{R}P^{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{4}(\Sigma^{2}\mathbb{R}P^{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{5}(\Sigma^{3}\mathbb{R}P^{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mod 2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the first horizontal arrows are surjections and the remaining horizontal arrows are isomorphisms by the Freudenthal suspension theorem. We must explain why the first vertical arrow is a surjection and the following vertical arrows are isomorphisms. We use the cofiber long exact sequence

\pi_{m+2}(\Sigma^{m-1}\mathbb{R}P^{2},S^{m})\to\pi_{m+1}(S^{m})\to\pi_{m+1}(\Sigma^{m-1}\mathbb{R}P^{2})\to\pi_{m+1}(\Sigma^{m-1}\mathbb{R}P^{2},S^{m})\to\pi_{m}(S^{m}),

and note that for
m=2
, the sequence is

\pi_{4}(\Sigma\mathbb{R}P^{2},S^{2})\to\mathbb{Z}/2\to\pi_{3}(\Sigma\mathbb{R}P^{2})\to\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z},

and for
m>2
, the sequence is

\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z}/2\to\pi_{m+1}(\Sigma^{m-1}\mathbb{R}P^{2})\to\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z}.\qed

To zero out the
\pi_{m+1}(K_{m}^{(m+1)})
, we attach a
(m+2)
-cell
e^{m+2}
, with the attaching map
\partial e^{m+2}\cong S^{m+1}\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{m-2}\eta}S^{m}\cong K_{m}^{(m)}
being a
(m-2)
-fold suspension of the Hopf map
\eta
.

3.
Permutohedra and twists

3.1.
Permutohedra

We first give our definition of permutohedra:

Definition 3.1
.

In
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
v_{\sigma}=(\sigma^{-1}(1),\ldots,\sigma^{-1}(n))\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the
\sigma
permutation of the tuple
(1,\ldots,n)
. The
(n-1)
-dimensional permutohedron
\Pi_{n-1}
is the convex hull in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of the
n!
points
v_{\sigma}
.

Note that
\Pi_{n-1}
is a polytope in the affine subspace
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}:=\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}|\Sigma_{i}x_{i}=n(n-1)/2\}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
\Pi^{n-1}
is indeed
(n-1)
-dimensional, so it is codimension
0
in
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
.

Notation 3.2
.

We let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
be the canonical ordered basis of unit vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
e_{i}-e_{j}
are tangent vectors in
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
for
1\leq i,j\leq n
, and since
\Pi^{n-1}
is codimension
0
, these are tangent vectors in
\Pi^{n-1}
.

We can also define
\Pi^{n-1}
as an intersection of half-spaces
H_{S}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
, which we shall define:

Definition 3.3
.

Let
S\in\underline{2}^{\{0,\ldots,n-1\}}\backslash\{\overline{1},\overline{0}\}
, that is
S
is a non-empty proper subset of
\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
. Let
|S|=k
. Define
H_{S}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
to be the half-space
\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\}\in\mathbb{A}^{n-1}\mid\Sigma_{i\in S}x_{i}\geq k(k-1)/2\}
. Define the
facet

F_{S}
of
\Pi^{n-1}
to be
\Pi^{n-1}\cap\partial H_{S}
.

There are
2^{n}-2
of these half-spaces
H_{S}
, and indeed
\bigcap_{S}H_{S}=\Pi^{n-1}
. Furthermore, the union of the facets
F_{S}
form the boundary
\partial\Pi^{n-1}
. These facets can also be identified with lower-dimensional permutohedra:

Lemma 3.4

(
[
LLS20
]
)
.

Let
a_{1}<a_{2}<\dots<a_{k}
be the elements of
S
and let
b_{1}<b_{2}<\dots b_{n-k}
be the elements of
\{1,2,\ldots n\}
. The map

f_{S}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=((x_{a_{1}}\ldots x_{a_{k}}),(x_{b_{1}}-k\ldots x_{b_{n-k}}-k))

identifies the facet
F_{S}
with
\Pi^{k-1}\times\Pi^{n-k-1}
.

Proof (
[
LLS20
]
).

It suffices to prove that
f_{S}
takes the vertices of
F_{S}
to the vertices of
\Pi^{k}\times\Pi^{n-k}
. But the vertices of
F_{S}
are the points
(x_{1},\ldots x_{n})
so that
\{x_{a_{1}}\ldots x_{a_{k}}\}=\{1,\ldots k\}
and
\{x_{a_{1}}\ldots x_{a_{k}}\}=\{k+1,\ldots n\}
.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
S,T
be two non-empty, proper subsets of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and
F_{S},F_{T}
their associated facets of
\Pi^{n-1}
.
F_{S}\cap F_{T}
is nonempty if and only if
S\subseteq T
or
T\subseteq S
.

Proof.

Suppose that
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in F_{S}\cap F_{T}
. Then

(1)

\sum_{i\in S}x_{i}+\sum_{i\in T}x_{i}=(0+\ldots+|S|)+(0+\ldots+|T|).

But since
F_{S}\cap F_{T}\subset H_{S\cup T}\cap H_{S\cap T}
,

(2)

\sum_{i\in S}x_{i}+\sum_{i\in T}x_{i}=\sum_{i\in S\cap T}x_{i}+\sum_{i\in S\cup T}x_{i}\geq(1+\ldots+|S\cap T|)+(1+\ldots+|S\cup T|).

and both hold only if either
S\subseteq T
or
T\subseteq S
.
∎

An intersection of facets
F_{S_{1}}\cap\ldots\cap F_{S_{m}}
can produce higher codimension boundary components of
\Pi^{n-1}
. Note that from Lemma
3.5
that the intersection only if the sets
S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}
can be ordered by inclusion.

Notation 3.6
.

Given the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we denote
\overline{0}=\emptyset
,
\overline{1}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Definition 3.7
.

Given a chain
c=\{\overline{1}=S_{m}>S_{m-1}>\ldots>S_{1}>S_{0}=\overline{0}\}
in
2^{\{1,\ldots,n\}}
, define the
face

F_{c}
of
\Pi^{n-1}
as the intersection of facets
\bigcap_{1\leq i\leq m-1}F_{S_{i}}
.

F_{c}=\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}):\sum_{i\in S_{k+1}\backslash S_{k}}x_{i}=(|S_{k}|+1)+(|S_{k}|+2)+\ldots+|S_{k+1}|\text{ for all }0\leq k\leq m-1\},

implying that
F_{c}
is an
(n-m)
-dimensional face.

Lemma 3.8
.

Given a chain
c=\{\overline{1}=S_{m}>S_{m-1}>\ldots>S_{1}>S_{0}=\overline{0}\}
in
2^{\{1,\ldots,n\}}
, the map

f_{c}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=(\mathbf{t}_{S_{1},S_{0}},\ldots,\mathbf{t}_{S_{m},S_{m-1}})

where if
S,T\in 2^{\{1,\ldots,n\}}
,
S>T
, then if
a_{1}<\ldots<a_{|S|-|T|}
are the elements of
S\backslash T
, then

\mathbf{t}_{S,T}=(x_{a_{1}}-|T|,\ldots x_{a_{|u|-|v|}}-|T|)

identifies the face
F_{c}
of
\Pi^{n-1}
with
\Pi^{|S_{1}|-|S_{0}|-1}\times\ldots\times\Pi^{|S_{m}|-|S_{m-1}|-1}
.

Certain facets of
\Pi^{n-1}
will be particularly useful to us:

Definition 3.9
.

Fix the permutohedron
\Pi^{n-1}
and let
0\leq i\leq n-1
be an integer. We define
G_{i}\subset\Pi^{n-1}
to be the facet
F_{\{1,\ldots,\widehat{i+1},\ldots,n\}}
. Equivalently,
G_{i}
is the subset of
\Pi^{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
where the
(i+1)^{\text{th}}
coordinate is
n
. In general, for
0\leq a_{1}<\ldots<a_{k}\leq n-1
, we define
G_{\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\}}:=F_{\{0,\ldots,n-1\}\backslash\{a_{1}+1,\ldots,a_{k}+1\}}
. Our upshifting of indices is done only to be compatibile with the notation in signed flow categories.

•

Note that the facets
G_{i}
can be identified with
\Pi^{n-2}
through the map

F_{i}\xrightarrow{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{S}\\
\sim\end{subarray}}\Pi^{n-2}\times\Pi^{0}\cong\Pi^{n-2},

where
S=\{1,\ldots,\widehat{i+1},\ldots,n\}
. We call this map
f_{i}
.

•

For shorthand, we write
\{1,\ldots,n\}\backslash S
as
S^{c}
. So for example, the above facet is
F_{i}=F_{\{i+1\}^{c}}
.

Remark 3.9.1
.

Note that the affine
(n-1)
-space
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
inherits a smooth structure and Riemannian metric from the space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that it lies in. Therefore,
\Pi^{n-1}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
inherits a well-defined tangent space
T(\Pi^{n-1})
and Riemannian metric from
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}
.

Remark 3.9.2
.

On any abstract polytope
P
of dimension
d
, there exists a polytope
t_{I}P
,
I=\{0,1,\ldots,d-1\}
called the
omnitruncation
of
P
. See (
[
Cox73
, Chapter 8]
, or
[
Mat15
, Chapter 3, Section 6]
for the defintion of omnitruncation). The omnitruncation
t_{I}P
has facets corresponding to the faces of
P
, and a vertex for each flag
P
. We outline one way to view
t_{I}P
: Chop off each vertex of
P
with a hyperplane normal to the vector pointing from the centroid of
P
to the vertex. These hyperplanes should just barely intersect
P
so that they do not intersect with one another
inside of

P
. We then chop off the original edges of
P
with similar hyperplanes that are normal to the vectors from the centroid to the midpoint. If
P
is higher than
3
-diensional, we continue this process of truncating higher and higher dimensional facets. An equivalent definition is that
t_{I}P
to be the dual of the barycentric subdivision
\mathcal{B}
of
P
(note that the vertices of
B
correspond to the faces of
P
and the facets of
B
corresponds to the flags of
P
).

Observe that the
\Pi^{n-1}
is the omnitruncation of the standard topological
(n-1)
-simplex
\Delta^{n-1}
with vertices
0<1<\ldots<n-1
. In this regard, the facets
G_{i}
correspond to the vertices
0,1,\ldots,n-1\in\Delta^{n-1}
,the facets
G_{\{i,j\}}
correspond to the edges of
\Delta^{n-1}
and the faces
G_{\{i,j,k\}}
correspond to
2
-faces. The
2
-skeleton of
\Delta^{m-1}
is simply connected for
n\geq 3
, so any loop
\mathcal{K}
in
\Pi^{n-1}
passing through just the facets of type
G_{i}
,
G_{\{i,j\}}
should be nullhomotopic. We will use this idea in Section
10
, where we manipulate circular tubes lying inside polytopes.

Figure 1.
Left, the solid tetrahedron
\Delta^{n-1}

(n=4)
. Right: The omnitruncation of
\Delta^{n-1}
, which is the
(n-1)
-dimensional permutohedron
\Pi^{n-1}
.

3.2.
Twists

Notation 3.10
.

Define
J:=[-1,1]\subset\mathbb{R}
. Let
e_{J}
be the unit vector in this
\mathbb{R}
direction.

Definition 3.11
.

Consider a connected topological group
G
, where we imagine
G
to be a group of rotations. We define a
twist
in
G
to be a continuous path
\gamma:[0,1]\to G
such that
\gamma(0)=\text{\text{Id}}
and
\gamma(t)
is constant for
t
in a neighborhood of
\{0,1\}
. Composition of twists is performed through the diamond operation
\diamond
. If
\phi,\psi:[0,1]\to G
are two twists, we define
\phi\diamond\psi:[0,1]\to G
to be the twist that is
\psi(2t)
on
[0,1/2]
and
\phi(2t-1)\circ\psi(1)
on
[1/2,1]
.

We denote the nontwist
c_{\Id}
by the constant map
[0,1]\to G
,
t\mapsto\Id
.
c_{\Id}
acts as the identity (up to homotopy) under the diamond composition.

Composition of twists is not associative, but it is associative up to homotopy. In fact, we observe the following property:

Lemma 3.12
.

If
g
and
f
are twists, then the twist
t\mapsto g(t)f(t)
is homotopic to
g\diamond f
relative the endpoints
0,1\in[0,1]
.

Example 3.12.1
.

Consider the product
J\times\Pi^{k}
and the center point
P=(1/2,p)
, where
p
is the center of
\Pi^{k}
. We can imagine the group of rotations of
J\times\Pi^{k}
to be given by
SO(T_{P}(J\times\Pi^{k}))
. Therefore, a twist of
J\times\Pi^{k}
is given by a path
[0,1]\to SO(T_{P}(J\times\Pi^{k}))
starting at
Id
.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
*
denote concatenation of paths. That is, if
f,g:[0,1]\to G
are paths with
f(1)=g(0)
, define
f*g:[0,1]\to G
to be the path that is
f(2t)
on the subinterval
[0,1/2]
and
g(2t-1)
on the subinterval
[1/2,1]
.

Proof.

At time
s
, define the homotopy
h_{s}:=g(st)f(t)*f(s+(1-s)t)f(1)
.
h_{0}=g\diamond f
and
h_{1}=c_{g(1)f(1)}*g(t)f(t)
, which is homotopic to
g(t)f(t)
. See Figure
2
.
∎

Lemma 3.14
.

Given twists
f:[0,1]\to G
and
g:[0,1]\to G
,
g(0)=f(0)=\text{Id}
,

g(1)f(t)g(1)^{-1}\cong g(t)f(t)g(t)^{-1}\cong g(t)\diamond f(t)\diamond g(t)^{-1},

where the homotopies are relative the endpoints
\{0,1\}\subset[0,1]
.

Proof.

The first homotopy is
(\left.g(\cdot)\right|_{[s,1]})f(\cdot)(\left.g(\cdot)^{-1}\right|_{[s,1]})
(see Figure
2
for a picture). To understand the second homotopy, use Lemma
3.12
to calculate

f(t)\diamond g(t)\diamond f(t)^{-1}\cong f(t)\diamond(g(t)f(t)^{-1})\cong f(t)g(t)f(t)^{-1}.\qed

Figure 2.
Left: The homotopy in Lemma
3.12
. Right: The homotopy in Lemma
3.14

Definition 3.15
.

Let
g
and
f
be twists
[0,1]\to G
with the same endpoint
f(1)=g(1)\in G
. The concatenation
g*\overline{f}=\overline{f}\diamond g
is a loop in
G
starting and ending at
Id
.
g*\overline{f}
represents an element in
\pi_{1}(G)
, which we call
g-f
.

3.3.
Twisting manifolds with corners

The twists we focus on in this paper are twists of the form
\varphi:[0,1]\to SO(T_{P}W)
, where
W
is a manifold with corners equipped with a Riemannian metric, and
P\in\Int W
(we think of
P
as a “midpoint” of
W
).

Notation 3.16
.

Let
P\in W
, and
c_{\Id}:[0,1]\to SO(T_{P}W)
be the nontwist. We often abuse notation and write
c_{\Id}=W
.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
W
,
Z
be manifolds with corners, with
P\in W
,
P^{\prime}\in Z
. If
f:[0,1]\to SO(T_{P}W)
,
g:[0,1]\to SO(T_{P^{\prime}}Z)
are twists, we identify
SO(T_{(P,P^{\prime})}(W\times Z))\cong T_{P}W\times T_{P^{\prime}}Z
and define the twist
f\times g:[0,1]\to SO(T_{(P^{\prime},P)}(W\times Z))
to be
f
in the
T_{P}W
component and
g
in the
T_{P^{\prime}}Z
component. So in particular,
f\times Z
is
f
in the
T_{P}W
component and the nontwist in the
T_{P^{\prime}}Z
component.

We call an element
g\in SO(T_{P}W)
a
symmetry
if
L
is induced by an isometry
W\to W
(which is therefore a diffeomorphism).

We call
f

gluable
if

•

f(1):T_{P}W\to T_{P}W
is a symmetry.

•

f
is constant near the endpoints
0,1
.

Note that
f\times g
is gluable if
f
and
g
are gluable.
See Figure
3
for an illustration of a gluable and non-gluable twist.

\to

,

\to

Figure 3.
Let
W
denote the
5
-pointed star. Left: An example of a symmetry of
T_{P}W
. We can view the transformation as a
72^{\circ}
counterclockwise rotation of
T_{P}W
. Right: An example of a non-symmetry of
T_{P}W
. We can view the transformation is a
108^{\circ}
counterclockwise rotation of
T_{P}W
. The dotted overlay shows how the non-symmetry would look if extended to the entire
W
. The transformation on the left can be extended, however. Therefore, a
72^{\circ}
counterclockwise twist is gluable, but not a
108^{\circ}
counterclockwise twist.

Remark 3.17.1
.

Observe that gluable twists are closed under the
\diamond
composition.

Now let
P=(0,P^{\prime})
be the midpoint of
X=J\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}
. Since there is a natural Riemannian metric on both
J
and
\Pi^{\kappa-1}
(see Remark
3.9.1
), we put the product metric on
T_{P}X
. We define special types of twists
\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}:[0,1]\to SO(T_{P}X)
which we will use frequently in this paper.

Definition 3.18
.

For
1\leq i,j\leq\kappa-2
,
i\neq j
, we define the twist
\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}
as the following: take the vector
\mathbf{a}=(0,e_{i}-e_{j}),\partial_{J}=(e_{J},0)\in T_{0}J\times T_{P^{\prime}}\Pi^{\kappa-1}\cong T_{P}X
.
Now identify
T_{P}X\cong\langle\mathbf{a},\partial_{J}\rangle^{\perp}\times\langle\mathbf{a},\partial_{J}\rangle
and define
\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}:[0,1]\to SO(T_{P}X)
to be the twist
\langle\mathbf{a},\partial_{J}\rangle^{\perp}\times\varphi
, where
\varphi
turns the oriented basis
(\mathbf{a},\partial_{J})

180^{\circ}
clockwise (see Figure
4
).

\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}

Figure 4.
An illustration of how the twist looks in the plane
\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{\partial_{J}}\rangle

(see Figure
5
for an illustration). Now the twist
\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}
is gluable. Indeed,
\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}(1)
is the isometry
X\to X
that maps
(\mathbf{v},t)
to
(\mathbf{v}_{i\rightleftarrows j},-t)
, where
\mathbf{v}_{i\rightleftarrows j}
is
\mathbf{v}
with the
i^{\text{th}}
and
j^{\text{th}}
coordinates swapped. Observe that
\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}(1)
swaps
J\times F_{\{i\}}
with
J\times F_{\{j\}}
,
J\times F_{\{i,k\}}
with
J\times F_{\{j,k\}}
, and so on.

Notation 3.19
.

We write
\varphi_{i}:=\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows i+1}
, which lightens up the notation in our paper.

Figure 5.
Let
X=J\times\Pi^{2}
. Left: The gluable twist
\varphi_{1}
in
T_{P}X
. Right: The gluable twist
\varphi_{2}
in
T_{P}X
.

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
X=J\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}
, and let
1\leq i,j,k\leq\kappa-1
. We have the identities

\displaystyle\varphi_{j\rightleftarrows k}\diamond\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows k}^{-1}\diamond\varphi_{j\rightleftarrows k}=\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}\diamond\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows k}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows k}\diamond\varphi_{j\rightleftarrows k}^{-1}=\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}^{-1}\diamond\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows k}.

In particular, we observe
\varphi_{i+1}\diamond\varphi_{i}=\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows i+2}^{-1}\diamond\varphi_{i+1}=\varphi_{i}\diamond\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows i+2}^{-1}
.

Proof.

We only prove the first equality; the other equality follows similarly. The first equality follows from the identity

\displaystyle\varphi_{j\rightleftarrows k}\diamond\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}\diamond\varphi_{j\rightleftarrows k}^{-1}

\displaystyle\cong\varphi_{j\rightleftarrows k}(1)\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}(t)\varphi_{j\rightleftarrows k}^{-1}(1)

\displaystyle=(P_{(jk)}\tau_{J})\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}(t)(P_{(jk)}\tau_{J})^{-1}

\displaystyle=P_{(jk)}\left(\tau_{J}\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}(t)\tau_{J}^{-1}\right)0.1cm_{(jk)}^{-1}

\displaystyle=P_{(jk)}\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows j}(t)^{-1}P_{(jk)}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{i\rightleftarrows k}(t)^{-1}.\qed

Definition 3.21
.

Suppose
W
is a manifold with corners equipped with a Riemannian metric, and that
\dim W=m\geq 4
. If
P\in\Int W
, then
\pi_{1}(SO(T_{P}W))\cong\pi_{1}(SO(m))\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
. We call a “full twist” to be any twist
g:[0,1]\to SO(T_{P}W)
such that
g(1)=\Id
and
g
defines the generator
1\in\pi_{1}(SO(T_{P}W))
.

Remark 3.21.1
.

Let
W,P
be as Definition
3.21
. Note that all full twists are homotopic relative the endpoints
0,1
, and that a
\diamond
composition of an even number of full twists is nullhomotopic. Note that by Lemma
3.14
, full twists in
SO(T_{P}W)
must commute (under the
\diamond
operation) with any twist
f
up to homotopy. Indeed, if
g
is such a full twist, then
g\diamond f\diamond g^{-1}\cong g(1)fg(1)^{-1}=f
.

Definition 3.22
.

Let
W,P
be as Definition
3.21
, and suppose
g
is a full twist. We denote the homotopy class of
g
informally as
[1]
, and furthermore a composition
g_{1}\diamond\ldots g_{l}
of full twists as
[l]
. We denote
[f\diamond g_{1}\diamond\ldots g_{l}]\cong[f]+[l]
. The “
+
” symbol is natural for us, since by Remark
3.21.1
, full twists commute under
\diamond
composition.

Now let
\varphi
,
\phi
be twists
[0,1]\to SO(T_{P}W)
with the same endpoint
\varphi(1)=\phi(1)
.
\varphi-\phi
defines an element
\omega\in\pi_{1}(SO(T_{P}W))\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
(see Definition
3.15
). We write
\varphi-\phi=(\omega)
. Observe the identities
\varphi-\phi=\phi-\varphi
and
(\varphi-\phi)+(\phi-\psi)=\varphi-\psi
if
\varphi(1)=\phi(1)=\psi(1)
. In view of Remark
3.21.1
, we conclude that
\varphi=\phi+(\omega)
.

3.4.
Adding twists to tubes

Let us consider the case where we have an embedding
\Theta:X\times[0,1]\to Y
, where
X=J\times\Pi^{n-1}
and
Y
is a manifold with corners. We call
\Theta
, a
tube
, with ends
X\times\{0\}
,
X\times\{1\}
. We define what it means to “add a twist” to
\Theta
.

Definition 3.23
.

Suppose we are given a gluable twist
\varphi:[0,1]\to SO(T_{P}W)
. If
W
is convex, each point
P^{\prime}\in W
is written as
P+v
for a unique
v\in T_{P}W
. Given a twist
\varphi:[0,1]\to SO(T_{P}W)
, we can construct an embedding
\Omega:[0,1]\times P\to[0,1]\times P
as the following.
\Omega(P+v,t)=(P+\lambda(t)\varphi_{t}(v),t)
, where
\lambda:[0,1]\to(0,1]
is a continuous function satisfying:

•

\lambda(t)
is the constant
1
for
t
near the endpoints
0,1
.

•

\lambda
decays in the middle quickly enough so that
P+\lambda(t)\varphi(W)
stays inside
W
.

To “add” the twist
\varphi
to a tube
\Theta:W\times[0,1]\to Y
, simply precompose
\Theta
with
\Omega
(see Figure
6
). We call the resulting tube
\Theta\diamond\varphi
.

The twist
\Theta\diamond\varphi
depends on our choices for
\lambda
, but it is straightforward to check that it is well-defined up to homotopy (relative the endpoints
0,1\in[0,1]
).

Lemma 3.24
.

Let
\Theta:X\times[0,1]\to Y
be a tube, and let
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}:[0,1]\to SO(T_{P}X)
be twists.
(\Theta\diamond\varphi)\diamond\varphi^{\prime}\cong\Theta\diamond\varphi\diamond\varphi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

From Definition
3.23
, the tube
(\Theta\diamond\varphi)\diamond\varphi^{\prime}
is
(\Theta\circ\Omega)\circ\Omega^{\prime}
, where
\Omega(P+v,t)=\left(P+\lambda(t)\varphi_{t}\cdot v,t\right)
and
\Omega^{\prime}(P+v,t)=\left(P+\lambda^{\prime}(t)\varphi^{\prime}_{t}\cdot v,t\right)
.

\displaystyle\left((\Theta\circ\Omega)\circ\Omega^{\prime}\right)(P+v,t)=\Omega\left(P+\lambda^{\prime}(t)\varphi^{\prime}(t)\cdot v,t\right)

\displaystyle=\left(P+\lambda(t)\varphi(t)\cdot(\lambda^{\prime}(t)\varphi^{\prime}(t)\cdot v),t\right)

\displaystyle=\left(P+\lambda(t)\lambda^{\prime}(t)(\varphi(t)\varphi^{\prime}(t))\cdot v,t\right).

Therefore,
(\Theta\diamond\Omega)\diamond\Omega^{\prime}\cong\Theta\diamond(t\mapsto\varphi(t)\varphi^{\prime}(t))\cong\Theta\diamond\varphi\diamond\varphi^{\prime}
, with the last equivalence being from Lemma
3.12
.
∎

\Omega

Figure 6.
Precomposing with the above map
\Omega
defines the twisted tube
\Theta\circ\Omega
.

3.5.
Doubly specified tubes

So far, we have focused on tubes
\Theta:X\times[0,1]\to Y
, where note that
\Theta
has a canonical starting face
X\times\{0\}
, and ending face
X\times\{1\}
, and (gluable) twists are added twisting from the starting face in the direction of the ending face. We next define a type of tube
T
in this paper that does not have canonical starting and ending face, where we can add twists that travel in both directions. We give a name to each side of
T
, and while there is no restriction to the names, we often make the arbitrary choice to name one side
\alpha
and the other side
\beta
.

Definition 3.25
.

Let
T=\{(\Theta,\alpha),(\Lambda,\beta)\}
be an unordered pair of tubes
W\times[0,1]\to Y
such that
\Theta(x,t)=\Lambda(D(x),1-t)
for some isometry
D:W\to W
fixing
P\in W
. We call
T
, a
doubly specified tube
(abbreviated
d.s. tube
). We denote the face
\Theta(X\times\{0\})
as the
\alpha
-end
, and the face
\Lambda(X\times\{0\})
as the
\beta
-end
.

If
G=\Id
, then
\Lambda
is just the reversed parametrization of
\Theta
. In this case, we say that
T
is
boundary-coherent
.

A family
\{T_{s}\}_{0\leq s\leq 1}
,
T_{s}=\{(\Theta_{s},\alpha_{s}),(\Lambda_{s},\beta_{s})\}
of d.s. tubes is called a
homotopy
if
\Theta_{s}
,
\Lambda_{s}
themselves are homotopies relative
t=0,1
, and
\Theta_{s}(x,t)=\Lambda_{s}(D(x),1-t)
for all
s
. (Note that
D
is fixed.) The tubes
T_{0}
,
T_{1}
are said to be
homotopic
.

Example 3.25.1
.

Consider the manifold with corners
Y:=J\times\Pi^{\kappa}
living inside
J\times\mathbb{A}^{\kappa}
, and define
X:=J\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}
We define a d.s. tube
T=\{(V_{i},\alpha),(V^{\prime}_{i},\beta)\}
with both ends on the boundary
\partial Y
(see Figure
7
). We first define
V_{i}:X\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{A}^{\kappa}
as follows:

Figure 7.
An illustration of the doubly specified tube
T_{i}=\{(V_{i},\alpha),(V^{\prime}_{i},\beta)\}
.
V_{i}
starts in the bottom (orange) portion and goes to the top (blue) portion.

•

We define the starting position
(t=0)
of
V_{i}
by
V_{i}(s,a,0)=(s,f_{i}(a))
, where, recall,
f_{i}
is the embedding from Definition
3.9
.

•

The vectors
dV_{i}(\partial_{t})
,
dV_{i}(\partial_{J})
should start
(t=0)
pointing in the
-\partial_{\mathbf{n}}
,
\partial_{J}
directions respectively, where
\mathbf{n}
is the inward unit normal of
X
.

•

As
t
increases from
0
to
1
, these vectors
dV_{i}(\partial_{t})
,
dV_{i}(\partial_{J})
should rotate
180^{\circ}
in the plane
\langle\mathbf{n},\partial_{J}\rangle
so that at time
t=\frac{1}{2}
, they point in the directions
\partial_{J}
,
\mathbf{n}
respectively.

•

We define the ending position
(t=1)
of
V_{i}
by
V_{i}(s,a,0)=(-s,f_{i}(a))
.

We now define
V_{i}:X\times[0,1]\to J\times\mathbb{A}^{\kappa}
by
V^{\prime}_{i}(s,a,t)=V_{i}(-s,a,1-t)
. In effect,
V_{i}
turns “up and around” back into
Y
, where
V^{\prime}_{i}
turns “down and around.”

Example 3.25.2
.

Let
X=J\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}
be, and let
Y
be as in the previous example. Consider the faces
J\times F_{i}
,
J\times F_{j}\subset Y
,
0\leq i<j\leq\kappa
. Let
E:=\conv((J\times F_{i})\cup(J\times F_{j}))
be the convex hull of the two faces (see Figure
8
for an illustration).

Figure 8.
The convex tubes
X\times[0,1]\to\Pi^{2}
, where
X=J\times\Pi^{1}
. Note that both the left and the middle tubes are boundary-coherent, while the right tube is boundary-incoherent.

E
is the image of a d.s. tube
T_{\{i,j\}}=\{(V_{ij},\alpha),(V_{ji},\beta)\}
containing parametrizations
X\times[0,1]\to Y
. Our definition of
T_{\{i,j\}}
is as follows:

\displaystyle V_{ij}(s,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\kappa}),t)

\displaystyle:=(s,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i},\kappa+1,a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{\kappa}))+t(\kappa+1-a_{j})(e_{j+1}-e_{i+1})

\displaystyle V_{ji}(s,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\kappa}),t)

\displaystyle:=(s,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{j},\kappa+1,a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{\kappa}))+t(\kappa+1-a_{i+1})(e_{i+1}-e_{j+1}).

We have intentionally defined
V_{ij},V_{ji}
such that
V_{ij}(\cdot,\cdot,0)
(resp.
V_{ji}(\cdot,\cdot,0)
) is the inclusion
J\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}\xhookrightarrow{\Id\times f_{i}}J\times\Pi^{\kappa}
(resp. the inclusion
J\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}\xhookrightarrow{\Id\times f_{j}}J\times\Pi^{\kappa}
) (again, see Figure
8
). To check the behavior when
t=1
, we compute

(3)

\displaystyle V_{ij}(s,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\kappa}),1)

\displaystyle=(s,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i},a_{j},a_{i+1},\ldots a_{j-1},\kappa+1,a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{\kappa})),

\displaystyle V_{ji}(s,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\kappa}),1)

\displaystyle=(s,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i},\kappa+1,a_{i+2},\ldots,a_{j},a_{i+1},a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{\kappa})),

so it is indeed the case that
V_{ij}(J\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}\times\{t\})
goes from
J\times F_{i}
to
J\times F_{j}
and
V_{ji}(J\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}\times\{t\})
goes from
J\times F_{j}
to
J\times F_{i}
. However, note from (
3
) that
T_{\{i,j\}}
is only boundary-coherent when
j=i+1
. Indeed, we have the relation
V_{i,j}(s,x,1)=V_{j,i}(s,P_{(i+1\ \ldots\ j)}(x),0)
, where
P_{(i+1\ \ldots\ j)}
permutes the coordinates of
\Pi^{\kappa-1}
as

P_{(i+1\ \ldots\ j)}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\kappa})=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i},a_{j},a_{i+1},\ldots,\widehat{a_{j}},\ldots,a_{\kappa}).

Definition 3.26

(Adding twists)
.

Suppose
T=\{(\Theta,\alpha),(\Lambda,\beta)\}
is a d.s. tube with parametrizations
\Theta,\Lambda:W\times[0,1]\to Y
, and the relation
\Theta(x,t)=\Lambda(Dx,1-t)
. Let
\varphi
be a gluable twist in
T_{P}W
, with
\varphi_{1}:T_{P}W\to T_{P}W
induced by an isometry
\Phi:W\to W
. We define
T\diamond(\varphi,\alpha)
to be the d.s. tube
T^{\prime}=\{(\Theta^{\prime},\alpha),(\Lambda^{\prime},\beta)\}
, where

\Theta^{\prime}=\Theta\diamond\varphi,\qquad\Lambda^{\prime}(x,t)=\Theta^{\prime}(\Phi^{-1}D^{-1}(x),1-t)

It is immediate from the property that
\Theta^{\prime}(x,t)=\Lambda^{\prime}(D\circ\Phi(x),1-t)
, so we indeed have that
T^{\prime}
is a d.s. tube (since
D\circ\Phi
is an isometry). Furthermore, we have
\Lambda^{\prime}(x,0)=\Lambda(x,0)
. We call our construction of
T^{\prime}

adding
\varphi
to
T
along the
\Theta
direction
.

Note how the names of the tube ends stay the same after adding twists. This makes it easier to continually add twists without having to keep track of the names of the ends.

Notation 3.27

(Adding full twists)
.

In view of Remark
3.21.1
, if
\dim W\geq 4
, we can add a full twist
f
along
T
in either direction and we should get the same result (up to homotopy). So similar to Notation
3.22
, we denote
T
plus a
\diamond
compostion of
k
full twists (in either direction) as
T+k
.

Example 3.27.1
.

Let
T_{\{i,j\}}=\{(V_{ij},i),(V_{ji},j)\}
be as in Example
3.25.2
, and consider the tube
T:=T_{\{i,j\}}\diamond(\varphi_{j-1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\varphi_{i+1},i)
(see Figures
9
,
10
for examples).

Figure 9.
We illustrate
T^{\prime}:=T_{\{0,2\}}\diamond(\varphi_{1},0)
, where
T=\{(V_{0,2},0),(V_{2,0},2)\}
. Here, we imagine
\kappa=2
, and we only draw the
J\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(v,\overline{0})
factor.
T^{\prime}
is not boundary-coherent, since the two sides differ by a flip in the
J
-factor

Figure 10.
An example of the tube
T\diamond(\varphi_{2},0)\diamond(\varphi_{1},0)
, where
T=\{(V_{0},\alpha),(V_{3},\beta)\}
Here, we imagine
\kappa=3
, and we only draw the
\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(v,\overline{0})
factor (so not the
J
factor). This tube
T
happens to be boundary-coherent.

Observe

(4)

\displaystyle(V_{ij}\diamond\varphi_{(j-1)}\diamond\ldots\diamond\varphi_{(i+1)})(s,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\kappa}),1)

\displaystyle=V_{ij}(\varphi_{(j-1)}(1)\ldots\varphi_{(i+1)}(1)(s,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\kappa}),1)

\displaystyle=V_{ij}\left((-1)^{j-i-1}s,P_{(j\ \ldots\ i+1)}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\kappa}),1\right)

\displaystyle=\left((-1)^{j-i-1}s,(a_{1},\ldots a_{j},\kappa+1,a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{\kappa})\right)

\displaystyle=V_{ji}((-1)^{j-i-1}s,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\kappa}),0),

implying that
T\diamond(\varphi_{(j-1)}\diamond\ldots\diamond\varphi_{(i+1)},i)
is boundary-coherent if and only if
j-i\equiv 1\mod 2
.

Now suppose we want to add a twist
\varphi
to
T
in the
\alpha
-direction and a twist
\varphi^{\prime}
in the
\beta
-direction. The following proposition shows that the order does not matter.

Proposition 3.28
.

Let
T=\{(\Theta,\alpha),(\Lambda,\beta)\}
be a d.s. tube as in Definition
3.25
, and let
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}
be twists.

T\diamond(\varphi,\alpha)\diamond(\varphi^{\prime},\beta)\cong T\diamond(\varphi^{\prime},\beta)\diamond(\varphi,\alpha).

Proof.

Follows from a direct computation.
∎

We now consider “sliding” twists from one end of
T
to another, whether it be from the
\alpha
-end to the
\beta
-end, or the other way (see Figure
11
for an illustration).

Figure 11.
An illustration of how the same tube
T^{\prime}
can be constructed by adding a twist to
T
in the
\alpha
-direction vs. adding a twist in the
\beta
-direction.

Proposition 3.29
.

Let
T=\{(\Theta,\alpha),(\Lambda,\beta)\}
, with relation
\Theta(x,t)=\Lambda(Dx,1-t)
. We have

T\diamond(\varphi\diamond\varphi^{\prime},\alpha)\diamond(\psi^{\prime},\beta)\cong T\diamond(\varphi^{\prime},\alpha)\diamond(\psi\diamond\psi^{\prime},\beta),

where
\psi
is the twist
\psi(t)=(dD)\varphi(1-t)\varphi(1)^{-1}(dD)^{-1}
. (Here,
dD
is the tangent map
dD:T_{P}W\to T_{P}W
.)

Proof.

Follows from another direct computation.
∎

Example 3.29.1
.

Let
T_{\{i,j\}}=\{(V_{ij},i),(V_{ji},j)\}
be as in Example
3.25.2
, with relation
V_{ij}(x,t)=V_{ji}(Dx,1-t)
, and consider again
T:=T_{\{i,j\}}\diamond(\varphi_{(j-1)}\diamond\ldots\diamond\varphi_{(i+1)},i)
. After a repeated application of Proposition
3.29
, we have the identity

(5)

T=T_{\{i,j\}}\diamond(\varphi_{(j-1)}\diamond\ldots\diamond\varphi_{(i+1)},i)=T_{\{i,j\}}\diamond(\psi_{(i+1)}\diamond\ldots\diamond\psi_{(j-1)},j),

where

\displaystyle\psi_{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle=(dD)\varphi_{(n)}(1-t)\varphi_{(n)}(1)^{-1}(dD)^{-1}

\displaystyle=P_{(i+1\ldots j)}\left(\varphi_{(n)}(t)^{-1}\right)\left(P_{(i+1\ldots j)}\right)^{-1}=\begin{cases}\varphi_{j\rightleftarrows i+1}^{-1}(t)&if $n=j-1$\\
\varphi_{(n+1)}^{-1}(t)&otherwise.\end{cases}

By a repeated application of Lemma
3.20
, the right hand side of (
5
) simplifies as

T\diamond(\varphi_{(i+2)}^{-1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\varphi_{(j-1)}^{-1}\diamond\varphi_{j\rightleftarrows i+1},j)=\ldots=T\diamond(\varphi_{(i+1)}\diamond\ldots\diamond\varphi_{(j-1)},j)

by “sliding” the
\varphi_{j\rightleftarrows i+1}
to the other end of the composition.

We are allowed to compose twists with d.s. tubes, with Lemma
3.24
showing that this composition rule is associative up to homotopy. Therefore, we can imagine that tubes are analagous with points in affine space and twists are analagous with vectors. With this analagy in mind, we define the difference
T-T^{\prime}
of tubes in terms of twists.

Definition 3.30
.

Let
T=\{(\Theta,\alpha),(\Lambda,\beta)\}
,
T^{\prime}=\{(\Theta^{\prime},\alpha),(\Lambda^{\prime},\beta)\}
both be doubly specified tubes, with

(6)

\Theta(x,0)=\Theta^{\prime}(x,0),\qquad\Lambda(x,0)=\Lambda^{\prime}(x,0).

We define the set
T-T^{\prime}:=\{\{(f,\alpha),(g,\beta)\}:T^{\prime}\cong T\diamond(f,\alpha)\diamond(g,\beta)\}
. If
T^{\prime}\cong T\diamond(f,\alpha)\diamond(g,\beta)
, then we write
T^{\prime}-T\cong(f,\alpha)\diamond(g,\beta)
. Furthermore, we can compose sets of the form
T^{\prime}-T^{\prime\prime}
,
T-T^{\prime}
as long as the end labels for
T
,
T^{\prime}
,
T^{\prime\prime}
all match up.

Note how the criterion (
6
) that the ends of
T
,
T^{\prime}
match up is necessary, but not sufficient for
T-T^{\prime}
to be nonempty. For example, the cores of
T,T^{\prime}
may not even be homotopic, in which case there is no way to express
T^{\prime}
as a twisting of
T
. Now if
T,T^{\prime}\subset Y
are smooth d.s. tubes, where
Y
is a smooth manifold, and the cores
T,T^{\prime}
are homotopic, the notation
T-T^{\prime}
does make sense.

Lemma 3.31
.

Whe have the relation
T-T^{\prime\prime}=(T^{\prime}-T^{\prime\prime})\diamond(T-T^{\prime})
In particular, if
T^{\prime}-T^{\prime\prime}\cong(f^{\prime},\alpha)\diamond(g^{\prime},\beta)
and
T-T^{\prime}\cong(f,\alpha)\diamond(g,\beta)
, we have
T-T^{\prime\prime}\cong(f^{\prime}\diamond f,\alpha)\diamond(g^{\prime}\diamond g,\beta)
.

Proof.

Follows from a direct computation using Definition
3.30
.
∎

3.6.
Composing doubly specified tubes

Consider two doubly specified tubes
T,T^{\prime}\subset Y
, and imagine that in our setup, the
\Lambda
-end of
T
is identified with the
\Omega^{\prime}
-end of
T^{\prime}
. (See Figure
12
for an illustration.)

Figure 12.
The tube
T\cup T^{\prime}
, where
T=\{(\Theta,\alpha),(\Lambda,\beta)\}
,
T^{\prime}=\{(\Theta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}),(\Lambda^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\}
. Here,
T\cup T^{\prime}
joins the
\beta
-end of
T
with the
\alpha^{\prime}
end of
T^{\prime}
.

We wish to doubly parametrize
T\cup T^{\prime}
, where the
T
-portion (resp.
T^{\prime}
-portion) is still parametrized using the “free”
\Theta
-end (resp.
\Lambda^{\prime}
-end).

Definition 3.32
.

Let
T,T^{\prime}
be doubly specified tubes, with
T=\{(\Theta,\alpha),(\Lambda,\beta)\}
,
T^{\prime}=\{(\Theta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}),(\Lambda^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\}
, and end relations
\Theta(x,t)=\Lambda(D(x),1-t)
and
\Theta^{\prime}(x,t)=\Lambda^{\prime}(D^{\prime}(x),1-t)
. Suppose additionally that
\Lambda(x,0)=\Theta^{\prime}(x,0)
. We define
T\cup T^{\prime}=T\cup_{\beta,\alpha^{\prime}}T^{\prime}:=\{(\widehat{\Theta},\alpha),(\widehat{\Lambda},\beta^{\prime})\}
, with the parametrizations
\widehat{\Theta},\widehat{\Lambda}
defined by

\displaystyle\widehat{\Theta}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\Theta(x,2t)\\[3.0pt]
\Theta^{\prime}(D(x),2t-1)\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip-50.00008pt\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}t\in[0,1/2]\\[3.0pt]
t\in[1/2,1],\end{array}

\displaystyle\widehat{\Lambda}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\Lambda^{\prime}(x,2t)\\[3.0pt]
\Lambda(D^{\prime}(x),2t-1)\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip-50.00008pt\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}t\in[0,1/2]\\[3.0pt]
t\in[1/2,1].\end{array}

Furthermore, if we are given a sequence of d.s. tubes
T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}
, with
T_{i}=\{(\Theta_{i},\alpha_{i}),(\Lambda_{i},\beta_{i})\}
, and
\Lambda_{i}(x,0)=\Theta_{i+1}(x,0)
, we can iterate the “
\cup
” operation to obtain a d.s.
T_{1}\cup\ldots\cup T_{n}
. The order of composition is invariant up to homotopy.

We intentionally defined
\widehat{\Theta}
and
\widehat{\Lambda}
in order to satisfy the identities
\widehat{\Theta}(x,t)=\Theta(x,2t)
,
\widehat{\Lambda}(x,t)=\Lambda(x,2t)
for
0\leq t\leq 1/2
. In other words,
\widehat{\Theta}|_{W\times[0,1/2]}
parametrizes
T
just like
\Theta
(and
\widehat{\Lambda}|_{W\times[0,1/2]}
parametrizes
T^{\prime}
just like
\Lambda^{\prime}
).

Consider a concatenation
(T\diamond(\varphi,\alpha))\cup T^{\prime}
. We describe how to “push”
\varphi
into
T^{\prime}
(see Figure
13
).

Figure 13.
Sliding the twist
\varphi
from
T
to
T^{\prime}
.

.

Let
X=J\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}
,
Y=J\times\Pi^{\kappa}
. Let
0\leq a,b,c\leq\kappa
be distinct integers.
T_{\{a,b\}}\cup_{b,\alpha}T_{b}\cup_{\beta,b}T_{\{b,c\}}

T_{\{a,c\}}\diamond(\rho,a)

. (see Figure

).

.

\rho
must be, consult Table
1
.

such that

Proposition 3.33
.

Suppose
T
,
T^{\prime}
are doubly specified tubes, with
T=\{(\Theta,\alpha),(\Lambda,\beta)\}
,
T^{\prime}=\{(\Theta^{\prime},\alpha),(\Lambda^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\}
, with the relation

. Suppose

. Then

.
