by

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{i}

\displaystyle:=[-R,R]^{A}\times[-R,R]^{B}\times J\times\overbrace{[0,1]\times F_{i}}^{\subset\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(u,\overline{0})}\subset\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(w,\overline{0}),\quad 0\leq i\leq\kappa+1.

Figure 17.
What we refer to as faces
\mathbf{G}_{1}
,
\mathbf{G}_{2}
,
\mathbf{G}_{3}
in the case
\mathcal{C}(y)
is a thickened
2
-dimensional permutohedron (
|y|=|v|=3
). We do not draw the extra
[0,1]
-factor.

Remark 4.31.1
.

Given a cell
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y)
of
Y^{\prime}
, there are embedded subsets
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{x}(y)\cong\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)\times\mathcal{M}(y,x)
of
\partial\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y)
for each
x
, where each component
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)\times\{p\}
of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{x}(y)
gets identified with
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)
and everything outside of
\bigcup_{x}C_{x}(y)
gets identified to the basepoint.

We make an analagous observation for cells
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z)
of
Y
. There are embedded subsets
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{y}(z)\cong\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y)\times\mathcal{M}(z,y)
, wehere each component
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y)\times\{q\}
of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{y}(z)
gets identified with
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y)
.

It is important to note that our indexing of facets is compatible with our cubical index assignment
S_{\mathbb{Z}}
in the following sense: if
p\in\mathcal{M}(y,x)
, then
S_{\mathbb{Z}}(p)
equals the index
i
of the facet
\mathbf{G}_{i}\subset\partial\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y)
that contains
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)\times\{p\}
. Similarly,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y)\times\{q\}\subset\mathbf{G}_{j}
for
q\in\mathcal{M}(z,y)
,
j=S_{\mathbb{Z}}(q)
.

5.
Constructing map to the Eilenberg-MacLane space and boundary matching

5.1.
Defining the truncated Eilenberg-MacLane space
K_{m}^{(m+2)}

We are going to more explicitly define
K_{m}^{(m+2)}
. We start with a single
0
-cell. Then we define the
m
-cell of
K_{m}^{(m+2)}
as

(16)

e^{m}=[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A}\times[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{B}\times J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(\kappa)}(\overline{1},\overline{0}),

with the entire boundary attached to the basepoint. Define the
(m+1)
-cell as
e^{m+1}=e^{m}\times[0,1]
. The map
e^{m+1}\to K_{m}^{(m+1)}
maps
e^{m}\times\{0\}
by Id to
e^{m}
and maps
e^{m}\times\{1\}
to
e^{m}
by the following map:

(17)

e^{m}\times\{1\}\cong e^{m}\xrightarrow{\tau}e^{m},\qquad\tau(a,b,t,p)=(a,b,-t,p),

which simply flips the
J
factor. The rest of the boundary of
e^{m+1}
gets mapped to the basepoint.
Also,
K_{m}^{(m-1)}
has the characterization

(18)

K_{m}^{(m+1)}={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left([-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A}\times[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{B}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(\kappa)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})\right)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\partial$}\right.}\wedge{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left(\frac{J\times[0,1]}{((t,0)\sim(-t,1)}\right)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\partial$}\right.},

which is mainly different from the construction using (
16
) in the sense that the
J
factor is moved to the right.

Now we move to the
(m+2)
-cells. We define
e^{m+2}=D^{m+2}
and define the attaching map
\partial e^{m+2}\to K_{m}^{(m+1)}
as a suspension of the Hopf map
\eta
.

\partial e^{m+1}\cong S^{m+1}\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{m-2}\eta}S^{m}\cong e^{m}/\partial e^{m}\subset K_{m}^{(m+1)}.

Let
\mathbf{c}\in C_{\cell}^{m}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})
be an
m
-dimensional cocycle denoted by

\mathbf{c}=\sum_{x}\mu_{x}\cdot\mathcal{C}(x),

and let

\mu=\sum_{x}\mu_{x}\cdot x

be the corresponding cocycle in
C_{\mathcal{M}}^{l}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})\cong C_{\cell}^{m}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})
. The
\mu_{x}
-terms are the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-coefficients. We say
x

appears in

\mu
if
\mu_{x}=1
.
We would like to construct a map
\mathfrak{c}:Y\to K_{m}^{(m+2)}
that pulls
\iota
back to
\mathbf{c}
(that is,
\mathbf{c}=\mathfrak{c}^{*}\iota
, where
\iota\in C_{\cell}^{m}(K_{m}^{(m+2)};\mathbb{F}_{2})
is the fundamental class
e^{m}
). Let the
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
be the generators of
C^{m}(Y)
appears in
\mu
(having coefficients
1
, not
0
). On the
m
-skeleton of
Y
, we ask
\mathfrak{c}
to map the cells
\mathcal{C}(x)
as follows:

•

\mathfrak{c}
maps
\mathcal{C}(x)=[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{d_{\kappa}}\times[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{d_{\kappa+1}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(u,\overline{0})
by identity to
e^{m}
if
x
appears in
\mu
. (
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(u,\overline{0})
, by definition, is equal to
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(\kappa)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})
.)

•

\mathfrak{c}
maps
\mathcal{C}(x)
to the basepoint if
\mu_{x}=0
.

Any
\mathfrak{c}
satisfying the above condition will pull
\iota
back to
\mathbf{c}
by
\mathfrak{c}^{*}
. Now we must specify how
\mathfrak{c}
maps the
(m+1)
-skeleton of
Y
, that is, specify how it maps cells of type
\mathcal{C}(y)
.

Notation 5.1
.

For a generator
y\in\Ob(\mathscr{C})
, define

\mathcal{M}(y,\mu):=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\text{ appears}\\
\text{in }\mu\end{subarray}}\mathcal{M}(y,x).

Note that since
\mu
is a cocycle,
\#(\mathcal{M}(y,\mu))=0\mod 2
.

Because
\mu
is a cocycle,
\mathfrak{c}
can only map an even number of these components homeomorphically onto
K_{m}^{(m)}\cong S^{m}
; everything outside of these components maps to the basepoint. We choose to group this set of these “boundary components” into pairs in a process called “boundary matching:” each component of some
C_{x}(y)
for
x
appears in
\mu
is “matched” with some other arbitrarily chosen component of another
C_{x^{\prime}}(y)
(see Figure
18
for an illustration).

Figure 18.
An illustration of a (facewise) boundary matching
\mathfrak{b}_{y}
of
\mathcal{M}(y,\mu)
in terms of embeddings of cells
\mathcal{C}(x)
into
\mathcal{C}(y)
. There are an even number of embedded cells of the form
\mathcal{C}(x)
where
x\in\mu
, so we pair them up and connect them through tubes
\eta\subset\mathcal{C}(y)
. (The faces
\mathbf{G}_{i}
are thickenings of
F_{i}\subset\Pi^{(\kappa+1)-1}
, defined in Definition
4.31
.) Note that the edge from
\mathbf{G}_{0}
to
\mathbf{G}_{2}
(green) and
\mathbf{G}_{1}
to
\mathbf{G}_{2}
(light blue) must be oriented from the lower index face to the higher index face. But the edge from
\mathbf{G}_{0}
to itself could have been oriented either way.

We use this boundary matching (matching an embedded
\mathcal{C}(x)\times\{p\}
with an embedded
\mathcal{C}(x^{\prime})\times\{p^{\prime}\}
) to extend
\mathfrak{c}
to the cell
\mathcal{C}(y)
. Our process is as follows:

(B-1)

The restriction
\mathfrak{c}|_{\partial\mathcal{C}(y)}
to the boundary
\partial\mathcal{C}(y)
is predetermined by
\partial\mathcal{C}(y)\xrightarrow{\text{attaching}}Y^{(m)}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{c}|_{Y^{(m)}}}K_{m}^{(m)}
. In particular, if
x
is represented in
\mu
,
\mathcal{C}_{x}(y)\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(y)
is mapped by
\mathcal{C}_{x}(y)\cong\mathcal{C}(x)\times\mathcal{M}(y,x)\to\mathcal{C}(x)\cong e^{m}\to K_{m}^{(m)}
.

(B-2)

Construct d.s. tubes
\eta:=\{(\Theta,0),(\Lambda,1)\}:e^{m}\times[0,1]\xhookrightarrow{}\mathcal{C}(y)
, where
\Theta
identifies the endpoints
e^{m}\times\{i\}
(
i=0,1
) with the boundary components
\mathcal{C}(x)\times\{p\}
,
\mathcal{C}(x^{\prime})\times\{p^{\prime}\}
in
\mathcal{C}_{x}(y)
,
\mathcal{C}_{x^{\prime}}(y)
. (This identification may not be canonical.)

(B-3)

Have
\mathfrak{c}
map everywhere in
\mathcal{C}(y)
outside of the tubes
\eta
to the basepoint.

(B-4)

Continuously extend
\mathfrak{c}
to
\eta
for each
\eta
. That is, define an extension
\mathfrak{c}|_{\eta}:\eta\to K_{m}^{(m+1)}
that agrees with
\mathfrak{c}|_{\partial\mathcal{C}(y)}
on the boundary components
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}
,
\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\}
.

5.2.
Constructing the boundary matching tubes
\eta

Here, we outline (
B-2
), which first involves examining a pair of matched boundary components. Suppose a component
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)\times\{p\}\subset\mathcal{C}(y)
is matched with another component
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\times\{p^{\prime}\}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y)
. We first construct an “unsigned” version
\eta_{\emptyset}:=\{(\Theta_{\emptyset},0),(\Lambda_{\emptyset},1)\}
of our eventual tube
\eta
.

Case 0: The matched boundary components are on the same face
\mathbf{G}_{i}\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(y)
.

Let us first focus on
\Theta_{\emptyset}
. Among the two boundary components
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)\times\{p\}\subset\mathcal{C}(y)
,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\times\{p^{\prime}\}\subset\mathcal{C}(y)
we arbitrarily choose a starting
(t=0)
component of
\Theta_{\emptyset}
, and an ending
(t=1)
component. This choice is made arbitrarily, and after renaming
x,x^{\prime},p,p^{\prime}
, we intend that
\Theta_{\emptyset}
goes from
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}
to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}
as
t
goes from
0
to
1
.

The tube
\Theta_{\emptyset}:e^{m}\times[0,1]\xhookrightarrow{}\mathcal{C}(y)
is characterized by its projection to the
\mathcal{C}(y)
factors
[-R,R]^{A}
,
[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{B}
, and
J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(v,\overline{0})
. In other words,
\Theta_{\emptyset}
is determined by its projections:

(1)

\pi_{\epsilon}\circ\Theta_{\emptyset}:e^{m}\times[0,1]\to[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{B}

(2)

\pi_{R}\circ\Theta_{\emptyset}:e^{m}\times[0,1]\to[-R,R]^{A}

(3)

\pi_{\mathcal{M}}\circ\Theta_{\emptyset}:e^{m}\times[0,1]\to J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(v,\overline{0})
.

Defining
\Theta_{\epsilon}:=\pi_{\epsilon}\circ\Theta_{\emptyset}
:

For
a\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A}
,
b\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{B}
,
s\in J
,
x\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(u,\overline{0})
, and
t\in[0,1]
, we define
\Theta_{\epsilon}(a,b,s,x,t)=b
.

Defining
\Theta_{R}:=\pi_{R}\circ\Theta_{\emptyset}
:

See Figure
19
. Let
\gamma(t):[0,1]\to[-R,R]^{A}
be a smooth embedded path from
a_{p_{0}}
to
a_{p_{1}}
(the points
a_{p_{0}},a_{p_{1}}
are defined in (
11
)). We define
\Theta_{R}(t,a,b,x)=\gamma(t)+a
.

Figure 19.
An illustration showing how the embedding
\Theta
behaves when projected to the
[-R,R]^{A}
-component of
\mathcal{C}(y)
.

Defining
\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}:=\pi_{\mathcal{M}}\circ\Theta_{\emptyset}
:

Our construction of
\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}
depends on a delicate treatment of the cases
u=\overline{0}
and
u\neq\overline{0}
, since the definition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(u,\overline{0})
is exceptional in the case
u=\overline{0}
(see Definition
4.22
). Now suppose we match two components
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}\subset\mathbf{G}_{i}
,
\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\}\subset\mathbf{G}_{j}
.

See Figure
20
for an illustration. We choose a tube
\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}
where as
t
moves from
0
to
1
, the slices
e^{m}\times\{t\}
move straight into
\Int(J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(v,\overline{0}))
, but then pull up and around back into the face
J\times G_{i}
, but with the
J
-direction flipped (see Figure
20
). This should look a lot like an “inward” version of our construction in Example
3.25.1
. Let us give a precise construction. Denote values in
e^{m}\times[0,1]
by
(a,b,s,p,t)
, where when
\kappa>0

a\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A}
,
b\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{B}
,
s\in J
,
p=(r,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\kappa}))\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(u,\overline{0})
, and
t\in[0,1]
. In the exceptional case
\kappa=0
, we denote
p=0
.

•

We define the starting position (
t=0
) of
\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}
by

(19)

\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(a,b,s,p,0)=\begin{cases}(s,r,(a_{1}\ldots,a_{i-1},\kappa+1,a_{i},\ldots,a_{\kappa}))\subset\mathbf{G}_{i}&in the case $\kappa>0$\\
(s,0,(1))\in\mathbf{G}_{1}&in the case $\kappa=0$.\end{cases}

•

The vectors
d\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(\partial_{t})
,
d\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(\partial_{J})
should start (
t=0
) pointing in the
\partial_{\mathbf{n}}
,
\partial_{J}
directions respectively, where
\mathbf{n}
is the inward unit normal.

•

As
t
increases from
0
to
1
, these vectors
d\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(\partial_{t})
,
d\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(\partial_{J})
should rotate
180^{\circ}
in the plane
\langle\mathbf{n},\partial_{J}\rangle
so that at time
t=\frac{1}{2}
, they point in the directions of
\partial_{J},-\mathbf{n}
respectively.

•

At the end (
t=1
),
d\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(\partial_{t})
,
d\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(\partial_{J})
should be pointing in the
-\partial_{\mathbf{n}}
,
-\partial_{J}
directions respectively.

•

We define the ending position of
\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}
by

(20)

\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}(a,b,s,p,1)=\begin{cases}(-s,r,(a_{1}\ldots,a_{i-1},\kappa+1,a_{i},\ldots,a_{\kappa}))\subset\mathbf{G}_{i}&in the case $\kappa>0$\\
(-s,0,(1))\in\mathbf{G}_{1}&in the case $\kappa=0$.\end{cases}

Figure 20.
An illustration of
\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}
for when
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)\times\{p\}
,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)\times\{p\}
are in the same face
\mathbf{G}_{i}

We now define
\Lambda_{\emptyset}(x,t)=\Theta_{\emptyset}(\tau(x),1-t)
. Essentially,
\Lambda_{\emptyset}
moves in reverse, starting at
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\}\subset\mathcal{C}(y)
, and ending at
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}\subset\mathcal{C}(y)
. We observe that
\Lambda_{\emptyset}
turns “down” and around back into
\mathbf{G}_{i}
, instead of “up” and around.

Case 1: The matched boundary components are on faces
\mathbf{G}_{i},\mathbf{G}_{j}\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(y)
,
i<j
.

\partial\mathcal{C}(y)
contains distinct faces
\mathbf{G}_{i},\mathbf{G}_{j}
. This assumption can only hold when
\kappa>0
, which is an important distinction, since we can say
J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(v,\overline{0})=J\times[0,1]\times\Pi^{\kappa}
, where
|v|=\kappa+1\geq 2

We first construct a “convex” version
\eta^{\conv}:=\{(\Theta^{\conv},0),(\Lambda^{\conv},1)\}
of the d.s. tube
\eta_{\emptyset}
, and we do so by first specifying
\Theta^{\conv}
.

Unlike in Case 0, where we could arbitrarily choose the starting component, there is a designated starting component for
\Theta^{\conv}
: the component in the lower index face. So after a relabeling of
x,x^{\prime},p,p^{\prime}
, we have two boundary components
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}
,
\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\}
, where importantly,
S_{\mathbb{Z}}(p_{0})=i<S_{\mathbb{Z}}(p_{1})=j
.

\Theta^{\conv}
is similarly determined by its projections:

(1)

\Theta_{\epsilon}^{\conv}:e^{m}\times[0,1]\to[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{B}

(2)

\Theta_{R}^{\conv}:e^{m}\times[0,1]\to[-R,R]^{A}

(3)

\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}^{\conv}:e^{m}\times[0,1]\to J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(v,\overline{0})
.

The projections
\Theta_{\epsilon}^{\conv}
,
\Theta_{R}^{\conv}
are defined exactly the same as in Case 0, but
(3)
is different.
Consider the doubly-parametrized tube
\{V_{ij},V_{ji}\}
from Example
3.25.2
(see Figure
21
), with

\displaystyle V_{ij}:\left(J\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}\right)\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow J\times\Pi^{\kappa}

\displaystyle(s,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\kappa}),t)\mapsto(s,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},\kappa+1,

\displaystyle a_{i},a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{\kappa}))+t(\kappa+1-a_{i})(e_{i+1}-e_{i})).

We define the tube
\Theta^{\conv}_{\mathcal{M}}
by

(21)

\displaystyle\Theta^{\conv}_{\mathcal{M}}:e^{m}\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\pi\times\text{Id}}\left(J\times[0,1]\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}\right)\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{(s,r,p,t)\mapsto(s,p,t,r)}J\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}\times[0,1]\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{V_{i,j}\times\text{Id}_{[0,1]}}J\times\Pi^{\kappa}\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\xhookrightarrow{(s,p,r)\mapsto(s,r,p)}J\times[0,1]\times\Pi^{\kappa},

where
\pi:e^{m}\to J\times[0,1]\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}
is the projection onto the factor
J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(\kappa)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})
. Now to define
\Lambda^{\conv}
, we define
\Lambda_{\epsilon}^{\conv}(x,t)=\Theta_{\epsilon}^{\conv}(x,1-t)
,
\Lambda_{R}^{\conv}(x,t)=\Theta_{R}^{\conv}(x,1-t)
, and we define
\Lambda^{\conv}
as the composition in (
21
), but with
V_{ij}
replaced by
V_{ji}
.

Figure 21.
Both pictures: examples of
\Theta^{\conv}_{\mathcal{M}}
for
|v|=\kappa+1=3

Case 1a:
j=i+1
:

In this case, simply define
\eta_{\emptyset}:=\eta^{\conv}
.

Case 1b:
j>i+1
:

In this case, we need to add some twists to
\eta^{\conv}
in the
J\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}
-plane (see Figures
9
,
10
).
Let
P\in e^{m}
be the midpoint and consider the twists in

T_{P}e^{m}\cong T_{P_{1}}\left([-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A+B}\times[0,1]\right)\times T_{P_{2}}\left(J\times\Pi^{\kappa-1}\right)

that are constant in the first tangent plane, but
\varphi_{r}
in the second tangent plane. We call these twists
\bm{\varphi}_{r}
. Using this notation, we define
\eta_{\emptyset}:=\eta^{\conv}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},0)
.

We are finally set up to define our boundary matching tube. The last step is incorporate the signs
\sigma(p_{0})
,
\sigma(p_{1})
:

Definition 5.2
.

Define the doubly specified
boundary matching
tube
\eta=\{\Theta,\Lambda\}
by

(22)

\Theta(x,t)=\Theta_{\emptyset}(\tau^{\sigma(p_{0})}(x),t),\quad\Lambda(x,t)=\Lambda_{\emptyset}(\tau^{\sigma(p_{1})}(x),t),

We constructed
\eta
intentionally so it has the property that the restrictions
\Theta|_{e^{m}\times\{0\}}

\Lambda|_{e^{m}\times\{0\}}
are the inclusions
e^{m}\cong C(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}(y)
,
e^{m}\cong C(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}(y)
. Note that in the unsigned flow category setting,
\eta=\eta_{\emptyset}
.

5.3.
Extending
\mathfrak{c}
to the tubes
\eta
.

Here, we outline Step (
B-4
) using our tubes
\eta
constructed in the previous section, thus finishing our extension of
\mathfrak{c}
to
\mathcal{C}(y)
. Observe that
\Theta^{-1}|_{\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}}:\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}\to e^{m}\times\{0\}
agrees with
\mathfrak{c}|_{\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}}:\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}\to e^{m}
. It might seem reasonable to extend
\mathfrak{c}
to the slices
\Theta(e^{m}\times\{t\})
similarly, but recall from equations (
20
), (
4
) that if the faces
\mathbf{G}_{i}
,
\mathbf{G}_{k}
are even index apart,
(\Theta_{e^{m}\times\{1\}})^{-1}
differs from
\mathfrak{c}|_{\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\}}
by a flip in the
J
factor. In these cases,
\mathfrak{c}
must also map to the
(m+1)
-cell of
K_{m}^{(m+1)}
.

Remark 5.2.1
.

The boundary matching tube
\eta
is boundary-coherent if and only if the “flipping” quantity
\omega=j-i+1+\sigma(p_{0})+\sigma(p_{1})=0\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

Definition 5.3
.

Suppose
\eta
is a boundary matching tube with ends
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}\subset\mathbf{G}_{i}
,
\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\}\subset\mathbf{G}_{j}
.

(T-1)

If
\eta
is boundary-coherent (see Remark
5.2.1
), we define

\mathfrak{c}|_{\eta}:\eta\xrightarrow{\Theta_{\sigma}^{-1}}e^{m}\times[0,1]\to e^{m}\hookrightarrow K_{m}^{(m)}.

We call
\eta
(also
\Theta
) a
boundary-coherent
tube in this case.

(T-2)

If
\eta
is boundary-incoherent, we define

\mathfrak{c}|_{\eta}:\eta\xrightarrow{\Theta_{\sigma}^{-1}}e^{m}\times[0,1]\cong e^{m+1}\to K_{m}^{(m+1)}.

Whether
\eta
is boundary-coherent or boundary-incoherent, our definition of
\mathfrak{c}|_{\eta}
agrees with the prescribed
\mathfrak{c}|_{\mathcal{C}(y)}
from (
B-1
) on the intersection
\eta\cap\partial\mathcal{C}(y)=(\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\})\cup(\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\})
, which confirms our extension to
\eta
is well-defined.

Remark 5.3.1
.

The terms “boundary-coherent” and “boundary-incoherent” tubes
\eta
should allude to the boundary-coherent and boundary-incoherent
arcs

\eta
from
[
LS14b
]
. The two concepts are linked; whereas the identifications
\mathcal{C}(x_{i})\times\{p_{i}\}\xrightarrow{\Theta^{-1}}e^{m}\times\{i\}
,
i=0,1
agree with
\mathfrak{c}
if
\eta
is a boundary-coherent
tube
, the framing of a boundary-incoherent
arc
in
[
LS14b
]
agrees with the framing of its endpoints. Similarly,
\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\}\xrightarrow{\Theta^{-1}}e^{m}\times\{1\}
disagrees with
\mathfrak{c}
by a flip if
\eta
is a boundary-incoherent tube, mirroring how the framing of a boundary-incoherent arc disagrees with the framing of one of its endpoints by a “flip.”

Let us examine the choices we could have made when following Steps (
B-1
)-(
B-4
). Steps (
B-1
), (
B-3
), and (
B-4
), were entirely predetermined, but we could have made a lot of choices in Step (
B-2
), affecting our extension of
\mathfrak{c}
to cells
\partial\mathcal{C}(z)
. In particular, we made pairs of boundary components
\{\mathcal{C}(x)\times\{p\},\mathcal{C}(x^{\prime})\times\{p^{\prime}\}\}
, and furthermore designated a starting
(t=0)
component and ending
(t=1)
component for each pair. This designation is predetermined if the components lie in different index faces
\mathbf{G}_{i},\mathbf{G}_{j}
of
\partial\mathcal{C}(y)
, but the designation is arbitrary if
\mathcal{C}(x)\times\{p\},\mathcal{C}(x^{\prime})\times\{p^{\prime}\}
lie in the same face
\mathbf{G}_{i}
. We encode these choices in what we call a
facewise boundary matching
.

Definition 5.4
.

Given a cycle
\mu\in C_{\mathcal{M}}^{l}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})
, we define a
facewise boundary-matching

\mathfrak{m}=(\mathfrak{b}_{y},\mathfrak{s}_{y})
, where:

•

\mathfrak{b}_{y}
is a fixed point free involution of
\mathcal{M}(y,\mu)
. We can also think of
\mathfrak{b}_{y}
as a partition of
\mathcal{M}(y,\mu)
into disjoint pairs
\{p,\mathfrak{b}_{y}(p)\}
.

•

\mathfrak{s}_{y}
is an ordering for each pair
\{p,p^{\prime}\}\in\mathfrak{b}_{y}
. We require that if
S_{\mathbb{Z}}(p)<S_{\mathbb{Z}}(p^{\prime})
, then
\{p,p^{\prime}\}
is ordered as
(p,p^{\prime})
. In other words, if
p
and
p^{\prime}
are in moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(y,x)
and
\mathcal{M}(y,x^{\prime})
respectively, and
\mathcal{C}(x)\times\{p\}
and
\mathcal{C}(x^{\prime})\times\{p^{\prime}\}
embed in faces
\mathbf{G}_{i},\mathbf{G}_{j}\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(y)
,
i<j
, then
\mathfrak{s}_{y}
orders the edge
\{p,p^{\prime}\}
as
(p,p^{\prime})
.

Again, the process of facewise boundary matching is illustrated in Figure
18
.

Note that if
S_{\mathbb{Z}}(p)=S_{\mathbb{Z}}(p^{\prime})
, in a matched pair
\{p,p\}
, then
\mathfrak{s}_{y}
can order
\{p,p^{\prime}\}
as
(p,p^{\prime})
or
(p^{\prime},p)
. This completely arbitrary choice encodes the choice we made in the beginning of Section
5.2
to designate a “
t=0
” component
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}
and a “
t=1
” component
\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\}
.

6.
Cycles and their homotopy classes

We have defined
\mathfrak{c}
on the
(m+1)
-skeleton of
Y
, and pur next goal is to extend
\mathfrak{c}
to the
(m+2)
-skeleton, which we can do one cell at a time. Following this idea, we fix an
(m+2)
-cell
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z)
of
Y^{\prime}
. “Most” of the boundary
\partial\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z)
must map to the basepoint under
\mathfrak{c}
; to explain what we mean, there are inclusions of type

(E-1)

\jmath^{\prime}_{z,x}:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}(z,x)\hookrightarrow\partial\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z)
(see Section
4.10
, Equation (
15
)),

(E-2)

\jmath^{\prime}_{y,x}:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y)\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}(z,y)\hookrightarrow\partial\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z)
(see Section
4.10
, Equation (
14
)),

and
\mathfrak{c}
must map any point outside of these images to the basepoint. Embeddings of type-(
E-1
) look like a collection of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)
-tubes and embeddings of type-(
E-2
) look like a collection of cells
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y)
(see Figure
16
for a visualization).

In fact, “most” of a type-(
E-2
) image must map to the basepoint. Indeed, if we consider an embedded
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y)\times\{q\}\subset\partial\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z)
, any point outside of the embedded boundary matching tubes
\eta\times\{q\}
gets mapped to the basepoint as well.

We have narrowed our attention to two types of “tubes,” that is, tubes of the form (
E-1
), and tubes of the form
\eta\times\{q\}
. We make these notions precise in the following definitions:

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)
be an
m
-cell of
Y^{\prime}
. If we consider a component
I\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}(z,x)
, we can define a tube

\Theta_{\sigma}:e^{m}\times[0,1]\xhookrightarrow{\Id\times\theta}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)\times I\xhookrightarrow{\jmath^{\prime}_{I}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z),

where
\theta:[0,1]\to I
is an arbitrary parametrization of
I
. By parametrizing
I
from the other direction, we obtain another tube
\Lambda_{\sigma}(x,t)=\Theta_{\sigma}(x,1-t)
, giving us a coherent d.s.
\zeta=\{\Theta_{\sigma},\Lambda_{\sigma}\}
. We call
\zeta
a
Pontrjagin-Thom tube
.

Observe that the restrictions
\Theta_{\sigma}|_{e^{m}\times\{0\}}
,
\Theta_{\sigma}|_{e^{m}\times\{1\}}
are precisely the inclusions

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)\times\{p_{0}\}\times\{q_{0}\}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z),\qquad\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)\times\{p_{1}\}\times\{q_{1}\}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z)

for
\{p_{i}\}\times\{q_{i}\}=\theta(i)
,
(i=0,1)
, keeping in mind that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)=e^{m}
.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
q\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}(z,y)
, and let
\eta=\{\Theta_{\sigma},\Lambda_{\sigma}\}
be a boundary matching tube in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y)
. We “embed”
\eta
in
\partial\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z)
by postcomposing
\Theta_{\sigma},\Lambda_{\sigma}
with the inclusion
\jmath^{\prime}_{q}:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y)\times\{q\}\hookrightarrow\partial\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z)
. Namely, define
\widetilde{\Theta}_{\sigma}:=\jmath^{\prime}_{q}\circ\Theta_{\sigma}
,
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\sigma}:=\jmath^{\prime}_{q}\circ\Lambda_{\sigma}
, giving us
\tilde{\eta}=\{\widetilde{\Theta}_{\sigma},\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\}
. We reuse our terminology and also call
\tilde{\eta}
a
boundary matching tube
.

Notation 6.3
.

We defined tubes
\eta
the previous section and tubes
\tilde{\eta}
in Definition
6.1
, both of which we call boundary mataching tubes. We always use the
\sim
symbol to distinguish the two.

Let
K\subset\bigl(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\eta}\text{ boundary}\\
\text{matching}\end{subarray}}\tilde{\eta}\bigr)\cup\bigl(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta\text{ Pontrjagin}\\
\text{-Thom}\end{subarray}}\zeta\bigr)

be a connected component, which we view (for now) as a topological space.
K
is composed by piecing together a sequence of tubes that alternate between Pontrjagin-Thom and boundary matching. (See Figure
22
for an illustration.)

Figure 22.
Left: a cycle
K
in
\partial\mathcal{C}(z)\cong\partial\mathcal{C}_{3}
, seen in Figure
22
. Right: the facet cycle
Z
that parametrizes
K
.

Indeed, the following procedure gives us a sequence
\zeta_{1},\tilde{\eta}_{1},\zeta_{2},\tilde{\eta}_{2}\ldots\zeta_{l},\tilde{\eta}_{l}
of consecutive tubes that alternate between Pontrjagin-Thom boundary matching such that
\bigcup_{i}\tilde{\eta}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{i}\zeta_{i}=K
:

(P-1)

Start with a Pontrjagin-Thom tube
\zeta_{1}\subset K
, which has end components
\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\}\times\{q_{1}\}
and
\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p^{\prime}_{2}\}\times\{q_{2}\}
.

(P-2)

\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p^{\prime}_{2}\}\subset\mathcal{C}(y_{1})
is boundary-matched with
\mathcal{C}(x_{2})\times\{p_{2}\}\subset\mathcal{C}(y_{1})
. There is a boundary matching tube
\tilde{\eta}_{1}
, which has ends
\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p^{\prime}_{2}\}\times\{q_{2}\}
and
\mathcal{C}(x_{2})\times\{p_{2}\}\times\{q_{2}\}
.

(P-3)

The interval
I\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{C}}(z,x_{2})
containing the endpoint
\{p_{2}\}\times\{q_{2}\}
has the other endpoint
\{p^{\prime}_{3}\}\times\{q_{3}\}
. There exists a Pontrjagin-Thom tube
\zeta_{2}
which has ends
\mathcal{C}(x_{2})\times\{p^{\prime}_{2}\}\times\{q_{2}\}
and
\mathcal{C}(x_{2})\times\{p_{3}\}\times\{q_{3}\}
.

(P-4)

Continue this process until we find a Pontrjagin-Thom tube connected to
\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\}\times\{q_{1}\}
.

Definition 6.4
.

Recall from Lemma
2.2
that
[\partial\mathcal{C}(z),K_{m}^{(m+1)}]\cong[S^{m+1},K_{m}^{(m+1)}]\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
, since we have already fixed
m>2
in this paper. For a cycle
K
of alternating Pontrjagin-Thom tubes and boundary matching tubes, the restriction
\mathfrak{c}|_{K}
specifies an element
[\mathfrak{c}|_{K}]\in[\partial\mathcal{C}(z),K_{m}^{(m+1)}]\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
, defined by sending
K
to
K_{m}^{(m+1)}
by
\mathfrak{c}
and
\partial\mathcal{C}(z)\backslash K
to the basepoint. By summing along all cycles
K
, we obtain the identity
\sum_{K\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(z)}[\mathfrak{c}|_{K}]=[\mathfrak{c}|_{\partial\mathcal{C}(z)}]\in\mathbb{Z}/2
.

This quantity
[\mathfrak{c}|_{\partial\mathcal{C}(z)}]
will end up being our coefficient for
\mathcal{C}(z)
in
\Sq^{2}(\mathbf{c})
.

Upon piecing together the whole
K
, we observe
K
looks like a tubular neighborhood of some closed curve, which we shall define.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
P
denote the midpoint of
e^{m}
and
P_{x}
denote the midpoint of
\mathcal{C}(x)
. We define the simple closed curve
\mathcal{K}\subset K
to be the union of the
\{P\}\times[0,1]
-identified subsets of the
\eta
’s and the
\{P_{x}\}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{C}}(z,x)
-identified subsets of the
\zeta
’s.

We observe that
K
is a fiber bundle over
\mathcal{K}
with fibers
e^{m}
, and
\mathcal{K}
looks like a “
0
-section” of
K
. We ask if we can trivialize this fiber bundle, and Proposition
6.7
answers this question in the affirmative. The style of our proof is similar to
[
LS14b
]
(the discussion preceding Lemma 3.9). But first we need a lemma.

Lemma 6.6
.

In a cycle
K
, there are an even number of boundary-incoherent tubes
\tilde{\eta}
.

We postpone the proof of this lemma to Section
7
.

Proposition 6.7
.

There exist exactly two trivializations
\Phi:K\hookrightarrow e^{m}\times\mathcal{K}
that satisfy the following:

•

For each Pontrjagin-Thom tube
\zeta\subset K
,
\Phi|_{\zeta}
is equal to

\zeta\cong e^{m}\times\mathcal{K}_{\zeta}\xhookrightarrow{\tau^{\omega}\times\Id}e^{m}\times\mathcal{K}_{\zeta},

where
\tau^{\omega}
is either
\tau^{0}=\Id
or
\tau^{1}=\tau
, and
\mathcal{K}_{\zeta}=\mathcal{K}\cap\zeta
.

•

For each boundary matching tube
\tilde{\eta}\subset K
,
\Phi|_{\tilde{\eta}}
is equal to

\tilde{\eta}\xhookrightarrow{\Theta^{-1}}e^{m}\times[0,1]\xhookrightarrow{\tau^{\omega}\times\Theta(P,\cdot)}e^{m}\times\mathcal{K}_{\tilde{\eta}},

where
\tau^{\omega}\in\{\Id,\tau\}
,
\mathcal{K}_{\tilde{\eta}}=\mathcal{K}\cap\tilde{\eta}
.

Furthermore, these two trivializations
\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}
are related by a flip in the
J
-factor
(\Phi^{\prime}=(\tau\times\Id_{\mathcal{K}})\circ\Phi)
.

Proof.

Use Steps (
P-1
)–(
P-4
) to find a sequence
\zeta_{1},\tilde{\eta}_{1},\zeta_{2},\tilde{\eta}_{2},\ldots,\zeta_{l},\tilde{\eta}_{l}
of consecutive d.s. tubes whose union is
K
. Following Definition
3.32
, we construct a single d.s. tube
T=\{\overline{\Theta}_{\sigma},\overline{\Lambda}_{\sigma}\}:=\zeta_{1}\cup\tilde{\eta}_{1}\cup\zeta_{2}\cup\tilde{\eta}_{2}\cup\ldots\cup\zeta_{l}\cup\tilde{\eta}_{l}
, with free ends being an end of
\zeta_{1}
and an end of
\tilde{\eta}_{l}
. All of the
\zeta_{i}
’s are boundary-coherent, and by Lemma
6.6
, an even number of the
\tilde{\eta}_{i}
’s are boundary-incoherent, so in total, an even number of the commponent tubes of
T
are boudary-incoherent. By induction, we find that
T
must be boundary-coherent. Therefore, the tube
\overline{\Theta}_{\sigma}:e^{m}\times[0,1]\to K
is identical on the ends
e^{m}\times\{0\},e^{m}\times\{1\}
, and so factors through a homeomorphism
e^{m}\times S^{1}\xhookrightarrow{\sim}K
. We define the
e^{m}
-component
\Phi_{e^{m}}
of
\Phi
by the composition
K\xhookrightarrow{\overline{\Theta}_{\sigma}^{-1}}e^{m}\times S^{1}\xrightarrow{\pi}e^{m}
, and we define the
\mathcal{K}
-component
\Phi_{\mathcal{K}}
of
\Phi
to be the canonical projection to the “
0
-section.” Now simply define
\Phi^{\prime}:K\to e^{m}\times\mathcal{K}
by
\Phi^{\prime}=(\tau\times\Id)\circ\Phi
.

The proof of uniqueness of the pair
\{\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}\}
is left as an exercise for the reader.
∎

Definition 6.8
.

Let
\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}
denote the canonical pair of trivializations of
K
from Proposition
6.7
. The pair
(K,\Phi)
(resp.
(K,\Phi^{\prime})
) specifies a class
[K,\Phi]
(resp.
[K,\Phi^{\prime}]
) in
[\partial\mathcal{C}(z),e^{m}/\partial e^{m}]\cong[S^{m+1},S^{m}]\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
, defined by the composition
\pi\circ\Phi
(resp.
\pi\circ\Phi^{\prime}
) on
K
and a constant map to the basepoint on
\partial C(z)\backslash K
. Since
\pi\circ\Phi
differs from
\pi\circ\Phi^{\prime}
by a flip in the
J
-factor,
[K,\Phi]
and
[K,\Phi^{\prime}]
are actually the same element, which we call
[K]
.

We now prove that the classes
[\mathfrak{c}|_{K}],[K]
are identical. We start by building a diagram. First consider the projection
\pi:e^{m}\times\mathcal{K}\to e^{m}
to the
e^{m}
-factor. Fix
\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\}\times\{q_{1}\}\subset K
, and let
\Phi:K\to e^{m}\times\mathcal{K}
be the trivialization of
K
defined in Proposition
6.7
such that
\pi\circ\Phi
agrees with
\mathfrak{c}|_{K}
on
\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\}\times\{q_{1}\}
. These maps are summarized in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{m}^{(m+1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{m}\times\mathcal{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

We will show that the above diagram commutes up to homotopy.

Lemma 6.9
.

The restriction
\mathfrak{c}|_{K}
is homotopic to
\pi\circ\Phi
relative
\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\}\times\{q_{1}\}\cup\partial K
. In other words, the above square commutes up to homotopy relative
(e^{m}\times\left(\{P\}\times\{p_{1}\}\times\{q_{1}\}\right))\cup(\partial e^{m}\times\mathcal{K})
.

Proof.

We consider the composition
\mathfrak{c}\circ\Phi^{-1}:e^{m}\times\mathcal{K}\to K_{m}^{(m+1)}
and the map
\pi:e^{m}\times\mathcal{K}\to K_{m}^{(m+1)}
. Using the characterization (
18
) of
K_{m}^{(m+1)}
(and a reshuffling of the
J
component in
e^{m}\times\mathcal{K}
), we can think of both
\mathfrak{c}\circ\Phi^{-1}
and
\pi
as maps

(23)

\displaystyle\left([-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A}\times[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{B}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(\kappa)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})\right)\times\left(J\times\mathcal{K}\right)

(24)

\displaystyle\to{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left([-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A}\times[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{B}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(\kappa)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})\right)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\partial$}\right.}\wedge{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left(\frac{J\times[0,1]}{((t,0)\sim(-t,1)}\right)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\partial$}\right.}

Note that in both
\mathfrak{c}\circ\Phi^{-1},\pi
, the first factor of (
23
) maps by quotient to the first factor of (
24
). Furthermore, the boundary of the second factor of (
23
) maps to the basepoint of (
24
). Therefore, both maps factor through maps

\displaystyle{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left([-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A}\times[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{B}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(\kappa)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})\right)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\partial$}\right.}\wedge{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left(J\times\mathcal{K}\right)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\partial$}\right.}

\displaystyle\to{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left([-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A}\times[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{B}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(\kappa)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})\right)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\partial$}\right.}\wedge{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left(\frac{J\times[0,1]}{((t,0)\sim(-t,1)}\right)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\partial$}\right.},

which are both
(m-1)
-fold suspensions of maps

f_{1},f_{2}:{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left(J\times\mathcal{K}\right)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\partial$}\right.}\to{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left(\frac{J\times[0,1]}{((t,0)\sim(-t,1)}\right)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\partial$}\right.}\cong\mathbb{R}P^{2},

where
f_{1}
is induced from
\mathfrak{c}\circ\Phi^{-1}
and
f_{2}
is induced from
g\circ\pi_{2}
.
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
agree on the subset
\{p_{1}\}\times\{q_{1}\}\times J\in\mathcal{K}\times J
, which implies that their homotopy classes (relative boundary) differ by an element
c\in\pi_{2}(\mathbb{R}P^{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}
. Taking suspensions, we see that
\Sigma^{m-1}f_{1}=\Sigma^{m-1}f_{2}+\Sigma^{m-1}c
. But the suspension map
\Sigma^{i}:\pi_{2}(\mathbb{R}P^{2})\to\pi_{i+2}(\Sigma^{i}\mathbb{R}P^{2})
is nullhomotopic for
i\geq 2
by Lemma
2.2
. Since
m\geq 3
, we have
\Sigma^{m-1}f_{1}\cong\Sigma^{m-1}f_{2}
relative
(\{p_{1}\}\times\{q_{1}\}\times J)\wedge e^{m}
. Therefore,
\mathfrak{c}\circ\Phi^{-1}
and
g\circ\pi
are homotopic relative
(e^{m}\times\{p_{1}\}\times\{q_{1}\})\cup(\mathcal{K}\times\partial e^{m})
.
∎

Proposition 6.10
.

For any cycle
K\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(z)
,
[K]=[\mathfrak{c}|_{K}]
.

Proof.

Fix
\mathcal{C}(x_{1})\times\{p_{1}\}\times\{q_{1}\}\subset K
as in Lemma
6.9
, and let
\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}:K\to e^{m}\times\mathcal{K}
denote the canonical trivializations. By Lemma
6.9
, either
[\mathfrak{c}|_{K}]=[K,\Phi]
or
[\mathfrak{c}|_{K}]=[K,\Phi^{\prime}]
, which are in both cases equal to
[K]
.
∎

7.
An introduction to special graph structures

The following definition is similar to a definition of Shütz
[
Sch22
]
, but not identical:

Definition 7.1

(
[
Sch22
]
)
.

A
special graph structure

\Gamma=\Gamma(V,E,E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime},S)
consists of a set of vertices
V
together with a function
S:V\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
, a subset
E
of edges, a subset
E^{\prime}\subset E
of edges, and a subset
E^{\prime\prime}\subset E-E^{\prime}
of directed edges.
Furthermore,
\Gamma
must satisfy the following criteria:

(G-1):

Each vertex is contained in two edges, with exactly one of the edges
e(v)
being in
E^{\prime}
.

(G-2):

If
e\in E^{\prime}
and
e=\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
, then
S(v_{1})\neq S(v_{2})
.

A
cubical special graph structure

\Gamma=\Gamma(V,E,E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime},S,S_{\mathbb{Z}},\sigma_{2})
is a special graph structure
\Gamma
equipped with maps
S_{\mathbb{Z}}:V\to\mathbb{Z}

\sigma_{2}:V\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
satisfying:

(G-3):

S_{\mathbb{Z}}(v_{1})=S_{\mathbb{Z}}(v_{2})
if
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\in E\backslash E^{\prime}
.

(G-4):

\sigma_{2}(v_{1})=\sigma_{2}(v_{2})
if
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\in E\backslash E^{\prime}
.

(G-5):

S_{\mathbb{Z}}(v_{1})\neq S_{\mathbb{Z}}(v_{2})
if
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}\in E^{\prime}
.

Example 7.1.1
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a signed cubical flow category,
\mu\in C^{l}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})
a cocycle, and
(\mathfrak{b}_{y},\mathfrak{s}_{y})
a facewise boundary matching for
\mu
. Given
z\in\Ob(\mathscr{C})
of grading
\gr(z)=\gr(x)+2
, we define a cubical special graph structure
\Gamma(z,\mu)
as follows. The vertex set
V
is the disjoint union

V=\coprod_{\gr(y)=l+1}\mathcal{M}(y,\mu)\times\mathcal{M}(z,y),

(Think of
V
as the set of chains
z\xrightarrow{q}y\xrightarrow{p}x
,
\gr(x)=\gr(y)-1=\gr(z)-2
, where
x\in\mu
and
y\in\Ob(\mathscr{C})
is some intermediate object.) Each interval component
I\subset\mathcal{M}(z,x)
,
x\in\mu
defines an edge in
E^{\prime}
: if
\partial I=\{p_{1}\circ q_{1},p_{2}\circ q_{2}\}
, then we have the edge
e=\{(p_{1},q_{1}),(p_{2},q_{2})\}\in E
.

We define the remaininng edges
E\backslash E^{\prime}
as follows: If
p\in\mathcal{M}(y,x)
is boundary matched with
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}(y,x^{\prime})
, then for all
q\in\mathcal{M}(z,y)
, we have an edge
e=\{(p,q),(p^{\prime},q)\}\in E\backslash E^{\prime}
. Furthermore, if
(p,p^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{s}_{y}
, then we require that
e
is directed from
(p,q)
to
(p^{\prime},q)
. Our construction directs every edge in
E\backslash E^{\prime}
, so we must define
E^{\prime\prime}:=E\backslash E^{\prime}
. For an example of a cycle in
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z,\mu)
, see Figure
23
.

We define
S:V\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
by
S(p,q):=S(p)+S(q)
, where the latter
S
denotes the cubical sign assignment
S(p)=s(\mathfrak{f}(p))+\sigma(p)
from Example
4.16
. Finally, we define
S_{\mathbb{Z}}(p,q)=S_{\mathbb{Z}}(q)
, where the latter
S_{\mathbb{Z}}
denotes the index assignment.
\sigma_{2}:V\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
is defined by
\sigma_{2}(p,q)=\sigma(q)
; in other words,
\sigma_{2}(v)
is the sign map
\sigma
applied to the second coordinate of
v\in V
.

Remark 7.1.1
.

The cubical special graph structure
\Gamma(z,\mu)
is a tool that records the relevant behavior of each cycle
K
. Let us start with the vertices
v=(p,q)
, which correspond with the embedded
\mathcal{C}(x)\times\{p\}\times\{q\}
. Furthermore, edges
\{v,v^{\prime}\}
correspond to tubes, with the ends
v,v^{\prime}
corresponding to tube ends. Edges
e\in E^{\prime}
correspond with Pontrjagin-Thom tubes
\mathcal{C}(x)\times I
and the edges
e\in E\backslash E^{\prime}
corresponding with boundary matching tubes
\tilde{\eta}
. These correspondences piece together so that every cycle
K
constructed in (
P-1
)–(
P-4
) indeed corresponds with a graph cycle
C=C(K)
in
\Gamma(z,\mu)
. Furthermore, the orientation of the edges in
E^{\prime\prime}
encodes the embedding of tubes
\tilde{\eta}
that match a face with itself.

Finally, the map
S_{\mathbb{Z}}
encodes the facets
\mathbf{G}_{i}
each embedded
\mathcal{C}(x)\times\{p\}\times\{q\}
lives in and the facets
\mathbf{G}_{ij}
each Pontrjagin-Thom tube
\zeta
lives in.

In view of Remark
7.1.1
, we say
C_{K}

parametrizes

K
.

6.6
:

for an example).

Figure 23.
Left: An example of a subset of chains
z\xrightarrow{q}y\xrightarrow{p}x
,
x\in\mu
. Middle: the functor
\mathfrak{f}
applied to the left diagram (the arrows are labled by the signs
S_{\mathbb{Z}}
). Right: A cycle
C\subset\Gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mu,z)
formed by these chains. We assume the squares in the left diagram corespond to components
I\subset\mathcal{M}(z,x)
(equivalently, from Pontrjagin-Thom tubes), and thus an edge
e\in E
which we draw as a straight line. We also assume the pairs
\{(p,p^{\prime}\}
are boundary-matched meaning there are edges
e\in E\backslash E^{\prime}
(which we draw as squiggly) between vertices
(p_{i},q_{i})
,
(p_{i}^{\prime},q_{i})
. Note how in this example,
S_{\mathbb{Z}}(p_{1})>S_{\mathbb{Z}}(p_{1}^{\prime})
and
S_{\mathbb{Z}}(p_{3})>S_{\mathbb{Z}}(p_{3}^{\prime})
, so we must direct the edges
\{(q_{1},p_{1}^{\prime}),(q_{1},p_{1})\}
,
\{(q_{3},p_{3}^{\prime}),(q_{3},p_{3})\}
as
(q_{1},p_{1}^{\prime})\rightsquigarrow(q_{1},p_{1})
and
(q_{3},p_{3}^{\prime})\rightsquigarrow(q_{1},p_{3})
.

\displaystyle\#

\displaystyle\{\text{boundary matching tubes in $K$}\}

\displaystyle=\#\{\text{edges $e\in E^{\prime}$ in $C_{K}$}\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{edges $e\in E^{\prime}$}\\
\text{in $C_{K}$}\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{edges}\\
\{v,v^{\prime}\}\in E^{\prime}\end{subarray}}S(v^{\prime})+S(v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\text{vertices $v$}}S(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{edges}\\
\{v,v^{\prime}\}\in E\backslash E^{\prime}\end{subarray}}S(v)+S(v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\#\{\text{edges $\{v,v^{\prime}\}\in E\backslash E^{\prime}$ in $C_{K}$}:S(v)\neq S(v^{\prime})\}

\displaystyle=\#\{\text{boundary-coherent tubes in $K$}\}\mod 2

Subtracting both sides of the equation by
\#\{\text{boundary-coherent tubes in $K$}\}
, we observe the number of boundary-incoherent arcs must be even.

8.
Parametrizing cycles
K

Consider cycles
K
living in
\partial\mathcal{C}(z)
. Boundary matching components lie in the faces
\mathbf{G}_{0},\ldots\mathbf{G}_{\kappa+1}
and the Pontrjagin-Thom components straddle two distinct faces. Recall that the our choice of boundary matching determines our cycles
K
, and this data is recorded in our cubical special graph structure
\Gamma(z,\mu)
.

.

e
as
e_{b}
.

Definition 8.2
.

Let
\Gamma=\Gamma(V,E,E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime},S,S_{\mathbb{Z}})
be a cubical special graph structure, and let

Let us consider the case
\Gamma=\Gamma(z,\mu)
, with

\overrightarrow{b}
,

of

Figure 24.
Left: A cycle
K
in
\partial\mathcal{C}(z)\subset\partial\mathcal{C}_{4}
. Right: the facet cycle
c
that parametrizes
K
. Note that this facet cycle, unlike the facet cycle in Figure
22
, has turnarounds.

So far, we have discussed how a cycle
C
in
\Gamma(z,\mu)
has signed facet cycle
Z(C)
, which takes values in
\{0\ldots\kappa+1\}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}
. The following construction examines a “reverse” procedure: given a cycle which takes values in
\{0\ldots\kappa+1\}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}
at each index, we create a “cycle”
K
living in
\partial\mathcal{C}(z)
. This
K
is
contrived
, in the sense that the Pontrjagin-Thom tubes in
K
do not arise from a cubical neat embedding, nor do the boundary matching tubes do not arise from a boundary matching
\mathfrak{m}
(see Definition
5.4
).

Definition 8.3
.

For
r\geq 0
, define

\displaystyle E(r)

\displaystyle:=[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A}\times[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{B}\times J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(r)

\displaystyle:=[-R,R]^{A}\times[-R,R]^{B}\times J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r)}(\overline{1},\overline{0}).

This definition gives a higher dimensional analogs of
e^{m}
and
\mathcal{C}(z)
. Indeed, fixing
r=\kappa
, we recover
e^{m}=E(\kappa)
and
\mathcal{C}(z)=\mathcal{C}(\kappa+2)
.

Construction 8.5
.

Suppose we are given a signed facet cycle
Z
(or an unsigned facet cycle
Z
, in which case we treat
Z
as a signed facet cycle with all signs
\omega=0
). We construct a tube cycle
K\cong E(r)\times S^{1}
living in
\partial\mathcal{C}(r+2)
as follows:

(T-1)

For every
(a,\omega^{\prime})\mathrel{\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(b,\omega)
, we define a d.s.
Pontrjagin-Thom
tube
\zeta
in
\mathbf{G}_{\{a,b\}}\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(r+2)
by defining first an embedding
\Theta:E(r)\times[0,1]\to\partial\mathcal{C}(r+2)
:

\displaystyle\Theta:E(r)\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\xhookrightarrow{\Id\times f}E(r)\times\Pi^{1}

\displaystyle=[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A+B}\times J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(\kappa)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})\times\Pi^{1}

\displaystyle=[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A+B}\times J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(\kappa+2)}(\overline{1}-\{a,b\},\overline{0})\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(\kappa+2)}(\overline{1},\overline{1}-\{a,b\})

\displaystyle\xhookrightarrow{\widehat{\iota}}[-R,R]^{A+B}\times J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(\kappa+2)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}(r+2).

Here,
f:[0,1]\to\Pi^{1}
is one of the two canonical identifications,
\widehat{\iota}(a,t,p,q)=(\gamma(q)+a,t,p\circ q)
, where
\gamma:\Pi^{1}\to[-R,R]^{A+B}
is a smoothly embedded path. We now define the second parametrization
\Lambda(x,t):=\Theta(x,1-t)
, thus defining our d.s. tube
\zeta=\{(\Theta,\alpha),(\Lambda,\beta)\}
. By possibly perturbing the
\gamma
’s for each
(a,\omega^{\prime})\mathrel{\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(b,\omega)
, we can ensure that the
\zeta
’s do not intersect.

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.17 -8.21 L -9.17 -8.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(c,\omega^{\prime\prime})
, we define a
boundary matching
tube
\tilde{\eta}=\{(\tilde{\Theta},0),(\tilde{\Lambda},1)\}
in
\mathbf{G}_{b}\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(z)
. We construct
\tilde{\eta}
so that it connects
\zeta^{\prime}
to
\zeta^{\prime\prime}
, matching
\zeta^{\prime}\cap\mathbf{G}_{b}\cong E(r)
with
\zeta^{\prime\prime}\cap\mathbf{G}_{b}\cong E(r)
. The construction is analagous to Section
5.2
, as it follows a similar outline that begins by defining an “unsigned” version
\tilde{\eta}_{\emptyset}=\{(\tilde{\Theta}_{\emptyset},0),(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\emptyset},1)\}
.

Indeed, if
a=c
, we define
\tilde{\Theta}_{\emptyset}
by defining the its projections

(1)

\tilde{\Theta}_{R},\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}:E(r)\times[0,1]\to[-R,R]^{A+B}
,

(2)

\tilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{M}}:E(r)\times[0,1]\to J\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r+2)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})
.

The map
\tilde{\Theta}_{R}
is defined by
\tilde{\Theta}_{R}(a,s,x,t)=\gamma(t)+a
for
a\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A+B}
,
s\in J
,
x\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(\kappa)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})
, where
\gamma:[0,1]\to[-R,R]^{A+B}
with the endpoints
\gamma(0),\gamma(1)

chosen to satisfy the equations

(25)

\tilde{\Theta}_{R}(E(r)\times\{0\})=\zeta^{\prime}\cap\mathbf{G}_{b},\qquad\tilde{\Theta}_{R}(E(r)\times\{1\})=\zeta^{\prime\prime}\cap\mathbf{G}_{b}.

This ensures that we can “continuously” travel from
\zeta^{\prime}
to
\tilde{\eta}
, and then through
\zeta^{\prime\prime}
. The projection
\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}
is defined by
\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}(x,t)=\tilde{\Theta}_{R}(x,-t)
.

The projection
\tilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{M}}
is defined in a way similar to the projection
\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}
in Section
5.2
, where the slices
E(r)
move directly into
\Int(J\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r+2)}(\overline{1},\overline{0}))
, but then pull up in the
J
-direction and back around into the same face
J\times G_{i}
. The projection
\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{M}}
is defined by
\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{M}}(x,t)=\tilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{M}}(\tau(x),-t)

If
a\neq c
, we first define a convex version
\tilde{\eta}^{\conv}=\{(\tilde{\Theta}^{\conv},0),(\tilde{\Lambda}^{\conv},1)\}
, where its comprising tubes
\tilde{\Theta}^{\conv},\tilde{\Lambda}^{\conv}
are determined by the following maps:

(1)

\tilde{\Theta}_{R}^{\conv},\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}^{\conv}:E(r)\times[0,1]\to[-R,R]^{A+B}
,

(2)

\tilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\conv},\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\conv}:E(r)\times[0,1]\to J\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r+2)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})
.

The map
\tilde{\Theta}^{\conv}_{R}
is defined in a similar way to before; namely,
\tilde{\Theta}^{\conv}_{R}(a,s,x,t)=\gamma(t)+a
, where
\gamma
is constructed to solve the boundary-matching conditions analagous to (
25
). We define
\tilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\conv}
as the following composition:

(26)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\conv}:E(r)\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\xhookrightarrow{\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}^{\conv}}J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(\kappa)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})

\displaystyle\cong J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r+1)}(\overline{1}-\{b\},\overline{0})\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r+1)}(\overline{1},\overline{1}-\{b\})

\displaystyle\xhookrightarrow{}J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r+2)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})

\displaystyle=\partial\mathcal{C}(r+2),

where the embedding
\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}^{\conv}
is defined in a similar way to the embedding
\Theta^{\conv}_{\mathcal{M}}
from Section
5.2
, moving from
J\times F_{j}
directly to
J\times F_{j}
(
i=a_{b}
,
j=c_{b}
).
\Lambda_{\mathcal{M}}^{\conv}
is defined by a composition similar to (
26
), but with
\Theta_{\mathcal{M}}^{\conv}
replaced with
\Lambda_{\mathcal{M}}^{\conv}
.

And similar to Section
5.2
, we define
\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}^{\conv}(x,t):=\tilde{\Theta}_{R}^{\conv}(x,1-t)
, and
\eta_{\emptyset}=\tilde{\eta}^{\conv}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},0)

Finally, we define
\tilde{\eta}
as the conjugation
\tau^{\omega}(\eta_{\emptyset})\tau^{-\omega}:=\{(\tau^{\omega}\tilde{\Theta}_{\emptyset}\tau^{-\omega},0),(\tau^{\omega}\tilde{\Lambda}_{\emptyset}\tau^{-\omega},1)\}
of
\eta_{\emptyset}
, where the comprising tubes get conjugated by the
J
-factor isometry
\tau^{\omega}
.

See Figure
25
for an example of this construction.

Figure 25.
Left: A cycle
K\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(z)
. Middle The facet cycle
c
of
K
. Right: a contrived cycle
K^{\prime}
parametrized by
K
. The blue Pontrjagin-Thom tubes were constructed in (

), and the magenta boundary matching tubes were constructed in (
(T-2)
). Notice here that boundary-matched components in
K
have to have the same
[-R,R]^{B}
coordinates, but there is no such requirement for
K^{\prime}
.

Definition 8.6
.

Let
\tilde{\eta}
be a boundary matching tube constructed in
(T-2)
, Construction
8.5
. We will denote the tube
\tilde{\eta}^{\conv}
used in the construction as the
convex version
of
\tilde{\eta}
.

Furthermore, if
U
is a d.s. tube parametrized by a facet chain, we denote the
convex version

U^{\conv}
of
U
to be a modification of
U
where we replace every boundary matching tube
\tilde{\eta}\subset U
with its convex version
\tilde{\eta}^{\conv}
. See Figure
33
for an illustration.

Definition 8.7
.

Let
Z
be a signed facet cycle and let
K
be as in Construction
8.5
. We say that
Z

parametrizes
the tube cycle
K
, and that
K
is a
(contrived) cycle
. If
K
is a cycle in
\partial\mathcal{C}(z)
arising from our cubical neat embedding
\iota^{\prime}
and boundary matching tubes
\eta\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(y)
, we call
K
an
honest cycle
.

Just as in Definition
6.5
, we define the
core

\mathcal{K}_{\zeta}
,
\mathcal{K}_{\tilde{\eta}}
of a Pontrjagin-Thom tube
\zeta
and a boundary matching tube
\tilde{\eta}
. For
\zeta\subset K
, define
\mathcal{K}_{\zeta}
to be the
\{P\}\times[0,1]
-identified subset of
\zeta
, and for
\tilde{\eta}\subset K
, define
\mathcal{K}_{\tilde{\eta}}
to be the
\{P\}\times[0,1]
-identified subset of
\tilde{\eta}
. Now define the
core

\mathcal{K}
of
K
to be the union of the cores of its comprising
\zeta
and
\tilde{\eta}
tubes.

Just as in Lemma
6.7
, there are two canonical ways to locate a point in
K
in terms of its “core” coordinate and its “fiber” coordinate.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
Z
be a signed facet cycle, and let
Z
parametrize the cycle
K=\bigcup_{\tilde{\eta}}\tilde{\eta}\cup\bigcup_{\zeta}\zeta
. Let
\mathcal{K}\subset K
be constructed as in Definition
6.5
. There are exactly two trivializations of the form
\Phi:K\to E(r)\times\mathcal{K}
that satisfy the following conditions:

•

For every Pontrjagin-Thom tube
\zeta
,
\Phi|_{\zeta}
is equal to

\zeta\xhookrightarrow{\Theta^{-1}}E(r)\times[0,1]\xhookrightarrow{\tau^{\omega}\times\Theta(P,\cdot)}E(r)\times\mathcal{K}_{\zeta},

where
\tau^{\omega}\in\{\Id,\tau\}
.

•

For every boundary matching tube
\tilde{\eta}
,
\Phi|_{\tilde{\eta}}
is equal to

\tilde{\eta}\xhookrightarrow{\Theta^{-1}}E(r)\times[0,1]\xhookrightarrow{\tau^{\omega}\times\Theta(P,\cdot)}E(r)\times\mathcal{K}_{\tilde{\eta}},

where
\tau^{\omega}\in\{\Id,\tau\}
.

These trivializations, which we call
\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}
, are related by a flip
\tau
in the
J
-factor in
E(r)\times\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

Let the components of
K
, ordered so that one tube follows another, be
\zeta_{1},\tilde{\eta}_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{l},\tilde{\eta}_{l}
. Note that all the
\zeta
tubes are boundary-coherent. One tricky part is to prove there are an even number of
\eta
boundary components, but the proof is along the same lines as the proof of Lemma
6.6
. The rest of the proof is similar to our proof of Lemma
6.7
, so we leave it to the reader to fill in the details.
∎

Similar to Definition
6.8
, both
\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}
define classes
[K,\Phi],[K,\Phi^{\prime}]\in[\partial\mathcal{C}_{\kappa+2},E(r)/\partial E(r)]
, which are identical, and which will call
[K]
.

Lemma 8.9
.

Let a facet cycle
Z
parametrize cycles
K\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(r+2)
,
K^{\prime}\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(s+2)
,
r,s\geq 0
. Then
[K]=[K^{\prime}]
.

Proof.

Suppose
r=s
, and thus
K
,
K^{\prime}
both live in the same set
\partial\mathcal{C}(r)
. Then since
K
,
K^{\prime}
are both parametrized by the same facet cycle
Z
, we can isotope
K
to
K^{\prime}
through a continuous family of cycles, all parametrized by
Z
. Thus
[K]=[K^{\prime}]
.

By the previous paragraph and an induction argument, it remains to prove the following statement: If
Z
parametrizes a cycle
K\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(r)
, then
Z
also parametrizes a cycle
K^{\prime}\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(r+1)
with
[K]=[K^{\prime}]
. So we fix
K
, which we see as a union of tubes
\zeta_{01}\cup\tilde{\eta}_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\zeta_{n0}\cup\tilde{\eta}_{0}
. With the Pontrjagin-Thom construction in mind, we describe a “suspension” of
K
to a cycle
K^{\prime}
.

Now
K
is an embedded
S^{1}
-family of
\mathcal{C}(r)=[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A}\times[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{B}\times J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})
in
\mathcal{C}(r+2)=[-R,R]^{A}\times[-R,R]^{B}\times J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r+2)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})
. Now consider
\mathbf{f}(K)
, where
\mathbf{f}
is the embedding
\mathbf{f}:\mathcal{C}(r+2)\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}_{\{0,\ldots,r+1\}}(\mathcal{C}(r+3))
defined by.

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}:

\displaystyle[-R,R]^{A}\times[-R,R]^{B}\times J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r+2)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})

\displaystyle=[-R,R]^{A}\times[-R,R]^{B}\times J\times\Pi^{0}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r+2)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})

\displaystyle\xhookrightarrow{\Id_{R}\times\Id_{R}\times\Id_{J}\times f}[-R,R]^{A}\times[-R,R]^{B}\times J\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r+3)}(\overline{1},\overline{0}),

where
f
is the embedding defined, for
r>0
, as

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r+2)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})=\Pi^{r+1}\times[0,1]\xhookrightarrow{f_{\{n\}}\times\Id}\Pi^{r+2}\times[0,1]=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r+3)}(\overline{1},\overline{0}),

and for
r=0
,
f
is defined as the embedding

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(2)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})=\Pi^{1}\times[0,1]\xhookrightarrow{}\Pi^{2}\times\{0\}\hookrightarrow\Pi^{2}\times[0,1]=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(2)}(\overline{1},\overline{0}).

See Figure
26
for an illustration of
\mathbf{f}
for
r+2=3
.

\lhook\joinrel\mathrel{\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\joinrel\longrightarrow

Figure 26.
The embedding
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r+2)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(r+3)}(\overline{1},\overline{0})
for
r=0

Observe that
\mathbf{f}
restricts to embeddings
\mathbf{G}_{i}(\mathcal{C}(r+2))\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}_{i}(\mathcal{C}(r+3))
,
\mathbf{G}_{\{i,j\}}(\mathcal{C}(r+2))\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}_{\{i,j\}}(\mathcal{C}(r+3))
, meaning that if
\eta\subset K
is in
\mathbf{G}_{\{i,j\}}(\mathcal{C}(r+2))
, then
\mathbf{f}(\eta)\subset\mathbf{G}_{\{i,j\}}(\mathcal{C}(r+3))
, and (similarly for
\zeta\subset K
). So we can view
\mathbf{f}(K)
as a “thinned out” version of a cycle in
\partial\mathcal{C}(r+3)
.

In the case
r>0
, we “thicken”
\mathbf{f}(K)
in the direction outwardly normal to
\mathbf{G}_{\{0,\ldots,r+1\}}(\mathcal{C}(r+3))\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(r+3)
to get a
S^{1}
-family of
E(r)
, which we view as a cycle
K^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{C}(r+3)
. In the case
r=0
, we simply “thicken”
\mathbf{f}(K)
uniformly in the
[0,1]
-coordinate of
\mathcal{C}(r+3)
to obtain
K^{\prime}
. (See Figure
27
for an illustration of an example of
K^{\prime}
.)

The class
[K]
is an element of
[\partial\mathcal{C}(r+2),\mathcal{C}(r)]\cong[S^{A+B+r+2},S^{A+B+\kappa^{\prime}+r+1}]\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
and
[K^{\prime}]
is an element of
[\partial\mathcal{C}(r+3),\mathcal{C}(r+1)]\cong[S^{A+B+r+3},S^{A+B+r+1}]\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
. Since
K^{\prime}
was defined from
K
through a Pontrjagin-Thom like construction, we see
[K^{\prime}]=\Sigma[K]=[K]
.

Figure 27.
Left: A loop in the 3-vertex complete graph and the cycle
K
it parametrizes. Right: The same loop embedded in the 4-vertex complete graph and the cycle
K^{\prime}
it parametrizes. As proved in Lemma
8.9
, the map
S^{(A+B)+2}\to S^{(A+B)+1}
from the right figure is the suspension of the map from the left figure
S^{(A+B)+3}\to S^{(A+B)+2}
.

∎

are unsigned.

Lemma 9.1
.

is parametrized by the facet cycle
a\mathrel{\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

. We have
[K]=ab+bc+ac+a+b+c+1
.

Proof.

See Figure
28
.

Figure 28.
Left: an illustration of a
3
-cycle
K
. Right: an illustration of
K^{\conv}
. In the proof of Lemma
9.1
, we write
K
as
K^{\conv}
plus some twists.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
a<b<c
.
K
is composed of boundary matching tubes
\tilde{\eta}_{a},\tilde{\eta}_{b},\tilde{\eta}_{c}
lying in facets
\mathbf{G}_{a}
,
\mathbf{G}_{b}
,
\mathbf{G}_{c}
, and Pontrjagin-Thom tubes
\zeta_{ab},\zeta_{bc},\zeta_{ac}
lying in facets
\mathbf{G}_{\{a,b\}}
,
\mathbf{G}_{\{b,c\}}
,
\mathbf{G}_{\{a,c\}}
. Before we investigate
K
, we construct a new tube
K^{\conv}
by modifying the boundary matching tubes. In place of the
\tilde{\eta}
tubes defined in Construction
8.5
, we define tubes
T_{a},T_{b},T_{c}
as follows: For each
\tilde{\eta}_{r}
, replace the
\{\Theta_{r},\Lambda_{r}\}
, used to define
\tilde{\eta}_{r}
in Section
5.2
, with the convex tube
T_{r}=\{\Theta_{r}^{\conv},\Lambda_{r}^{\conv}\}
. This should result in a more “flat” looking cycle
K^{\conv}
. Furthermore, if we view
K^{\conv}
as a d.s. tube

K^{\conv}=T_{a}\cup\zeta_{ac}\cup T_{c}\cup\zeta_{bc}\cup T_{b}\cup\zeta_{ab},

then
K^{\conv}
is boundary-coherent, meaning we can trivialize
K^{\conv}
as in Proposition
6.7
, and define an element
[K^{\conv}]\in\mathbb{Z}/2
. Note that
[K^{\conv}]=0
, since
K^{\conv}
looks like an
S^{1}
family of fibers
E(r)
that circles once around a hexagon without any further twisting.

Now we note that if we view
K
as a d.s. tube

K=\tilde{\eta}_{a}\cup\zeta_{ac}\cup\tilde{\eta}_{c}\cup\zeta_{bc}\cup\tilde{\eta}_{b}\cup\zeta_{ab},

then we can obtain
K
by adding twists to
K^{\conv}
(see Figure
29
).

Figure 29.
A diagram of the boundary matching and Pontrjagin-Thom neighborhoods behave in a 3-cycle. The straight edges represent Pontrjagin-Thom tubes and the coily edges represent boundary matching tubes. The coils represent the possibly twists that have been added.

Indeed,
\tilde{\eta}_{a}=T_{a}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{c-2}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{b},\alpha_{a})
,
\tilde{\eta}_{b}=T_{b}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{c-2}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{a+1},\alpha_{b})
,
\tilde{\eta}_{c}=T_{c}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{b-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{a+1},\alpha_{c})
. Therefore, we can use Proposition
3.33
to compare
K
with
K^{\conv}

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=(T_{a}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{c-2}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{b},\alpha_{a}))\cup\zeta_{ab}\cup(T_{b}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{c-2}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{a+1},\alpha_{b}))\cup\zeta_{bc}

\displaystyle\quad\cup(T_{c}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{b-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{a+1},\alpha_{c}))\cup\zeta_{ac}

\displaystyle\cong(T_{a}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{b}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{c-2},\alpha_{a}))\cup\zeta_{ab}\cup(T_{b}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{c-2}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{a+1},\beta_{b})\cup\zeta_{bc}

\displaystyle\quad\cup(T_{c}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{a+1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{b-1},\beta_{c}))\cup\zeta_{ac}

\displaystyle(\text{using Example \ref{Vij twists} to rewrite the boundary matching tubes})

\displaystyle\cong(T_{a}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{b}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{c-2},\beta_{a})\cup\zeta_{ab}\cup((T_{b}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{c-2}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{a+1},\alpha_{b})\diamond(\tau^{-\omega}(\bm{\varphi}_{a+1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{b-1})\tau^{\omega},\alpha_{b})\cup\zeta_{bc}

\displaystyle\quad\cup T_{c}\cup\zeta_{ac}

\displaystyle(\text{by Proposition \ref{twist through concat}, setting }\omega=c+a\mod 2)

\displaystyle\cong(T_{a}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{b}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{c-2},\beta_{a})\cup\zeta_{ab}\cup(T_{b}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{c-2}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{a+1}\tau^{-\omega}(\bm{\varphi}_{a+1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{b-1})\tau^{\omega},\alpha_{b})\cup\zeta_{bc}

\displaystyle\quad\cup T_{c}\cup\zeta_{ac}

\displaystyle\cong(T_{a}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{b}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{c-2},\beta_{a})\cup\zeta_{ab}\cup(T_{b}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{c-2}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{a+1}(\bm{\varphi}_{a+1}^{-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{b-1}^{-1}),\alpha_{b})\cup\zeta_{bc}\cup T_{c}\cup\zeta_{ac}

\displaystyle\quad+((\omega+1)(b-a-1))

\displaystyle\cong(T_{a}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{b}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{c-2},\beta_{a}))\cup\zeta_{ab}\cup(T_{b}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{c-2}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{b},\alpha_{b}))\cup\zeta_{bc}\cup T_{c}\cup\zeta_{ac}

\displaystyle\quad+((\omega+1)(b-a-1))

\displaystyle\cong(T_{a}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{b}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{c-2}\tau^{-\omega^{\prime}}(\bm{\varphi}_{c-2}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{b})\tau^{\omega^{\prime}},\beta_{a}))\cup\zeta_{ab}\cup T_{b}\cup\zeta_{bc}\cup T_{c}\cup\zeta_{ac}

\displaystyle\quad+((\omega+1)(b-a-1))

\displaystyle(\text{by Proposition \ref{twist through concat}, setting }\omega=b+c\mod 2)

\displaystyle\cong T_{a}\cup\zeta_{ab}\cup T_{b}\cup\zeta_{bc}\cup T_{c}\cup\zeta_{ac}+((\omega^{\prime}+1)(c-b-1))+((\omega+1)(b-a-1))

\displaystyle=K^{\conv}+(ab+bc+ac+a+b+c+1),

implying
[K]=[K^{\conv}]+ab+bc+ac+a+b+c+1=ab+bc+ac+a+b+c+1
.
∎

10.
Simplifying facet cycles
Z
without turnarounds

We again only consider unsigned facet cycles
Z
in this section.

To motivate our strategy, we recall Remark
3.9.2
, which says a loop in
\Pi^{n-1}
occupying facets of the form
G_{i}
,
G_{\{i,j\}}
can nullhomotope by only traveling through facets of the form
G_{\{i,j,k\}}
. We adapt this remark by noting
K
is a tubular cycle that only passes through facets of the form
\mathbf{G}_{i}
,
\mathbf{G}_{\{i,j\}}
, meaning we can isotope
K
to a small loop by only passing
K
through facets of the form
\mathbf{G}_{\{i,j,k\}}
. It is imporatant, therefore, for us to know how
K
looks after each traversal. We explore this behavior on the level of facet cycles.

Consider a (unsigned) facet cycle
Z
that has no turnarounds, that is, there are no portions that look like

\rightsquigarrow

Figure 31.
For this path move to be valid, all triples of adjacent numbers in both the left and right diagram should consist of different numbers (e.g.
c,d,e
must be different, but so must

).

We call this act of replacing
D
with
D^{\prime}
, thus replacing
Z
with
Z^{\prime}
, an
elementary move
. Our goal in this section is to study the difference
[K]-[K^{\prime}]
if
K
(resp.
K^{\prime}
) is parametrized by
Z
(resp.
Z^{\prime}
), and
Z
,
Z^{\prime}
differ by an elementary move.

We briefly outline this section’s argument: Observe that we can obtain
K^{\prime}
by replacing a tubular cutout
U\subset K
(parametrized by
D
) with another d.s. tube
U^{\prime}
(parametrized by
D^{\prime}
). (See Figure
32
for an illustration.)

Figure 32.
Left: The tubular chain

parametrized by
a\text{---}b\text{---}c\text{---}d\text{---}e
. , Right: The tubular chain

parametrized by
a\text{---}b\text{---}d\text{---}e
. We can imagine that
U
is homotopic to
U^{\prime}
plus a twist.

We observe that if
U-U^{\prime}=(\omega)
, then
[K]-[K^{\prime}]=\omega\in\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Figure 36.
A step-by-step illustration of how we simplify

Our computation is as follows:

(29)

\displaystyle U-U^{\conv}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}_{b}\cup\zeta_{bc}\cup\tilde{\eta}_{c}\cup\zeta_{cd}\cup\tilde{\eta}_{d}-T_{b}\cup\zeta_{bc}\cup T_{c}\cup\zeta_{cd}\cup T_{d}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}_{b}\cup V_{1}\cup\tilde{\eta}_{d}-T_{b}\cup V_{1}^{\conv}\cup T_{d}

(30)

\displaystyle\cong\tilde{\eta}_{b}\cup V_{2}\cup\tilde{\eta}_{d}-T_{b}\cup V_{2}^{\conv}\cup T_{d}+(bc+cd+bd+b+c+d+1)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}_{b}\cup\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}_{b}\cup\zeta_{bd}\cup\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}_{d}\cup\tilde{\eta}_{d}-T_{b}\cup T^{\prime}_{b}\cup\zeta_{bd}\cup T^{\prime}_{d}\cup T_{d}+(bc+cd+bd+b+c+d+1)

\displaystyle\cong(T_{b}\diamond(g_{b},\alpha))\cup(T^{\prime}_{b}\diamond(g^{\prime}_{b},\alpha^{\prime}))\cup\zeta_{bd}\cup(T^{\prime}_{d}\diamond(g^{\prime}_{d},\beta^{\prime}))\cup(T_{d}\diamond(g_{d},\beta))

\displaystyle\quad-T_{b}\cup T^{\prime}_{b}\cup\zeta_{bd}\cup T^{\prime}_{d}\cup T_{d}+(bc+cd+bd+b+c+d+1)

\displaystyle(\text{$g_{b}$, $g_{d}$, $g^{\prime}_{b}$, $g^{\prime}_{d}$ are defined using Table \ref{pulling along cycle table}})

\displaystyle\cong(T_{b}\diamond(f_{b},\alpha))\cup T^{\prime}_{b}\cup\zeta_{bd}\cup T^{\prime}_{d}\cup(T_{d}\diamond(f_{d},\beta))-T_{b}\cup T^{\prime}_{b}\cup\zeta_{bd}\cup T^{\prime}_{d}\cup T_{d}

\displaystyle\quad+(bc+cd+bd+b+c+d+1)

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}(\text{$f_{b}$, $f_{d}$ are defined using Table \ref{pulling along cycle table}. The}\\
\text{equivalence is due to Proposition \ref{twist through concat}.})\end{gathered}

(33)

\displaystyle\cong(T_{b}\diamond(f_{b},\alpha))\cup V_{2}^{\conv}\cup(T_{d}\diamond(f_{d},\beta))-T_{b}\cup V_{2}^{\conv}\cup T_{d}

\displaystyle\quad+(bc+cd+bd+b+c+d+1)

(34)

\displaystyle\cong(T_{b}\diamond(f_{b},\alpha)\cup V_{1}^{\conv}\cup(T_{d}\diamond(f_{d},\beta))-T_{b}\cup V_{1}^{\conv}\cup T_{d}

\displaystyle\quad+(bc+cd+bd+b+c+d+1)

\displaystyle\cong U^{\conv}\diamond(f_{b},\alpha)\diamond(f_{d},\beta)-U^{\conv}+(bc+cd+bd+b+c+d+1),

where (

), (

), (

), (

) represent the

,

,

, and

rows of Figure

Proof.

Our idea is to homotope
U
into a tube that, just like
U^{\prime}
, only occupies the facets
\mathbf{G}_{b}
,
\mathbf{G}_{\{b,d\}}
,
\mathbf{G}_{d}
. It is after this homotopy that we can more easily compute
U-U^{\prime}
. Following the illustration in Figure
37
,

:

\cong

\cong

\cong

Figure 37.
The homotopy of
U^{\conv}
(top left) to
U^{\prime\conv}\diamond(\rho_{b},\alpha)\diamond(\rho_{d},\beta)
(bottom right) described in the proof of Theorem
10.2
. We first homotope
U^{\conv}
to
W
(bottom left) by flipping over
\mathbf{G}_{\{b,c,d\}}
, and then using Example
3.32.1
, we homotope
W
to
U^{\prime\conv}\diamond(\rho_{b},\alpha)\diamond(\rho_{d},\beta)
.

we start with the tube
U^{\conv}
, lifting the far end
T_{c}\subset\mathbf{G}_{c}
up in the
+J
-direction, and carrying it through the face
\mathbf{G}_{\{b,c,d\}}
, “turning”
T_{c}
over and laying it back down in the facet
\mathbf{G}_{bd}
. The result
W
is a concatenation of several tubes: On one end, we have a tube in
\mathbf{G}_{b}
which moves directly from
\mathbf{G}_{a}
to
\mathbf{G}_{c}
, a tube in
\mathbf{G}_{\{b,c\}}
which flips up and around back into
\mathbf{G}_{b}
, and a tube in
\mathbf{G}_{b}
that moves directly from
\mathbf{G}_{c}
to
\mathbf{G}_{d}
. These tubes look like they arise from Example
3.32.1
, and in fact, this is exactly what we use. We describe
W
as

W\cong(T^{\prime}_{b}\cup\zeta_{bd}\cup T^{\prime}_{d})\diamond(\rho_{b},\alpha)\diamond(\rho_{d},\beta)=U^{\prime\conv}\diamond(\rho_{b},\alpha)\diamond(\rho_{d},\beta),

where we consult the second column of Table
3
to define
\rho_{b}
,
\rho_{d}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\cong U^{\conv}\diamond(f_{b},\alpha)\diamond(f_{d},\beta)+(bc+cd+bd+b+c+d+1)

\displaystyle\cong W\diamond(f_{b},\alpha)\diamond(f_{d},\beta)+(bc+cd+bd+b+c+d+1)

\displaystyle\cong(U^{\prime\conv}\diamond(\rho_{b},\alpha)\diamond(\rho_{d},\beta))\diamond(f_{b},\alpha)\diamond(f_{d},\beta)+(bc+cd+bd+b+c+d+1)

\displaystyle\cong U^{\prime\conv}\diamond(\rho_{b}\diamond f_{b},\alpha)\diamond(\rho_{d}\diamond f_{d},\beta)+(bc+cd+bd+b+c+d+1),

where
f_{b}
,
f_{d}
are determined in the column
3
of Table
3
. Furthermore, note
U^{\prime}\cong U^{\prime\conv}\diamond(f^{\prime}_{b},\alpha)\diamond(f^{\prime}_{d},\beta)
, where
f^{\prime}_{b}
,
f^{\prime}_{d}
defined in column
4
of Table
3
. Our computation of
U-U^{\prime}
proceeds as follows:

\displaystyle U-U^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(U^{\prime\conv}\diamond(\rho_{b}\diamond f_{b},\alpha)\diamond(\rho_{d}\diamond f_{d},\beta)+(bc+cd+bd+b+c+d+1)\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(U^{\prime\conv}\diamond(f^{\prime}_{b},\alpha)\diamond(f^{\prime}_{d},\beta)\right)

\displaystyle=(\rho_{b}\diamond f_{b}\diamond f_{b}^{\prime-1},\alpha)\diamond(\rho_{d}\diamond f_{d}\diamond f_{d}^{\prime-1},\beta)+(bc+cd+bd+b+c+d+1).

We can verify case-by-case that the twists
\rho_{b}\diamond f_{b}\diamond f_{b}^{\prime-1}
and
\rho_{d}\diamond f_{d}\diamond f_{d}^{\prime-1}
are always full twists, and in fact we can write
\rho_{b}\diamond f_{b}\diamond f_{b}^{\prime-1}=(\omega_{b})
,
\rho_{d}\diamond f_{d}\diamond f_{d}^{\prime-1}=(\omega_{d})
, where
\omega_{b}
,
\omega_{d}
are defined in the last column of Table
3
. We finally have
U-U^{\prime}=(\omega_{b})+(\omega_{d})+(bc+cd+bd+b+c+d+1)
, as promised.
∎

using Lemma

Suppose that

,

parametrize the cycles

,

(35)

where we again consult the fifth column of Table
3
to determine the constants
\omega_{b},\omega_{d}
.

Proof.

, it suffices to prove Equation (

) for just
some

K^{\prime}
parametrized by

. Now let

be the tube parametrized by

to yield a cycle

. Since

, we have the identity

∎

11.
Computing
[K]
for more cycles
K
.

Let
K
be parametrized by
Z
, where
Z
is an (unsigned) facet cycle. We derive a formula for
[K]
that generalizes our result for
3
-cycles. Starting with Corollary
10.3
, we have explicit formula for
[K]-[K^{\prime}]
, so long as their facet cycles
Z
,
Z^{\prime}
differ by an elementary move. We now have a strategy for computing
[K]
for cycles
K
with no turnarounds. Indeed, we denote
Z
the facet cycle for
K
and look for a sequence
Z=Z_{1}\rightsquigarrow Z_{2}\rightsquigarrow\ldots\rightsquigarrow Z_{r}
of elementary moves terminating at a
3
-cycle, and repeatedly apply Corollary
10.3
. In the spirit of this strategy, we derive an explicit function
Q:\{\text{facet cycles }Z\}\to\mathbb{Z}/2
such that
Q(Z)-Q(Z^{\prime})
measures the difference
[K]-[K^{\prime}]
for any two facet cycles
Z
,
Z^{\prime}
differing by an elementary move.

is well-defined.

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
K
be parametrized by a facet cycle
Z
and let
K^{\prime}
be parametrized by a facet cycle
Z^{\prime}
. Suppose that
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
differ by an elementary move. We have the equality of differences
Q(Z)-Q(Z^{\prime})=[K]-[K^{\prime}]
.

We compare
Q(Z)
with
Q(Z^{\prime})
, summand by summand:

Difference of
\sum_{a\text{---}b}ab
:

The difference is
bc+bd+cd
.

Difference of
\sum_{b}b
:

The difference is
c
.

Difference of
\sum_{a\text{---}b}\max(a,b)

The difference is
\max(b,c)+\max(c,d)+\max(b,d)=\Mid(b,c,d)
.

Difference of
\sum_{a\text{---}b\text{---}c}\Mid(a,b,c)
:

\Mid(a,c,d)+\Mid(b,c,d)+\Mid(b,c,e).

Indeed, notice that between
Z_{K}
and
Z_{K^{\prime}}
, the list of consective triples of vertices differs by only
5
triples: namely
\{a,b,c\}
,
\{b,c,d\}
,
\{c,d,e\}
,
\{a,c,d\}
,
\{b,c,e\}
. Therefore, it suffices to prove that

(42)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Mid(a,b,c)+\Mid(b,c,d)+\Mid(c,d,e)+\Mid(a,c,d)+\Mid(b,c,e)\\
&\quad=\Mid(a,c,d)+\Mid(b,c,d)+\Mid(b,c,e)\end{split}

We use the following lemma:

Lemma 11.3
.

Let
p,q,r,s
be four possibly repeating integers. Then

\Mid(p,q,r)+\Mid(p,q,s)+\Mid(p,r,s)+\Mid(q,r,s)=0\mod 2.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, it suffices to assume
p\leq q\leq r\leq s
. The lemma follows in this case.
∎

Using Lemma
11.3
now, we prove (
42
) by observing

\displaystyle\Mid(a,b,c)+\Mid(a,b,d)

\displaystyle=\Mid(b,c,d)+\Mid(a,c,d),

\displaystyle\Mid(a,b,c)+\Mid(a,b,d)

\displaystyle=\Mid(b,c,d)+\Mid(a,c,d)

Difference of
\left(\#\left\{a\text{---}b\text{---}c\kern 5.0pt\middle|\text{ $b$ is not between $a$ and $c$}\right\}/2\right)
:

1|_{b<\Mid(a,c,d)}+1|_{d<\Mid(e,c,b)}+1
. We can verify this fact case-by-case.

Adding up the differences:

We get

\displaystyle Q(Z_{K})-Q(Z_{K^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=bc+bd+cd+c

\displaystyle\quad+\Mid\{b,c,d\}+(\Mid(a,c,d)+\Mid(b,c,d)+\Mid(b,c,e))

\displaystyle=bc+bd+cd+b+^{\prime}c+d+1+\Mid(a,c,d)|_{b}+\Mid(b,c,e)|_{d}

\displaystyle=bc+bd+cd+b+c+d+1+\omega^{\prime}_{b}+\omega^{\prime}_{d}\mod 2,

where we refer to the third column of Table

for

. By Corollary

Q(Z_{K})-Q(Z_{K^{\prime}})=[K]-[K^{\prime}]
.
∎

Definition 11.4
.

By Lemma
8.9
, there exists a well-defined function
\mathcal{Q}:\{\text{signed facet cycles}\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
such that
\mathcal{Q}(Z)=[K]
for every
K
parametrizing
Z
. Now let
\widetilde{Q}:B\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
be a map, where
B\subset\{\text{signed facet cycles}\}
is some subset. We say that
\widetilde{Q}
is
sincere on
B
if
\widetilde{Q}|_{B}=\mathcal{Q}|_{B}
. If
\widetilde{Q}\equiv\mathcal{Q}
, we simply say
\widetilde{Q}
is
sincere
.

.

The map
Q
defined in Definition

is sincere on the set of unsigned facet cycles
Z
without backtracks.

Proof.
