12.
Proving
Q
is sincere, and a general formula for
\Sq^{2}

In the previous section, we only looked at instances where
[K]
is parametrized by an unsigned facet cycle
Z
with no turnarounds. In this section, we now examine all cases of
K
, so we include the possibility that
Z
has both turnarounds and signs.

Proposition 12.1
.

Q
is sincere.

Proof.

The outline of our proof is proving the above formula in successively general levels.

•

Level 1:
Q
is sincere on the set of unsigned facet cycles without turnarounds

•

Level 2:
Q
is sincere on the set of unsigned facet cycles
Z
where all the turnarounds of
Z
point in the same direction.

•

Level 3:
Q
is sincere on the set of unsigned facet cycles.

•

Level 4:
Q
is sincere.

We already proved Level 1 in Lemma
11.2
. The following remark shows that it suffices to prove Level 2:

Remark 12.1.1
.

Level 3 follows from Level 2.

Proof.

let
Z
be a facet cycle with turnarounds. If we switch the orientation of a turnaround, we see that
[K]
changes by adding
1
. But so does the quantity (
40
) in
Q(Z)
. We can repeat the process of switching turnarounds in
Z
to reduce ourselves to Level 2.
∎

Proof of Level 2 (all turnarounds in the same direction):

From any unsigned facet cycle
Z
, we can perform a sequence of simplifying moves to obtain a cycle
Z^{\prime}
that either 1. does not contain any direct turnarounds, or 2. is a
2
-cycle

. If
a=b=d
, then the orientation of the vertex
b\in D^{\prime}
should agree with the orientation of the vertex
c\in D
. The move
D\rightsquigarrow D^{\prime}
, and likewise,
Z\rightsquigarrow Z^{\prime}
, is called a
pruning move
.

We exhibit these pruning moves in the first column of Table
5
.

Definition 12.3
.

Given (possibly repeating) integers
a,b,c
, we define
\sgn(a,b,c)
as the sign of the permutation

(a,b+1/4,c+1/2)\mapsto(\min\{a,b+1/4,c+1/2\},\Mid\{a,b+1/4,c+1/2\},\max\{a,b+1/4,c+1/2\}).

We can imagine
\sgn(a,b,c)
to generically mean the sign of the permutation

(a,b,c)\mapsto(\text{min}\{a,b,c\},\Mid\{a,b,c\},\text{max}\{a,b,c\}),

while imagining in the cases that
a=b
,
b=c
, and
a=c
, that respectively
b
is a little bigger than
a
,
c
is a little bigger than
b
, and
c
is a little bigger than
a
.

and

. Then

Pruning move
Z\rightsquigarrow Z^{\prime}

Difference
[K]-[K^{\prime}]

\rightsquigarrow

w\neq y=z

-

\rightsquigarrow

\rightsquigarrow

\rightsquigarrow

w,y,z
are all different

Table 5
.
The following table gives us how the type of pruning move
Z\rightsquigarrow Z^{\prime}
determines the difference
[K]-[K^{\prime}]
. The change in each case is
y_{x}+(\Mid\{w,y,z\})_{x}+\sgn(w,y,z)
.

Proof.

We break up our proof into cases of pruning moves
Z\rightsquigarrow Z^{\prime}
described in the rows of Table
5
. We may assume that
K^{\prime}
is defined by taking the tube
U\subset K
parametrized by
D
, and replacing it with a tube
U^{\prime}
parametrized by
D^{\prime}
. And just as in Corollary
10.3
, it suffices to compute
U-U^{\prime}=([K]-[K^{\prime}])
. We first look at the second, third, and fourth rows, since they are the easiest.

Rows 1 and 2

The difference
U-U^{\prime}
can be seen as the following:

-

And as we can see,
U\cong U^{\prime}
, since
U^{\prime}
is a homotopy of
U
by “pulling”
U
tight between faces
\mathbf{F}_{y}
and
\mathbf{F}_{x}
. The third row can be visualized similarly, and in both cases,
U-U^{\prime}=(0)
.

Row 3:

The difference
U-U^{\prime}
can be visualized as the following:

\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{third_row_before.pdf}}\end{picture}\raisebox{28.45274pt}{ $-$ }\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{third_row_after.pdf}}\put(0.16651,0.59581){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$a$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.72719,0.90974){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$b$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{third_row_after.pdf}}\end{picture},

which can be seen as unfolding a crease in
U
between faces
\mathbf{F}_{x}
and
\mathbf{F}_{y}
. So
U\cong U^{\prime}
, and therefore
U-U=(0)
.

So in other words, the
i^{\text{th}}
face and

face of the face

are the faces that point twoards to

and

.

By a repeated application of Proposition
3.29
, we see

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=U^{\conv}+(\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\alpha)+(\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\beta)

\displaystyle=U^{\conv}+(\bm{\varphi}_{j-2}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\alpha)+(\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\beta)

\displaystyle=\ldots

\displaystyle=U^{\conv}+(\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\alpha)+(\bm{\varphi}_{i+2}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\beta)

\displaystyle=U^{\conv}+(\bm{\varphi}_{i+1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\beta)

\displaystyle=U^{\conv}+(j-i-1),

where we realize the last equality by realizing each diamond composition of half-twists
\bm{\varphi}\diamond\bm{\varphi}
becomes a full twist
(1)
.
Therefore,
U-U^{\conv}=U-U^{\prime}=i+j-1=a_{b}+c_{b}-1
.

The case
a>c
is similar to our worked case
a<c
.

U^{\conv}\cong U^{\prime\conv}\diamond(\bm{\rho},\alpha)
, where
\bm{\rho}=[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{A+B}\times\rho\times[0,1]
, and
\rho
is the twist determined by Table
1
with input

c_{b}
d_{b}
a_{b}

. So in total, we observe
(U^{\conv}-U^{\prime\conv})=(\bm{\rho},\Theta^{\prime\conv}_{ad})
and thus compute

U-U^{\prime\conv}=(\bm{\rho},\alpha)\diamond(U-U^{\conv}),\qquad U-U^{\prime}=(U^{\prime\conv}-U^{\prime})\diamond(U-U^{\prime\conv}).

a<d<c
.

. We compute

Thus, we have

\displaystyle U-U^{\prime\conv}

\displaystyle=(\bm{\varphi}_{k\rightleftarrows j},\alpha)\diamond((\bm{\varphi}_{k-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\alpha)\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{k-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{j+1},\beta))

\displaystyle=(\bm{\varphi}_{k\rightleftarrows j}\bm{\varphi}_{k-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\alpha)\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{k-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{j+1},\beta)

\displaystyle=(\bm{\varphi}_{k-1}\bm{\varphi}_{k-1\rightleftarrows j}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\alpha)\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{k-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{j+1},\beta)

\displaystyle=\ldots

\displaystyle=(\bm{\varphi}_{k-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{j+1}\bm{\varphi}_{j+1\rightleftarrows j}^{(-1)^{k-j-1}}\bm{\varphi}_{j}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\alpha)\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{k-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{j+1},\beta)

\displaystyle=(\bm{\varphi}_{k-1}\ldots\widehat{\bm{\varphi}_{j}}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\Theta^{\prime\conv}_{ad})\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{k-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{j+1},\Theta^{\prime\conv}_{da})+(k-j-1)

\displaystyle=(\bm{\varphi}_{k-1}^{-1}\bm{\varphi}_{k-1}\ldots\widehat{\bm{\varphi}_{j}}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\alpha)\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{k-2}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{j+1},\beta)+(k-j-1)

\displaystyle=\ldots

\displaystyle=(\bm{\varphi}_{j+1}^{-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{k-1}^{-1}\bm{\varphi}_{k-1}\ldots\widehat{\bm{\varphi}_{j}}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\alpha)\diamond(c_{\Id},\beta)+(k-j-1)

\displaystyle=\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\ldots\widehat{\bm{\varphi}_{j}}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\alpha)\diamond(c_{\Id},\beta)+(k-j-1),

which implies

\displaystyle U-U^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(U^{\prime\conv}-U^{\prime})\diamond(U-U^{\prime\conv})

\displaystyle=(\bm{\varphi}_{i+1}^{-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}^{-1},\alpha)\diamond(\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\ldots\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\alpha)+(k-j-1)

\displaystyle=(k-j-1)

\displaystyle=(d_{b}+c_{b}+1)=(\Mid(a,c,d)_{b}+c_{b}+\sgn(a,c,d)).\qed

.

. If
K
is parametrized by
Z
and
K^{\prime}
is parametrized by
Z^{\prime}
, then we have
Q(Z)-Q(Z^{\prime})=\Mid(a,c,d)_{b}+c_{b}+\sgn(a,c,d)
.

Proof.

Orient
Z
as, for example
a_{1}\to a_{2}\to\ldots a_{n}\to a_{1}
, so that the orientation agrees with the direction of the turnarounds. Also orient
Z^{\prime}
in the same way as
Z
(so
Z
agrees with
Z^{\prime}
outside of the pruning). Now we compute the difference:

\displaystyle Q(C_{K})-Q(C_{K}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Delta\left(\sum_{a\to b}ab+\sum_{a}a+\sum_{a\to b}\max\{a,b\}+\sum_{a\to b\to c}\text{Middle$(\{a,b,c\})$}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\Delta\left(\#\left\{a\to b\to c\kern 5.0pt\middle|\text{ $b$ is not between $a$ and $c$}\right\}/2\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\Delta\left(\#\left\{a\to b\to c\kern 5.0pt\middle|\text{ $a-1/2$ is between $b$ and $c$}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\overbrace{\Delta(1)}^{=0}

\displaystyle+\Delta\bigl(\#\left\{\text{turnarounds }e_{a}\to\overleftarrow{e_{b}}\to e^{\prime}_{a}\right\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\overbrace{(xy+yx)}^{=0\mod 2}+(y+x)+(\overbrace{\max\{x,y\}+\max\{y,x\}}^{=0\mod 2})

\displaystyle\quad+(\overbrace{\Mid(\{w,x,y\})+\Mid(\{y,x,z\})+\Mid(\{w,x,z\})}^{=\Mid(\{w,y,z\})\mod 2}+\overbrace{\Mid(\{x,y,x\})}^{=x})

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\overbrace{1_{x\text{ not between }w,y}+1_{x\text{ not between }y,z}+1_{x\text{ not between }w,z}}^{=-1\text{ if }x\text{ between }y,w\text{ and }x\text{ between }y,z,\text{ }=1\text{ otherwise}}+\overbrace{1_{y\text{ not between }x,x}}^{=1}\right)/2

\displaystyle\quad+1_{w-1/2\text{ between }x,y}+\underbrace{1_{x-1/2\text{ between }y,x}}_{=1_{y<x}}+1_{y-1/2\text{ between }x,z}+1_{w-1/2\text{ between }x,z}

\displaystyle=y_{x}+\Mid(w,y,z)

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{1_{(x\text{ between }y,w\text{ and }x\text{ between }y,z)}+1_{w-1/2\text{ between }x,y}+1_{y-1/2\text{ between }x,z}+1_{w-1/2\text{ between }x,z}}_{=\sgn(w,y,z)+1_{x<\Mid(\{w,y,z\})}}

\displaystyle=y_{x}+\Mid(w,y,z)_{x}+\sgn(w,y,z).

The equality in the second to last line

\displaystyle 1_{(x\text{ between }y,w,\text{ and }x\text{ between }y,z)}+1_{w-1/2\text{ between }x,y}+1_{y-1/2\text{ between }x,z}+1_{w-1/2\text{ between }x,z}

\displaystyle=\sgn(w,y,z)+1_{x<\Mid(\{w,y,z\})}

is stated without proof, but can be computed by examining the different cases on the ordering of
w,y,z
.
∎

12.1.
Continuation of proof of Level 2:

Let
Z
be a facet cycle where all the possible turnarounds are pointing in the same direction. If we perform any of the pruning moves
Z\rightsquigarrow Z^{\prime}
illustrated in Figure
5
, Lemmas
12.4
and
12.5
tell us that
Q(Z)-Q(Z^{\prime})=[K]-[K^{\prime}]
, where
K
(resp
K^{\prime}
) is parametrized by
Z
(resp.
Z^{\prime}
). After a sequence of pruning moves
Z=Z_{0}\rightsquigarrow Z_{1}\rightsquigarrow\ldots\rightsquigarrow Z_{n}
we can obtain either

•

Case 1: A cycle
Z_{n}
that has no turnarounds, or

•

Case 2: A
2
vertex cycle
Z_{n}
of the form
\overrightarrow{a}\mathrel{\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\overrightarrow{b}\mathrel{\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

.

(47)

Let
Z_{n}
parametrize
K_{n}
. If
Z_{n}
is in Case 1, then
Q(Z_{n})=[K_{n}]
by our proof of Level 1. If
Z_{n}
is in Case 2, we can directly compute
Q(Z_{n})=1=[K_{n}]
.

We see through a connectedness argument that the difference
Q(Z)-[K]=Q(Z_{n})-[K_{n}]=0
.
∎

12.2.
Proof of Level 4.

We use another connectedness argument to prove that
Q
is sincere for all facet cycles.

Suppose
Z=(a_{1},\omega_{1})\mathrel{\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{

, and

.

\displaystyle Q(Z)-Q(Z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})\mathrel{\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(a,\omega)\\
\text{in }Z\end{subarray}}(a_{b}\omega+b_{a}\omega^{\prime})+\sum_{(a,\omega)\in Z}\omega-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\mathrel{\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(a,\nu)\\
\text{in }Z^{\prime}\end{subarray}}(a_{b}\nu+b_{a}\nu^{\prime})-\sum_{(a,\nu)\in Z^{\prime}}\omega

\displaystyle=(a_{r})_{(a_{1})}+(a_{2})_{(a_{1})}-1.

Now let
K
be a tube parametrized by
Z
, and let
\tilde{\eta}
be the boundary matching tube corresponding to
(a_{1},\omega_{1})
. If we “reflect”
\tilde{\eta}
about the
J
-coordinate, we obtain a tube
\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}
which is parametrized by
D^{\prime}
. Substituting in
\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}
for
\tilde{\eta}
therefore gives us a tube
K^{\prime}
paramaetrized by
Z^{\prime}
. Now observe that
\tilde{\eta}-\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}=(a_{r})_{(a_{1})}+(a_{2})_{(a_{1})}-1
. Indeed, if
\tilde{\eta}=\tilde{\eta}^{\conv}+(\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\alpha)
, then
\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}=\tilde{\eta}^{\conv}\diamond(\bm{\varphi}^{-1}_{j-1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\bm{\varphi}^{-1}_{i+1},\alpha)
, so

\tilde{\eta}-\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}\cong(\bm{\varphi}_{i+1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\diamond\bm{\varphi}_{j-1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\bm{\varphi}_{i+1},\alpha)=(j-i-1)=(b_{c}+d_{c}+1).

Therefore,
[K]-[K^{\prime}]=b_{c}+d_{c}-1
.
In other words, if
\eta_{c}
is
\eta^{\conv}
with
(b_{c}+d_{c}-1)
half-twists added. The tube
\eta^{\prime}_{c}
is simply
\eta_{c}
with each half-twist reversed, so the difference
\eta_{c}-\eta^{\prime}_{c}
is
(b_{c}+d_{c}-1)
full twists. (See Figure
38
for an illustration of
\eta_{c}
compared with
\eta^{\prime}_{c}
.)

Figure 38.
Changing
\sigma(v)
has the effect of flipping the J-factor in the boundary-matching tube of
K
corresponding to
v
. Each successive flip
\bm{\varphi}_{i}
effectively gets replaced with
\bm{\varphi}_{i}^{-1}
.

So if
Z
is an arbitrary signed facet cycle we find a sequence
Z_{0},Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}=Z
of facet cycles where
Z_{0}
has vanishing signs and
Z_{i}
differs from
Z_{i+1}
by one vertex sign. A connectedness argument shows us that
Q(Z)=[K]
for any
K
parametrized by
Z
, thus proving that
Q
is sincere.

12.3.
The main theorem

Definition 12.6
.

Let

(48)

\mathbf{c}=\sum_{x}\mu_{x}\cdot\mathcal{C}(x)\in C^{l}(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}(\mathscr{C});\mathbb{F}_{2})

Given a facewise boundary matching
\mathfrak{m}=(\mathfrak{b}_{y},\mathfrak{s}_{y})
for
\mu:=\sum_{x}\mu_{x}\cdot x
, we define the cochain
\sq^{2}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbf{c})\in C^{l+2}(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}(\mathscr{C});\mathbb{F}_{2})
as

(49)

\sq^{2}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbf{c})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\Ob(\mathscr{C})\\
\gr(z)=l+2\end{subarray}}\nu_{z}\cdot\mathcal{C}(z),

where

(50)

\nu_{z}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{cycles }\\
C\subset\Gamma(z,\mu)\end{subarray}}Q(Z(C)).

Theorem 12.7
.

For a signed cubical flow category
\mathscr{C}
, suppose we have a cocycle
\mathbf{c}\in C^{l}(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}(\mathscr{C});\mathbb{F}_{2})
written as in (
48
), and a facewise boundary matching
\mathfrak{m}
for
\mu
. We have the identity
\left[\sq_{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}(\mathbf{c})\right]=\Sq^{2}([\mathbf{c}])
.

Our cocycle
\sq_{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}(\mathbf{c})
is defined combinatorially, and by the above theorem, is a representative for
\Sq^{2}([\mathbf{c}])
. Therefore, we can define the operation
\Sq^{2}:H^{l}(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}(\mathscr{C});\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H^{l+2}(\mathcal{X}_{\sigma}(\mathscr{C});\mathbb{F}_{2})
combinatorially.

Proof.

Recall from Procedure
4.30
our method for computing
\Sq^{2}
. Take the corresponding cycle
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}=\sum_{x}\mu_{x}\cdot\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x)\in C_{\cell}^{m}(Y^{\prime};\mathbb{F}_{2})
. We have
\Sq^{2}([\mathbf{c}^{\prime}])=\Sq^{2}([c^{*}e^{m}])=\mathfrak{c}^{*}\Sq^{2}([e^{m}])=[\mathfrak{c}^{*}e^{m+2}]
, and for all
z
with
\gr(z)=l+2
,

\langle\mathfrak{c}^{*}e^{m+2},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z)\rangle=[\mathfrak{c}|_{\partial\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z)}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{cycles }\\
K\subset\partial\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z)\end{subarray}}[\mathfrak{c}|_{K}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{cycles }\\
K\subset\partial\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z)\end{subarray}}[K]=\sum_{C\subset\Gamma(z,\mu)}Q(Z(C)).

Therefore,
\Sq^{2}([\mathbf{c}^{\prime}])=\left[\sum_{z}\nu_{z}\cdot\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(z)\right]
, where
\nu_{z}
is defined in (
50
). By the last step in Procedure
4.30
, we conclude
\Sq^{2}([\mathbf{c}])=\left[\sum_{z}\nu_{z}\cdot\mathcal{C}(z)\right]
.
∎

13.
Defining
\Sq^{2}
on the family of signed cubical realizations
\mathcal{X}_{k}(\mathscr{C})
.

Sarkar-Scaduto-Stoffregen
[
SSS20
]
have introduced not only the odd Khovanov homotopy type
\mathcal{X}_{o}(L)
, but a family of spaces
\mathcal{X}_{k}(L)
, for
k\geq 0
. The space
\mathcal{X}_{0}(L)
is, by definition,
\mathcal{X}_{e}(L)
, and
\mathcal{X}_{1}(L)
is
\mathcal{X}_{o}(L)
. Furthermore, the spaces
\mathcal{X}_{2k}(L)
have identical cellular chain complexes, and the spaces
\mathcal{X}_{2k+1}(L)
have identical cellular chain complexes. In
[
SSS20
]
, these spaces are defined as homotopy colimits of doubly, triply—and so on—signed refinements (doubly signed for
\mathcal{X}_{2}(L)
, triply signed for
\mathcal{X}_{3}(L)
). But in this paper, it is more convenient to view these spaces as
l
-signed cubical
realizations.

Definition 13.1
.

Given a cubical neat embedding
\iota
of a signed flow category
(\mathscr{C},\mathfrak{f},\sigma)
relative to a tuple
\mathbf{d}
. We construct a CW complex
||\mathscr{C}||_{k}
, following the same exact construction of the signed cubical realization
||\mathscr{C}||
(outlined in Definition
4.22
), but with some changes:

(C-1)

The CW complex
||\mathscr{C}||_{k}
has one cell
\mathcal{C}(x)
for each
x\in\Ob(\mathscr{C})
. Letting
u
denote
\mathfrak{f}(x)
, this cell is given by

\mathcal{C}(x)=\prod_{i=0}^{|u|-1}[-R,R]^{d_{i}}\times\prod_{i=|u|}^{n-1}[-\epsilon,\epsilon]^{d_{i}}\times J^{k}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathscr{C}_{C}(n)}(u,\overline{0}).

Note the factor
J^{k}
instead of
J
.

(C-2)

For any
x,y\in\Ob(\mathscr{C})
with
\mathfrak{f}(x)=u>\mathfrak{f}(y)=v
, the cubical neat embedding
\iota
gives an embedding
\jmath_{\gamma}
very similar to the signed cubical realization, but we highlight the difference:

\jmath_{\gamma}:\mathcal{C}(y)\times\gamma\xhookrightarrow{\tau_{k}^{\sigma(\gamma)}\times\text{Id}}\mathcal{C}(y)\times\gamma\hookrightarrow\partial\mathcal{C}(x),

where
\gamma\in\widehat{A}_{x,y}
, and
\tau_{k}:\mathcal{C}(y)\to\mathcal{C}(y)
denotes the negation
(t,\ldots,t)\mapsto(-t,\ldots,-t)
in the
J^{k}
-factor.

(C-3)

The attaching maps
\partial\mathcal{C}(x)\to\mathcal{C}(y)
are defined similarly to Definition
4.22
.

The
k
-signed cubical realization

\mathcal{X}_{k}(\mathscr{C})
is defined to be the formal desuspension

\mathcal{X}_{k}(\mathscr{C}):=\Sigma^{-(N+|\mathbf{d}|+k)}||\mathscr{C}||.

Given a cocycle
\mathbf{c}\in C^{l}(\mathcal{X}_{k}(\mathscr{C});\mathbb{F}_{2})
, we repeat a similar boundary matching argument with slightly different boundary matching tubes
\eta
, again creating similar cycles
K
yielding classes
[K]
with balues we can compute. Now these cycles
K
are parametrized by cycles
C
in our special graph structure
\Gamma(z,\mu)
. The quantity
Q_{k}(C)
defined below gives the value of
[K]
:“”

Theorem 13.3
.

View the second Steenrod square
\Sq^{2}
on
\mathcal{X}_{k}(\mathscr{C})
as an operation
H^{*}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H^{*+2}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{F}_{2})
. Then we have
\Sq^{2}([\mathbf{c}])=[\sq_{k,\mathfrak{m}}^{2}(\mathbf{c})]
.

Proof.

The proof is analagous to the proof of Theorem
12.7
.
∎

Notation 13.4
.

We write
\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\mathscr{C})}:=\Sq^{2}|_{H^{*}(\mathcal{X}_{k}(\mathscr{C});\mathbb{F}_{2})}
as the second Steenrod square on the space
\mathcal{X}_{k}(\mathscr{C})
. We also view
\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\mathscr{C})}
as an operation
H^{*}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H^{*+2}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})
. In particular, for any link
L
we view
\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(L)}
as an operation on
Kh(L;\mathbb{F}_{2})
.

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

This is a direct consequence of our definition of
\sq_{k,\mathfrak{m}}^{2}(\mathbf{c})
.
∎

While
\Sq^{2}
on
\mathcal{X}_{k}(L)
does not depend on
k\ \mathrm{mod}\ 4
, we shall see by computations in Section
15
that
\Sq^{2}
on
\mathcal{X}_{k}(L)
does indeed depend on
k\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2
.

14.
A combinatorial proof that
\Sq^{2}
agrees with earlier formulas.

Lipshitz-Sarkar
[
LS14b
]
derived a combinatorial formula for
\Sq^{2}
on the cubical realization of an unsigned flow category. Their formula allowed them to compute the stable homotopy types of the even Khovanov spectra
\mathcal{X}_{e}(L)
of links
L
up to 11 crossings.

Furthermore, Shütz
[
Sch22
]
introduced combinatorially defined operations

\Sq_{0}^{2},\ \Sq_{1}^{2}:H^{*}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H^{*+2}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2}),

which are both defined on signed flow categories (more precisely, Shütz only requires that
\mathscr{C}
is a
signed
1
-flow category
—a looser requirement).

Shütz’s operations
\Sq_{0}^{2}
,
\Sq_{1}^{2}
are generalizations of Sarkar-Lipshitz’s formula for
\Sq^{2}
in the sense that if
\mathscr{C}
is a trivially signed cubical flow category (or analagously,
F:\underline{2}^{n}\to B_{\sigma}
has all signs
+1
),
\Sq_{0}^{2}
,
\Sq_{1}^{2}
agree with
\Sq^{2}
on the level of cycles
\mu
. However, it has not been known how
\Sq_{0}^{2}
,
\Sq_{1}^{2}
relate to
\Sq^{2}
on the odd Khovanov spectrum. We give a combinatorial proof that for a signed cubical flow category We give a combinatorial proof that
\Sq_{0}^{2}
,
\Sq_{1}^{2}
do arise as honest Steenrod squares, with
\Sq_{1}^{2}
being the Steenrod square on the odd Khovanov homotopy type. We first begin with a quick overview of
\Sq_{0}^{2}
,
\Sq_{1}^{2}
.

Definition 14.1
.

The
standard frame assignment

f\in C^{2}(\mathcal{C}(n),\mathbb{F}_{2})
is the following
2
-cochain. If
w=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\kappa+2}\}
and
u=\{a_{1},\ldots,\widehat{a_{i}},\ldots,\widehat{a_{j}},\ldots,a_{\kappa+2}\}
, then

f(\mathcal{C}_{w,u})=(i-1)(j-i-1)=ij+i+j+1\pmod{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}.

Definition 14.2
.

Given a cycle
\mu\in C^{l}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})
, we define a
signwise boundary matching

\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
of
\mu
as a collection of pairs
(\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{y},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{y})
where

•

\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{y}
is a fixed point free involution of
\mathcal{M}(y,c)
. We can also think of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{y}
as a partition of
\mathcal{M}(y,c)
into unordered pairs of the form
\{p,p^{\prime}\}
.

•

\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{y}
is an ordering for all the pairs
\{p,p^{\prime}\}
where
S(p)=S(p^{\prime})
. In other words, if the
p
and
p^{\prime}
-summands in (
9
), Definition
4.16.1
, agree, then
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{y}
orders
\{p,p^{\prime}\}

Definition 14.3
.

Given a facewise boundary matching
\mathfrak{m}=(\mathfrak{b}_{y},\mathfrak{s}_{y})
, we define the
corresponding signwise boundary matching

\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}=(\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{y},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{y})
as follows:

•

The fixed-point free involutions are the same, that is
\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{y}:=\mathfrak{b}_{y}
.

•

If
\{p,p^{\prime}\}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{y}
is a matched pair with
S(p)=S(p^{\prime})
, then
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}
must order
\{p,p^{\prime}\}
as
(p,p^{\prime})
or
(p^{\prime},p)
. We simply choose the ordering to be the ordering from
\mathfrak{s}_{y}
(recall that every pair
\{p,p^{\prime}\}\in\mathfrak{b}_{y}
is ordered by
\mathfrak{s}_{y}
).

The ordering
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{y}
does not order all pairs (compare with
\mathfrak{s}_{y}
). But for the pairs
\{p,p^{\prime}\}
that must be ordered,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{y}
uses the ordering of
\mathfrak{s}_{y}
.

Definition 14.4

(Variant of
[
Sch22
]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a signed flow category,
\mu\in C^{l}(\mathcal{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})
a cocycle, and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}=(\widetilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{y},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}_{y})
a signwise boundary matching for
\mu
. Given
z\in\Ob{\mathscr{C}}
, we define another special graph structure
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z,\mu):=\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}(z,\mu)
(see Definition
7.1
) as follows. (See Figure
39
for an illustration.) The vertex set
V
, edge set
E
, and function
S:V\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
are defined the same as in Example
7.1.1
:

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle:=\coprod_{\gr(y)=l+1}\mathcal{M}(y,\mu)\times\mathcal{M}(z,y)

\displaystyle E^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\{e=\{(p_{1},q_{1}),(p_{2},q_{2})\}\mid e=\partial I\text{ for some }x\in\mu\text{, }I\subset\mathcal{M}(z,x)\}

\displaystyle E\backslash E^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{\{(p,q),(p^{\prime},q)\}\mid\text{$p$ is boundary-matched with $p^{\prime}$}\}\}

\displaystyle S(p,q)

\displaystyle:=S(p)+S(q)\qquad\begin{gathered}\text{($S$ is the cubical sign assignment}\\
\text{from Definition \ref{canonical sign assignment})}\end{gathered}

The directed edges
E^{\prime\prime}
are different from the directed edges in Example
7.1.1
: we define
E^{\prime\prime}=\{\{(p,q),(p^{\prime},q)\}\in E\backslash E^{\prime}\ |\ S(p,q)=S(p^{\prime},q)\}
, and
e\in E^{\prime\prime}
is directed from
(p,q)
to
(p^{\prime},q)
if
(p,p^{\prime})\in\widetilde{s}_{y}
.

Additionally, we equip
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z,\mu)
with a function
f:E^{\prime}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
, which we call a “framing” of
\mathscr{C}
. (The concept of a framing is discussed in
[
Sch22
]
and generalizes the frame assignment of
[
LS14b
]
.)

Note that each graph component
C
of
\Gamma(z,\mu)
has an even number of directed edges. The proof is almost exactly the proof of Lemma
6.6
.

Remark 14.4.1
.

The cubical special graph structure
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z,\mu)
has the exact same edge set
E
as
\Gamma(z,\mu)
, but the difference arises with the directed edge set
E^{\prime\prime}
. In
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z,\mu)
, only the edges
e=\{v,v^{\prime}\}
where
S(v)=S(v^{\prime})
are oriented, but the orientation of these edges must agree with their orientation in
\Gamma(z,\mu)
.

Definition 14.5

(
[
Sch22
]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
,
\mu
, and
(\widetilde{b}_{y},\widetilde{s}_{y})
be as in Definition
14.4
, and let
C
be a cycle in
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z,\mu)
. We define
F(C)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
to be the sum of the framing values
f(e^{\prime})
for
e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
in
C
. Also let
D(C)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
denote the number of oriented edges in
C
that point in a given direction. This quanitity
D(C)
is well-defined given the fact that there are an even number of edges
E^{\prime\prime}
in
C
.

1&if $a\neq a^{\prime}$, $b\neq b^{\prime}$, $c=c^{\prime}$, $d=d^{\prime}$\\
c+d&if $a=a^{\prime}$, $b=b^{\prime}$, $c\neq c^{\prime}$, $d\neq d^{\prime}$\\
a&if $a\neq a^{\prime}$, $b=b^{\prime}$, $c=c^{\prime}$, $d\neq d^{\prime}$\\
b&if $a=a^{\prime}$, $b\neq b^{\prime}$, $c\neq c^{\prime}$, $d=d^{\prime}$\\
\epsilon+b&if $a\neq a^{\prime}$, $b=b^{\prime}$, $c\neq c^{\prime}$, $d=d^{\prime}$\\
\epsilon+a&if $a=a^{\prime}$, $b\neq b^{\prime}$, $c=c^{\prime}$, $d\neq d^{\prime}$\\
a+b&if $a\neq a^{\prime}$, $b\neq b^{\prime}$, $c\neq c^{\prime}$, $d\neq d^{\prime}$\end{cases}

Here,
a,b,c,d
denote the sign assignment values of
s
, and
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime},d^{\prime}
denote the sign assignment values of
S
. In terms of our sign map
\sigma
, we write

(52)

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to53.16pt{\vbox to51.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.9638pt\lower-5.48578pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

c+d&if $\sigma(p)=\sigma(p^{\prime})=0$, $\sigma(q)=\sigma(q^{\prime})=1$\\
a&if $\sigma(p^{\prime})=\sigma(q)=0$, $\sigma(p)=\sigma(q^{\prime})=1$\\
b&if $\sigma(p)=\sigma(q^{\prime})=0$, $\sigma(p^{\prime})=\sigma(q)=1$\\
\epsilon+b&if $\sigma(p^{\prime})=\sigma(q^{\prime})=0$, $\sigma(p)=\sigma(q)=1$\\
\epsilon+a&if $\sigma(p)=\sigma(q)=0$, $\sigma(p^{\prime})=\sigma(q^{\prime})=1$\\
a+b&if $\sigma(p)=\sigma(p^{\prime})=\sigma(q)=\sigma(q^{\prime})=1$\end{cases}

Definition 14.7

(
[
Sch22
]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a signed cubical flow category with standard sign assignment
S
and frame assignment
f_{\epsilon}
. Given a cycle
\mu\in C^{l}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})
and boundary matching
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
, we define
\sq_{\epsilon,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(\mu)\in C^{l+2}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})
by

\langle\sq_{\epsilon,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(\mu),z\rangle=\sum_{C\in\Gamma(z,a)}1+F(C)+D(C).

It is proved in
[
LOS20
]
that
\sq_{\epsilon,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(\mu)
is a cocycle, and in fact, only differs by a coboundary if we change
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
. Therefore, we have a well-defined operation
\Sq_{\epsilon}^{2}:H^{l}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H^{l+2}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})

Lemma 14.8
.

(54)

F(C)=\sum_{e^{\prime}_{a}\overset{e}{\mathrel{\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}e_{b}}\left(ab+\sigma(e)+a_{b}\sigma_{2}(e^{\prime}_{a})+b_{a}\sigma_{2}(e_{b})\right).

Proof.

When
\epsilon=1
, the right hand side of Equation (
52
) is equal to
a\sigma(q)+b\sigma(q^{\prime})+(c+b)\sigma(p^{\prime})+(d+a)\sigma(p)
(this can be checked case by case). Now recall
c=b_{a}
and
d=a_{b}
. So simplifying, we find

(55)

\displaystyle(\ref{frame difference})

\displaystyle=a\sigma(q)+b\sigma(q^{\prime})+(1|_{a<b})\sigma(p^{\prime})+(1|_{b<a})\sigma(p)

\displaystyle=(a_{b}+1|_{b<a})\sigma(q)+(b_{a}+1|_{a<b})\sigma(q^{\prime})+(1|_{a<b})\sigma(p^{\prime})+(1|_{b<a})\sigma(p)

\displaystyle=a_{b}\sigma(q)+b_{a}\sigma(q^{\prime})+(1|_{b<a})(\sigma(p)+\sigma(q))+(1|_{a<b})(\sigma(p^{\prime})+\sigma(q^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=a_{b}\sigma(q)+b_{a}\sigma(q^{\prime})+(1|_{b<a})(\sigma(p\circ q))+(1|_{a<b})(\sigma(p^{\prime}\circ q^{\prime})),

which is equal to the right hand side of Equation (
54
) whether
b<a
or
a<b
.
Combine Equation (
55
) with the identity

Lemma 14.9
.

(56)

\displaystyle D(\widetilde{C})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\to b\to c\\
a<c\end{subarray}}(a+c)+\#\{a\to b\to c:a<c,\ b\text{ not between }a,c\}

\displaystyle+\#\left\{\text{turnarounds }a\to\overrightarrow{b}\to a\right\}

\displaystyle+\sum_{e^{\prime}_{a}\to\overrightarrow{e_{b}}\to e^{\prime\prime}_{c}}\Delta\sigma(e_{b})

Proof.

\displaystyle D(\widetilde{C})

\displaystyle=\#\left\{e^{\prime}_{a}\to\overrightarrow{e_{b}}\to e^{\prime\prime}_{c}\ \middle|\ \Delta S(e_{b})=1\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{e^{\prime}_{a}\to\overrightarrow{e_{b}}\to e^{\prime\prime}_{c}}(\Delta S(e_{b})+1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e^{\prime}_{a}\to e_{b}\to e^{\prime\prime}_{c}\\
a<c\end{subarray}}(\Delta S(e_{b})+1)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{turnarounds}\\
e^{\prime}_{a}\to\overrightarrow{e_{b}}\to e^{\prime\prime}_{a}\end{subarray}}(\Delta S(e_{b})+1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e^{\prime}_{a}\to e_{b}\to e^{\prime\prime}_{c}\\
a<c\end{subarray}}\left(a_{b}+c_{b}+1+\Delta\sigma(e_{b})\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{turnarounds}\\
e^{\prime}_{a}\to\overrightarrow{e_{b}}\to e^{\prime\prime}_{a}\end{subarray}}(1+\Delta\sigma(e_{b}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\to b\to c\\
a<c\end{subarray}}(a_{b}+c_{b}+1)+\sum_{e^{\prime}_{a}\to\overrightarrow{e_{b}}\to e^{\prime\prime}_{c}}\Delta\sigma(e_{b})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{turnarounds}\\
e^{\prime}_{a}\to\overrightarrow{e_{b}}\to e^{\prime\prime}_{a}\end{subarray}}1

which, in turn, simplifies to the right hand side of (
56
).

∎

Theorem 14.10
.

Fix a signed flow category
\mathscr{C}
and fix
l\geq 0
,
l\equiv 1\mod 4
. If we view
\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\mathscr{C})}
as an operation
H^{*}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H^{*+2}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})
, then we have
\Sq^{2}=\Sq_{1}^{2}
, where
\Sq_{1}^{2}
is defined in
[
Sch22
]
. In particular,
\Sq_{1}^{2}
agrees with the Steenrod square on the odd Khovanov homotopy types
\mathcal{X}_{k}(L)
,
k\equiv 1\mod 4
.

We remark that if
\mathscr{C}
is an
unsigned
cubical flow category, then
\Sq^{2}_{0}=\Sq^{2}_{1}
, and both formulas agree on the nose with the Lipshitz-Sarkar formula for
\Sq^{2}
. Therefore, the above theorem provides a combinatorial proof that our even second Steenrod square
\Sq^{2}:H^{*}(\mathcal{X}_{e}(L);\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H^{*+2}(\mathcal{X}_{e}(L);\mathbb{F}_{2})
agrees with Lipshitz-Sarkar’s
\Sq^{2}
formula on even
Kh
.

Proof.

Let
\mu\in C^{l}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})
be a cocycle, let
\mathfrak{m}
be a facewise boundary matching for
\mu
, and let
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
be the corresponding signwise boundary matching. For a fixed
z
of grading
\gr(z)=l+2
, we recall the
z
-coefficient
\langle\sq_{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}(\mu),z\rangle=\sum_{C\in\Gamma(z,\mu)}Q(Z(C))
, and the
z
-coefficient
\langle\sq_{1,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(\mu),z\rangle=\sum_{C\in\widetilde{\Gamma}(z,\mu)}\widetilde{Q}(C)
, where
\widetilde{Q}(C)=1+F(C)+D(C)
. Fix a cycle
C\in\Gamma(z,\mu)
and its corresponding cycle
\widetilde{C}\in\widetilde{\Gamma}(z,\mu)
; then subtract
\widetilde{Q}(\widetilde{C})
from
Q(Z(C))

\pmod{2}
to obtain

\displaystyle Q(Z(C))-\widetilde{Q}(\widetilde{C})

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{aligned} &&&\sum_{a\to b}ab+\sum_{a}a+\sum_{a\to b}\max\{a,b\}+\sum_{a\to b\to c}\Mid(a,b,c)\\
&&&\quad+\#\left\{a\to b\to c\kern 5.0pt\middle|\text{ $b$ is not between $a$ and $c$}\right\}/2\\
&&&\quad+\#\left\{a\to b\to c\kern 5.0pt\middle|\text{ $a-1/2$ is between $b$ and $c$}\right\}\\
&&&\quad+1\\
&&&\quad+\#\left\{\text{turnarounds }a\to\overleftarrow{b}\to a\right\}\\
&&&+\sum_{e^{\prime}_{a}\to e_{b}}(a_{b}\sigma_{2}(e_{b})+b_{a}\sigma_{2}(e^{\prime}_{a}))+\sum_{e_{b}}\sigma_{2}(e_{b})\end{aligned}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\begin{aligned} &&&1+\sum_{e^{\prime}_{a}\xrightarrow{e}e_{b}}\left(ab+\sigma(e)+a_{b}\sigma_{2}(e^{\prime}_{a})+b_{a}\sigma_{2}(e_{b})\right)\\
&&&\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\to b\to c\\
a<c\end{subarray}}(a+c)+\#\{a\to b\to c:a<c,\ b\text{ not between }a,c\}\\
&&&+\#\left\{\text{turnarounds }a\to\overrightarrow{b}\to a\right\}\\
&&&+\sum_{e^{\prime}_{a}\to\overrightarrow{e_{b}}\to e^{\prime\prime}_{c}}\Delta\sigma\left(\overrightarrow{e_{b}}\right)\end{aligned}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\to b}\max\{a,b\}+\overbrace{\sum_{a}a+\sum_{a\to b\to c}\Mid(a,b,c)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\to b\to c\\
a<c\end{subarray}}\left(a+c\right)}^{=\sum_{a\to b\to c}(b+\Mid\{a,b,c\}+(a+c)|_{a<c})}

\displaystyle\quad+\#\left\{a\to b\to c\kern 5.0pt\middle|\text{ $b$ is not between $a$ and $c$}\right\}/2

\displaystyle\quad+\#\left\{a\to b\to c\kern 5.0pt\middle|\text{ $a-1/2$ is between $b$ and $c$}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\#\{a\to b\to c\ |\ a<c,\ b\text{ not between }a,c\}

\displaystyle\quad+\#\{\text{turnarounds }a\to b\to a\}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{e^{\prime}_{a}\xrightarrow{e}e_{b}}\sigma(e)+\sum_{q/b}\sigma(q)+\sum_{e^{\prime}_{a}\to\overrightarrow{e_{b}}\to e^{\prime\prime}_{c}}\Delta\sigma\left(e_{b}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\to b}\max\{a,b\}+\sum_{a\to b\to c}(b+\Mid\{a,b,c\}+(a+c)|_{a<c})

\displaystyle\quad+\#\left\{a\to b\to c\kern 5.0pt\middle|\text{ $b$ is not between $a$ and $c$}\right\}/2

\displaystyle\quad+\#\left\{a\to b\to c\kern 5.0pt\middle|\ b<a\leq c\text{ or }c<a<b\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\#\{a\to b\to c\ |\ b<a<c\text{ or }a<c<b\}

\displaystyle\quad+\#\{\text{turnarounds }a\to b\to a\}

\displaystyle\quad+\overbrace{\sum_{e^{\prime}_{a}\xrightarrow{e}e_{b}}\sigma(e)+\sum_{e^{\prime}_{a}\to\overrightarrow{e_{b}}\to e^{\prime\prime}_{c}}\Delta\sigma(e_{b})}^{=\sum_{\overrightarrow{e_{b}}}\sigma(t(\overrightarrow{e_{b}}))}+\sum_{e_{b}}\sigma_{2}(e_{b})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\to b}\max\{a,b\}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\to b\to c\\

\text{between }a,c\end{subarray}}b+\overbrace{\sum_{a\to b\to c}(a|_{b>a}+c|_{b<c})}^{=\sum_{a\to b}\max\{a,b\}}

\displaystyle\quad+(\#\left\{a\to b\to c\kern 5.0pt\middle|\ b>a,c\right\}+\overbrace{\#\left\{a\to b\to c\kern 5.0pt\middle|\ b<a,c\right\}}^{=\#\left\{a\to b\to c\kern 4.09723pt\middle|\ b>a,c\right\}})/2

\displaystyle\quad+\#\left\{a\to b\to c\kern 5.0pt\middle|\ b>a,c\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\overbrace{\#\{a\to b\to c\ |\ b>a=c\}+\#\{a\to b\to c\ |\ b<a=c\}}^{=\#\{\text{turnarounds }a\to b\to a\}}

\displaystyle\quad+\#\{\text{turnarounds }a\to b\to a\}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{e_{b}}\sigma_{2}(e_{b})+\sum_{\overrightarrow{e_{b}}}\sigma\left(t\left(\overrightarrow{e_{b}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\to b}\max\{a,b\}+\overbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\to b\to c\\
b\text{ not}\\
\text{between }a,c\end{subarray}}b}^{=\sum_{a\to b}\max\{a,b\}}+\sum_{a\to b}\max\{a,b\}+\sum_{\overrightarrow{e_{b}}}\sigma_{1}\left(t\left(\overrightarrow{e_{b}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\mathrel{\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle T_{1}(C)+T_{2}(C).

Observe that

\sq_{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}(\mu)-\sq_{1,\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}}^{2}(\mu)=\sum_{z}\left(\sum_{C\in\Gamma(z,\mu)}T_{1}(C)\right)z+\sum_{z}\left(\sum_{C\in\Gamma(z,\mu)}T_{2}(C)\right)z=:T_{1}+T_{2}.

Our final goal is to prove that
T_{1}+T_{2}
is a coboundary. We proceed by simplifying
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
:

\displaystyle\langle T_{1},z\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{C\in\Gamma(z,\mu)}T_{1}(C)

\displaystyle=\sum_{C\in\Gamma(z,\mu)}\sum_{e^{\prime}_{a}\mathrel{\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{

\displaystyle=\sum_{C\in\Gamma(z,\mu)}\sum_{e^{\prime}_{a}\mathrel{\hbox to11.78pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}e_{b}}\max\{S_{\mathbb{Z}}(q),S_{\mathbb{Z}}(q^{\prime})\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\text{ appears}\\
\text{in }\mu\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{intervals}\\
I\in\mathcal{M}(z,x)\end{subarray}}m(I),

\displaystyle\langle T_{2},z\rangle=\sum_{C\in\Gamma(z,\mu)}\sum_{\overrightarrow{e_{b}}}\sigma_{1}\left(t\left(\overrightarrow{e_{b}}\right)\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gr(y)=\gr(z)-1\\
q\in\mathcal{M}(z,y)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathcal{M}(y,x)\\
(\mathfrak{b}_{y}(p),p)\in\mathfrak{s}_{y}\end{subarray}}\sigma(p).

The following lemma will help us prove that
T_{1}
is a coboundary:

Lemma 14.11
.

The linear map
L:C^{*}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})\to C^{*+2}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})
defined on generators
x
by

L(x)=\sum_{\gr(z)=\gr(x)+2}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{intervals}\\
I\in\mathcal{M}(z,x)\end{subarray}}m(I)\right)z

is a nullhomotopic chain map.

Proof.

Define the homotopy
H:C^{*}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})\to C^{*+1}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathscr{C};\mathbb{F}_{2})
for generators
x\in\mathfrak{f}^{-1}(u)
,
y\in\mathfrak{f}^{-1}(v)
by

\langle H(x),y\rangle=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{M}(y,x)}\binom{S_{\mathbb{Z}}(p)+1}{2}=\#\left\{p\in\mathcal{M}(y,x)\right\}\cdot\binom{s_{\mathbb{Z}}(v,u)+1}{2}.

We prove that
dH+Hd=L
using the following diagrams:

q
q^{\prime}
p^{\prime}
p
z
y
y^{\prime}
x

\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{f}}

a
b
a-1
b
w
v
v^{\prime}
u

where
a=S_{\mathbb{Z}}(q):=s_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathcal{C}_{w,v})
,
b=S_{\mathbb{Z}}(q)=s_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathcal{C}_{w,v^{\prime}})
, with
a>b
. The contribution to
\langle(dH+Hd)x,z\rangle
from each interval
I\in\mathcal{M}(z,x)
with boundary in
\{\mathcal{M}(y,x)\circ\mathcal{M}(z,y),\mathcal{M}(y^{\prime},x)\circ\mathcal{M}(z,y^{\prime})\}
is
\binom{a+1}{2}+\binom{a}{2}+\binom{b+1}{2}+\binom{b+1}{2}=a=m(I)\mod 2
.
∎

To conclude our proof, observe that
T_{1}=L(\mu)
, which must be a coboundary by Lemma
14.11
, and

T_{2}=d\left(\sum_{y}\xi_{y}\cdot y\right),\qquad\xi_{y}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathcal{M}(y,x)\\
(\mathfrak{b}_{y}p,p)\in\mathfrak{s}_{y}\end{subarray}}\sigma(p).\qed

Recall the operation
\Sq_{0}^{2}
outlined in Definition
14.7
. It is a similar combinatorial exercise to prove the following:

Theorem 14.12
.

Fix
l\geq 0
,
l\equiv 3\mod 4
. If we view
\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)}
as an operation on
Kh_{o}(L;\mathbb{F}_{2})
, then we have
\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)}=\Sq_{0}^{2}
.

Proof.

It might be easier to use the fact proved in Theorem
14.10
that
\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{o}(L)}=\Sq_{1}^{2}
. Indeed, we are left with comparing the cancelled out terms in
\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{o}(L)}-\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{3}(L)}
and
\Sq_{0}^{2}-\Sq_{1}^{2}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Follows from Theorems
14.10
,
14.12
.
∎

15.
Khovanov homotopy types for width three knots

Fix
l\geq 0
and let
\bullet=l\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2
(we interpret
e=0\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2
,
o=1\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2
).

Definition 15.1

(
[
LS14b
]
)
.

For any link
L
, we define a function
St_{l}=St_{l}(L):\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}^{4}
is defined as follows: Fix gradings
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, let
k\in\{i,i+1\}
, and let
\Sq_{(k)}^{1}
denote the map
\Sq^{1}:Kh_{\bullet}^{k,j}(L)\to Kh_{\bullet}^{k+1,j}(L)
. Now let
\Sq^{2}
denote the map
\Sq^{2}|_{\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)}:Kh_{\bullet}^{*,j}(L)\to Kh_{\bullet}^{*+2,j}(L)
.

Let
r_{1}
be the rank of
\Sq_{l}^{2}:Kh_{\bullet}^{i,j}(L)\to Kh_{\bullet}^{i+2,j}(L)
, and let
r_{2}=\rank(\Sq^{2}|_{\ker\Sq^{1}_{(i)}})
,
r_{3}=\dim\left(\im\Sq^{1}_{(i+1)}\cap\im\Sq^{2}\right)
, and
r_{4}=\dim\left(\im\Sq^{1}_{(i+1)}\cup\im\bigl(\Sq^{2}|_{\ker\Sq^{1}_{(i)}}\bigr)\right)
. We define

St_{l}(i,j):=(r_{2}-r_{4},r_{1}-r_{2}-r_{3}+r_{4},r_{4},r_{3}-r_{4}).

Proposition 15.2

(
[
LS14b
]
)
.

Suppose the Khovanov homology
Kh_{\bullet}(L)
of a link
L
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

Kh^{i,j}_{\bullet}(L)
lies on three adjacent diagonals, say,
2i-j=\sigma,\sigma+2,\sigma+4
.

(2)

Kh^{i,j}_{\bullet}(L)
is a product of copies of
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{Z}/3
, and
\mathbb{Z}/2
.

(3)

There is no torsion on the diagonal
2i-j=\sigma
.

Then the stable homotopy types of the spectra
\mathcal{X}^{j}_{l}(L)
are determined by
Kh_{\bullet}(L)
and
St_{l}(L)
as follows: Fix a
q
-grading
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
i=(\sigma+j)/2
, and let
St_{l}(i,j)=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
; the Khovanov spectrum
\mathcal{X}_{l}^{j}(L)
is stably homotopy equivalent to

\Bigl(\bigvee^{x_{1}}\Sigma^{i-2}\mathbb{C}P^{2}\Bigr)\vee\Bigl(\bigvee^{x_{2}}\Sigma^{i-3}\mathbb{R}P^{5}/\mathbb{R}P^{2}\Bigr)\vee\Bigl(\bigvee^{x_{3}}\Sigma^{i-2}\mathbb{R}P^{4}/\mathbb{R}P^{1}\Bigr)\vee\Bigl(\bigvee^{x_{4}}\Sigma^{i-2}\mathbb{R}P^{2}\wedge\mathbb{R}P^{2}\Bigr)

and a wedge of Moore spaces. Furthermore, such a wedge decomposition into these factors is unique. In particular,
\mathcal{X}_{l}^{j}
is a wedge sum of Moore spaces if and only if
x_{1}=x_{2}=x_{3}=x_{4}=0
.

In the absence of Criterion
(2)
, we have a slight weakening of Proposition
15.2
, which uses
[
Bau95
, Theorems 11.2, 11.7]
to decompose
X_{l}(L)
into elementary Chang complexes.

Proposition 15.3

(
[
Bau95
]
)
.

Suppose the Khovanov homology
Kh_{\bullet}(L)
of a link
L
satisfies Criteria
(1)
and
(3)
. Then we can decompose
\mathcal{X}^{j}_{l}(L)
into a wedge of Moore spaces and a wedge of Chang complexes of the form
X(\eta)
,
X(\eta q)
,
X(_{p}\eta)
,
X(_{p}\eta q)
, where
p
and
q
are powers of
2
. Furthermore, this decomposition is unique.

16.
Computations

Let
\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)
be a Khovanov spectrum, where
l\geq 0
and
L
a link, and let
\bullet=l\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2
. It can be checked from the databases
[
LM25
]
that, with the exception of the case where both
l
is odd and
K\in\{K11n19,m(K11n19)\}
,
\mathcal{X}_{l}(K)
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
15.2
for all knots
K
of 11 crossings or fewer. Therefore, the homotopy types of the rest of the sapces
\mathcal{X}_{l}(L)
are determined by the (integral) Khovanov homology
Kh_{\bullet}
and the function
St_{l}
.

Remark 16.0.1
.

Indeed, the Khovanov homologies
Kh_{o}(K_{0})
and
Kh_{o}(m(K_{0}))
each have a copy of
\mathbb{Z}/4
, violating Condition
(2)
. However we can use Proposition
15.3
to conclude that
\mathcal{X}_{1}(K_{0})\cong\mathcal{X}_{3}(m(K_{0}))
is a wedge of Moore spaces together with
\Sigma^{-3}(\mathbb{R}P^{5}/\mathbb{R}P^{2})
, and
\mathcal{X}(m(K_{0}))
is a wedge of Moore spaces together with an elementary Chang complex
{}_{4}\eta 2
.

We present the
St_{1}
,
St_{3}
values for prime knots
K
of 11 or fewer crossings in Table
6
(for the
St_{0}
values, see
[
LS14b
]
). Interestingly,
St_{2}(L)
is trivial for all prime knots and links up to 11 crossings, so we do not list the
St_{2}
values. To save space, we only include the knots
K
for which
St_{l}(K)
is not identically
(0,0,0,0)
for all
l
, and for these knots, we only list the tuples
(i,j)
for which
St_{l}(i,j)\neq(0,0,0,0)
. We refer to
[
LM25
]
to check the even and odd Khovanov homologies
Kh_{e}(K)
,
Kh_{o}(K)
.

We collect the data for the MorseLink presentations of
[
KAT
]
and use several Python programs to carry out the computations. All the programs and computations are available
https://github.com/charuvinda/KhovanovSteenrod
.
We summarize some results obtained by our computations of
St_{l}
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

We find that
St_{2}(T_{3,4})
is identically
(0,0,0,0)
, and so by Proposition
15.2
, we see that
\mathcal{X}_{2}(T_{3,4})
is a wedge sum of Moore spaces. However,
\mathcal{X}_{2}(T_{3,4})
is not a wedge sum of Moore spaces by
[
LS14b
, Theorem 1]
. For the second statement, we note that
St_{1}(T_{3,4})
is identically
(0,0,0,0)
, while
St_{3}(T_{3,4})(2,11)=(1,0,0,0)
.
∎

Theorem 16.1
.

The spectra
\mathcal{X}_{o}(T_{3,-4})
,
\mathcal{X}_{o}(T_{3,4})
are not wedge sums of Moore spaces.

Proof.

From Table
6
, we see that for
T_{3,-4}=m(8_{19})
,
St(-4,-11)=(0,1,0,0)
, and so, in particular
\mathcal{X}_{o}(T_{3,-4})
has a nontrivial
\Sq^{2}
.
∎

Ozsváth-Rasmussen-Szabó
[
ORS13
]
showed that
Kh_{o}^{*,j}(L)=Kh_{o}^{*,j-1}(L)\oplus Kh_{o}^{*,j+1}(L)
for any link
L
. However, we cannot extend this fact to the statement
\mathcal{X}_{o}^{j}(L)\simeq\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{o}^{j-1}(L)\vee\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{o}^{j+1}(L)
, as by Theorem
1.4
,
\mathcal{X}^{-11}_{o}(T_{3,-4})\not\sim\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}^{-12}_{o}(T_{3,-4})\vee\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}^{-10}_{o}(T_{3,-4})
.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

\mathcal{X}_{o}^{-11}(T_{3,-4})
has nontrivial
\Sq^{2}
, but for degree reasons, neither
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}^{-12}_{o}(T_{3,-4})
nor
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}^{-10}_{o}(T_{3,-4})
has nontrivial
\Sq^{2}
.
∎

It was asked in
[
SSS20
]
whether the odd Khovanov homotopy type
\mathcal{X}_{o}(L)
respects the identity
\mathcal{X}_{o}(L\coprod L^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{X}_{o}(L)\wedge\mathcal{X}_{o}(L^{\prime})
. The following theorem answers this question in the negative:

Theorem 16.2
.

We have
\mathcal{X}_{l}(T_{2,3}\coprod T_{2,3})\not\sim\mathcal{X}_{l}(T_{2,3})\wedge\mathcal{X}_{l}(T_{2,3})
for all
l\equiv 1\ \mathrm{mod}\ 4
and all
l\equiv 3\ \mathrm{mod}\ 4
.

Proof.

Fix an
l
as in the theorem. Observe that for degree reasons and homological reasons (no torsion in
Kh_{o}(T_{2,3})
),
\mathcal{X}_{l}(T_{2,3})
is a wedge of spheres
S^{n}
, implying
\mathcal{X}_{l}(T_{2,3})\wedge\mathcal{X}_{l}(T_{2,3})
is also a wedge of sphers. However,
St_{l}(T_{2,3}\coprod T_{2,3})
maps
(-6,-14)\mapsto(1,0,0,0)
, meaning
\mathcal{X}_{l}(T_{2,3}\coprod T_{2,3})
is not a wedge of spheres.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

A direct corollary of Theorem
16.2
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

\mathcal{X}_{o}(T_{3,-4})^{\vee}
has nontrivial
\Sq^{2}
(since
\mathcal{X}_{o}(T_{3,-4})
does), but
\mathcal{X}_{o}(m(T_{3,-4}))=\mathcal{X}_{o}(8_{19})
has trivial
\Sq^{2}
(see Table
6
). Furthermore,
\mathcal{X}_{3}(T_{3,4})^{\vee}
has nontrivial
\Sq^{2}
(since
\mathcal{X}_{3}(T_{3,4})
does), but
\mathcal{X}_{3}(m(T_{3,-4}))=\mathcal{X}_{o}(8_{19})
has trivial
\Sq^{2}
.
∎

We include some questions that arose from our
St_{l}
computations:

Question 16.3
.

Does there exist a pair of links
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
with
Kh_{o}(L_{1})=Kh_{o}(L_{2})
, but
\mathcal{X}_{o}(L)\not\sim\mathcal{X}_{o}(L)
?

Question 16.4
.

Does there exist a prime knot or link
L
for which
\mathcal{X}_{l}^{j}(L)
contains
\Sigma^{m}\mathbb{C}P^{2}
in some wedge sum decomposition, for some
l,j,m
?

A point related to this question is that for
L=T_{2,3}\coprod T_{2,3}
, we have that
\mathcal{X}_{o}^{j}(L)
contains a copy of
\Sigma^{-8}\mathbb{C}P^{2}
in its wedge sum decomposition, so the question has already been answered in the affirmative for arbitrary links.

Question 16.5
.

Does the stable homotopy type of the space
\mathcal{X}^{j}_{l}(L)
only depend on
l\ \mathrm{mod}\ 4
?

Question 16.6
.

Does there exist a non-split link
L
such that
\mathcal{X}^{j}_{2}(L)
is not a wedge sum of Moore spaces?

Question 16.7
.

Is
\mathcal{X}^{j}_{o}(L)=\mathcal{X}^{j}_{1}(L)
Spanier-Whitehead dual to the spectrum
\mathcal{X}^{j}_{3}(L)
? Is it dual to
\mathcal{X}^{j}_{l}(L)
for some
l\equiv 3\mod 4
?

Question 16.8
.

Is
\mathcal{X}^{j}_{2}(L\coprod L^{\prime})=\mathcal{X}^{j}_{2}(L)\wedge\mathcal{X}^{j}_{2}(L^{\prime})
for arbitrary links
L
,
L^{\prime}
?
